Game design dimensions that reduce the cost of punishment, allow communication, or enable coordination are likely to moderate the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Burton-Chellew, M. N., & Guérin, C. (2021). Decoupling cooperation and punishment in humans shows that punishment is not an altruistic trait. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study shows that in a standard repeated public goods game with punishment, contributions remain stable when all can punish and be punished. However, when some individuals are made immune to punishment, these immune individuals (even those classified as conditional cooperators) learn to contribute less over time, despite being able to punish others and being surrounded by high contributors. Punishment is frequent but often hypocritical or anti-social among immune individuals, and overall, the costs of punishment are greater than the benefits from increased contributions, making punishment destructive to group welfare. The results indicate that punishment does not reliably increase efficiency or group payoff, and that the link between cooperation and punishment is not altruistic but may be driven by confusion and learning.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, this paper provides strong evidence that punishment can be destructive: the costs of punishment can outweigh any gains from increased cooperation, leading to lower overall efficiency. The design closely matches standard PGGs with linear returns, four players, repeated rounds, and costly punishment (1 MU cost to reduce 3 MU from another). The paper does not report efficiency directly, but states that punishment is 'ultimately destructive' in terms of group welfare. Therefore, when using game design dimensions similar to this study (small groups, linear PGG, costly punishment, no communication, anonymous punishment), one should expect that enabling punishment may not increase, and may even decrease, group efficiency relative to the control. The findings are especially relevant for settings where punishment is frequent and costly, and where the marginal per capita return is less than one.

- source: Chen, X. J., & Perc, M. (2014). Optimal distribution of incentives for public cooperation in heterogeneous interaction environments. *Frontiers in Behavioral Neuroscience*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games on heterogeneous (scale-free) networks, the optimal way to distribute limited institutional punishment (and reward) depends on the network structure, the enhancement factor (r), and whether payoffs are absolute or degree-normalized. For low enhancement factors, distributing punishment/reward equally is optimal for maximizing cooperation. For high enhancement factors, targeting high-degree nodes (punishing or rewarding them more) is optimal, but the direction depends on whether payoffs are absolute or normalized. The main outcome is the stationary fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games, especially in heterogeneous network settings. It suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation (and thus potentially efficiency) depends on the distribution of punishment, the enhancement factor (analogous to MPCR), and the network structure. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited to inferring that higher cooperation rates (under optimal punishment schemes) may translate to higher efficiency, especially when the enhancement factor is high. The findings are most relevant for settings with network heterogeneity and institutional (not peer) punishment.

- source: Muthukrishna, M., Francois, P., Pourahmadi, S., & Henrich, J. (2017). Corrupting cooperation and how anti-corruption strategies may backfire. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This paper experimentally demonstrates that allowing bribery in an institutional punishment public goods game (IPGG) substantially reduces public good provisioning (by about 25%). In the standard IPGG (control), stronger leaders (higher punishment multiplier) increase contributions and public good provisioning. However, when bribery is possible (bribery game, BG), stronger leaders actually decrease public good provisioning, as they use their power to extract bribes rather than enforce cooperation. Anti-corruption strategies (partial/full transparency, leader investment) can increase contributions in some contexts (strong leaders or high economic potential), but may have no effect or even reduce contributions further in contexts with weak leaders and poor economic potential. The main outcome is mean contribution to the public good, which is a close proxy for efficiency. The results show that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly context-dependent and can be reversed or undermined by corruption opportunities.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, this paper provides strong evidence that the presence of corruption opportunities (e.g., bribery) can reverse the usual positive effect of institutional punishment on efficiency. The effect of punishment depends critically on whether leaders can be corrupted, the strength of the leader's punitive power, and the economic potential (mpcr). In standard IPGGs, stronger punishment increases efficiency, but in bribery-enabled games, stronger punishment can decrease efficiency. Anti-corruption interventions (transparency, leader investment) can help, but only under certain structural conditions. When using this paper for prediction, it is important to consider whether the game design allows for corruption/bribery, the strength of punishment, and the economic context. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not reported directly, but the relative changes are clear: punishment increases efficiency in standard IPGG, but decreases it when bribery is possible.

- source: Baker, F., & Rachlin, H. (2002). Teaching and learning in a probabilistic prisoner's dilemma. *Behavioural Processes*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing punishment or reward mechanisms—whether applied individually (peer-to-peer) or collectively (by group consensus)—substantially increases contributions in public goods games. The effect is stronger as the penalty/reward size or effectiveness increases, and as the proportion of punishers/rewarders in the population rises. Both mechanisms are effective, but punishment is found to be marginally more effective than reward in promoting contributions. The model also shows that collective mechanisms (pool punishment/reward) can be more effective than individual ones under certain parameter regimes. Although efficiency is not directly measured, the payoff structure implies that higher contributions lead to higher group payoffs, except for the initial cost to punishers/rewarders. The findings are robust across a range of parameter values and network structures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in a public goods game will increase group contributions and, by implication, group efficiency/payoff, relative to a control without punishment. The model explores a wide range of parameter values for punishment cost, effectiveness, and the proportion of punishers, showing that higher punishment effectiveness and more punishers lead to higher contributions. While the paper does not report efficiency as a ratio, the payoff equations and discussion make it clear that group payoff increases with contribution. The results are most directly applicable to networked, repeated PGGs with binary (cooperate/defect) choices and both individual and collective punishment/reward options. The findings should inform predictions that, for similar game designs, enabling punishment will yield higher efficiency, especially when punishment is effective and not prohibitively costly.

- source: Podder, S., Righi, S., & Pancotto, F. (2021). Reputation and punishment sustain cooperation in the optional public goods game. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in the OPGG, punishment alone (including anti-social punishment) does not increase cooperation or group fitness, and can even reduce them by increasing the prevalence of loners. Reputation alone can increase cooperation and fitness, but only under specific social norms (e.g., when defectors are assigned a strictly worse reputation than loners). The combination of reputation and punishment leads to the highest levels of cooperation and population fitness, with most agents adopting conditional cooperation strategies and rarely using punishment. The results are robust to variations in group size, synergy factor, punishment cost and effectiveness, and other parameters. The findings suggest that reputation and punishment interact synergistically to sustain cooperation and improve group outcomes, but that reputation can substitute for costly punishment in many cases.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in optional public goods games, the addition of punishment alone (especially when anti-social punishment is possible) does not reliably increase efficiency or group payoff, and may even reduce it. However, when reputation mechanisms are also present—especially those that allow agents to distinguish defectors from loners—punishment and reputation together can synergistically increase cooperation and group fitness. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency depends critically on the presence and nature of reputation mechanisms and social norms. In the absence of reputation, enabling punishment may not increase efficiency; with reputation, especially under certain norms, enabling punishment can have a more positive effect. The model's robustness checks indicate these findings are not highly sensitive to group size, rounds, or punishment cost within the tested ranges.

- source: Janssen, M. A., & Bushman, C. (2008). Evolution of cooperation and altruistic punishment when retaliation is possible. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that when agents in a public goods game can retaliate against those who punish them, the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation is greatly reduced. In the absence of retaliation, punishment can support high levels of cooperation (and thus higher group payoffs/efficiency). When retaliation is possible, cooperation and punishment both decline, especially in larger groups or when the cost of retaliation is low relative to the cost of punishment. The visibility of the punisher (i.e., whether punished agents can identify who punished them) is also important: if punishers are less visible, retaliation is less likely and cooperation can be higher. The model also shows that spiteful punishment (defectors punishing others unconditionally) does not undermine the main result: retaliation is the key factor that reduces cooperation and, by implication, efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the efficiency gains from enabling punishment in public goods games are highly contingent on whether retaliation is possible and on the relative costs of punishment and retaliation. In environments where punished agents can easily identify and retaliate against punishers, the positive effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to be much smaller or even negative, especially in larger groups. The model also highlights the importance of punisher anonymity: making it harder to identify punishers can help preserve the efficiency benefits of punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of retaliation and punisher identifiability are critical moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency.

- source: Sigmund, K. (2007). Punish or perish? Retaliation and collaboration among humans. *Trends in Ecology & Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of punishment in public goods games increases cooperation and contributions, but the cost of punishment can reduce average group income, at least initially. Over time, as cooperation becomes the norm and punishment is less frequently used, the group can approach efficient outcomes. However, the efficiency gains depend on the cost-effectiveness of punishment and the dynamics of the group. The review also notes that rewards alone are less effective than punishment in boosting cooperation, but combining indirect reciprocity (reputation-based rewards) with punishment can increase efficiency. Voluntary participation in public goods games with punishment can also enhance cooperation and efficiency compared to compulsory participation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and review-based support for the idea that punishment increases cooperation in public goods games, but its effect on efficiency is mixed and context-dependent. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment is likely to increase contributions, but whether this translates into higher efficiency depends on the cost of punishment, the number of rounds, and whether cooperation stabilizes. If punishment is costly and used frequently, efficiency may decrease; if it is used mainly as a threat and actual punishment becomes rare, efficiency can increase. The review also suggests that combining punishment with reputation-based rewards or allowing voluntary participation can improve efficiency. Thus, when predicting efficiency with punishment enabled, consider not only the presence of punishment but also its cost, the game structure, and whether mechanisms exist to reduce the frequency or cost of punishment over time.

- source: Brandt, H., Hauert, C., & Sigmund, K. (2003). Punishment and reputation in spatial public goods games. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in spatial public goods games with three players, the introduction of punishment opportunities transforms the system from one where cooperators and defectors can coexist (with cooperation only possible above a relatively high threshold r_c ≈ 2.07) to one where the threshold for stable cooperation is much lower (r_c ≈ 1.35 with punishment, and r_c ≈ 1.25 with both punishment and reputation). This means that punishment enables cooperative, group-beneficial outcomes under a wider range of game parameters. The presence of punishment eliminates coexistence and leads to bistability: either a fully cooperative or a fully defecting state, depending on initial conditions. Reputation further enhances cooperation and fairness by making the cooperative state more robust and reducing the prevalence of non-punishing cooperators. The findings are based on evolutionary game-theoretic modeling and simulations, not on laboratory or field experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in spatial public goods games increases the likelihood of efficient, cooperative outcomes, as measured by the range of parameters (specifically, the multiplication factor r) under which cooperation is stable. The reduction in the threshold r_c for cooperation is a clear signal that punishment makes high-efficiency outcomes possible in settings where they would otherwise not occur. While the paper does not report efficiency as a direct ratio of actual to maximal group payoff, the shift in r_c is a strong proxy for increased group welfare. The findings are most applicable to spatial, all-or-nothing, three-player, repeated games with binary contribution and punishment choices, and may be less directly transferable to well-mixed or continuous-contribution settings.

- source: Helbing, D., Szolnoki, A., Perc, M., & Szabo, G. (2010). Evolutionary Establishment of Moral and Double Moral Standards through Spatial Interactions. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that costly punishment in spatial public goods games enables the spread and dominance of moralists (cooperators who punish), eliminating both defectors and non-punishing cooperators over time. This resolves the second-order free-rider problem and leads to higher levels of cooperation than would be expected in well-mixed populations, where defectors dominate. The effect depends on the punishment fine-to-cost ratio and the synergy factor (MPCR analog), with higher synergy and/or higher fine-to-cost ratios favoring the spread of moralists. The presence of a small number of defectors can accelerate the spread of moralists. The model shows that spatial structure is crucial: in well-mixed populations, punishment does not prevent the tragedy of the commons, but in spatial settings, punishment can establish and maintain cooperation. While the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, the dominance of moralists and elimination of defectors implies a more efficient outcome than the baseline.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling punishment in spatially structured public goods games can substantially increase group efficiency by promoting the dominance of punishing cooperators (moralists) and eliminating defectors and non-punishing cooperators. The effect is sensitive to the punishment fine-to-cost ratio and the synergy factor (MPCR), but is robust to group size and network structure as long as spatial clustering exists. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment (with sufficient effectiveness and not too high cost) in a spatial or networked setting should be expected to increase efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition, especially when the control is dominated by defectors. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency, so predictions should be qualitative or based on the expected dominance of cooperative strategies.

- source: Flores, L. S., Fernandes, H. C. M., Amaral, M. A., & Vainstein, M. H. (2021). Symbiotic behaviour in the public goods game with altruistic punishment. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial PGG, the introduction of altruistic punishment allows for the survival of cooperation in a wider range of parameters than in the classic PGG. For low punishment costs, clusters of punishers can survive and support cooperation. At intermediate punishment costs, both punishers and cooperators are vulnerable to defectors. At high punishment costs, a symbiotic spatial structure emerges where neither cooperators nor punishers can survive alone, but together they can outcompete defectors by forming clusters with punishers in the core and cooperators at the boundary. The effect of punishment on cooperation is thus highly dependent on the cost and the spatial structure. The paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff directly, but the persistence of altruistic strategies is used as a proxy for cooperative success.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation and, by implication, efficiency in spatial PGGs is highly sensitive to the cost of punishment and the spatial structure of interactions. While it does not report efficiency directly, it shows that punishment can expand the parameter region where cooperation survives, especially when spatial clustering allows for symbiosis between cooperators and punishers. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that punishment is most effective at low cost, can be detrimental at intermediate cost, and can again be effective at high cost if spatial symbiosis is possible. The results are most applicable to spatially structured games with local interactions and may not generalize to well-mixed or non-spatial settings.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2013). Effectiveness of conditional punishment for the evolution of public cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that conditional punishment, where the severity of punishment is proportional to the number of punishers in a group, is more effective than unconditional punishment in promoting cooperation in spatial public goods games, especially when punishment is costly. Conditional punishers can maintain cooperation at lower fines and costs, and are more efficient in terms of cooperation per unit cost. Unconditional punishers only outperform conditional punishers when punishment is very cheap. The results are robust across different values of the multiplication factor (mpcr), and the findings are explained through the mechanism of indirect territorial competition and pattern formation on the spatial grid.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in spatial public goods games, the design of the punishment mechanism (conditional vs. unconditional) and the cost-to-fine ratio are critical moderators of the effect of punishment on cooperation and cost-effectiveness. When predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, the results suggest that conditional punishment is more likely to yield higher cooperation per unit cost, especially when punishment is not cheap. However, the paper does not directly report group efficiency as a fraction of the maximum possible payoff, so predictions about absolute efficiency should be made with caution. The findings are most applicable to spatially structured games with local interactions and no communication.

- source: BOYD, R., & RICHERSON, P. J. (1992). PUNISHMENT ALLOWS THE EVOLUTION OF COOPERATION (OR ANYTHING ELSE) IN SIZABLE GROUPS. *Ethology and Sociobiology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in n-person public goods games, the introduction of targeted punishment (retribution) allows for the stable evolution of cooperation in larger groups than is possible with reciprocity alone. When the benefits to a punisher from induced cooperation exceed the costs of punishing, cooperation and punishment can coexist at equilibrium, leading to higher average payoffs (i.e., greater efficiency) than in the absence of punishment. The model also shows that 'moralistic' strategies—where individuals punish both noncooperators and those who fail to punish—can stabilize cooperation (and any other costly behavior) even when the group benefit is not sufficient to compensate individual punishers. Increasing group size makes cooperation harder to sustain, but the negative effect is less severe with punishment than with reciprocity. The analysis is theoretical and does not provide empirical data, but it directly models payoff flows and evolutionary stability.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public goods games increases group efficiency (payoff) relative to the no-punishment baseline, especially in larger groups and with repeated interactions. The model specifies how the costs and effectiveness of punishment, group size, and the expected number of rounds interact to determine whether cooperation (and thus higher efficiency) can be sustained. It also warns that if punishment is too costly or group size is too large, cooperation may not evolve. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, given knowledge of group size, punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and the structure of repeated interactions. However, the results are theoretical and assume evolutionary dynamics rather than short-run behavioral responses.

- source: Pacheco, J. M., Vasconcelos, V. V., & Santos, F. C. (2014). Climate change governance, cooperation and self-organization. *Physics of Life Reviews*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing local (group-level) sanctioning institutions (punishment) in threshold public goods games with risk of collective loss significantly increases the fraction of groups that successfully achieve the public good, especially under low risk perception and high contribution thresholds. Global (population-wide) sanctioning institutions provide only marginal improvement. Smaller group sizes and higher risk perception also promote higher group achievement. Threshold uncertainty (uncertainty about the required contribution level) can sharply reduce group achievement. The model suggests that decentralized, polycentric punishment is more effective than centralized punishment for promoting cooperation and group success.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that enabling punishment (specifically, local/group-level sanctioning institutions) in threshold public goods games with risk of collective loss will increase group achievement (a close proxy for efficiency), especially when group size is small, risk perception is high, and contribution thresholds are stringent. The effect of punishment is much stronger when implemented locally rather than globally. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of local punishment should be expected to yield a more positive effect on efficiency-related outcomes than global punishment or no punishment, particularly in small groups and under high risk. However, the main outcome is group achievement, not exact efficiency, so predictions should be interpreted accordingly.

- source: Greenwood, G. W., Abbass, H. A., & Petraki, E. (2018). When is altruistic punishment useful in social dilemmas?. *Biosystems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that altruistic punishment in a public goods game is only effective at promoting cooperation (and thus improving group outcomes) if the frequency of punishers exceeds a threshold determined by the cost of contribution and the punishment level. If this threshold is not met, defection remains the dominant strategy and group efficiency does not improve. The analysis provides explicit formulas for the returns to each strategy and the minimum frequency of punishers required for punishment to be effective. The findings imply that simply enabling punishment is not sufficient to improve efficiency; the population composition and punishment parameters are critical.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting when punishment will improve efficiency in public goods games. It shows that the effect of punishment on efficiency is conditional: punishment only increases efficiency if the proportion of punishers is above a threshold that depends on the cost of contribution and the punishment level. For prediction tasks, this means that the presence of punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency; the effectiveness depends on the game parameters (contribution cost, punishment cost/impact) and the frequency of punishers. The explicit formulas can be used to estimate the required conditions for punishment to be effective, but the paper does not provide empirical efficiency values or group payoff data.

- source: Liu, X. S., & Guo, W. L. (2010). Persistent cooperators in nature. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game with compulsory participation, if contributors are willing to pay a second cost to retrieve a portion of the public good (persistent cooperation), this can functionally act as punishment and promote cooperation. When the second cost is not too high relative to the benefit, persistent cooperators can dominate the population, leading to higher average payoffs and more stable cooperation. The model provides explicit conditions (in terms of benefit-to-cost ratios and the size of the second cost) under which persistent cooperation is evolutionarily stable, risk-dominant, or advantageous. When the second cost is high, persistent cooperators act as traditional costly punishers and are less favored. The abundance of persistent cooperators is used as a proxy for group welfare and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms (modeled as a second cost paid by persistent cooperators) can increase the prevalence of cooperation and, by extension, group efficiency, as long as the cost of punishment is not too high relative to the benefit. The model gives explicit parameter thresholds for when punishment is beneficial. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling punishment (with moderate cost and sufficient benefit) in a public goods game is likely to increase efficiency compared to a control without punishment, especially when the punishment cost is not excessive. The model also highlights the importance of the ratio between the benefit of cooperation, the cost of contributing, and the cost of punishment in determining the outcome. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions of efficiency, and its results are theoretical rather than empirical.

- source: Milinski, M. (2016). Reputation, a universal currency for human social interactions. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper reviews evidence that punishment in public goods games can maintain high levels of cooperation, but often at a net cost due to the expenses of punishing and being punished. Reputation mechanisms, such as indirect reciprocity, can also sustain cooperation and do so more efficiently by avoiding these costs. When both punishment and reputation mechanisms are available, punishment is used less frequently and more selectively, and overall group efficiency is increased. The review also notes that reward mechanisms are generally less effective than punishment in sustaining cooperation. The findings are based on a synthesis of multiple experimental studies, with particular emphasis on the interaction between punishment and reputation in public goods environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper supports the prediction that enabling punishment in public goods games generally increases cooperation and can improve group efficiency, especially when combined with reputation mechanisms. However, punishment alone may not always increase efficiency due to its costs. The review provides indirect evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by the presence of reputation systems and the ability to observe or communicate about others' actions. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the efficiency gain from punishment is context-dependent and is maximized when reputation mechanisms are also present. The review does not provide new quantitative estimates but synthesizes qualitative and comparative findings from the literature.

- source: Skatova, A., & Ferguson, E. (2013). Individual differences in behavioural inhibition explain free riding in public good games when punishment is expected but not implemented. *Behavioral and Brain Functions*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that both the implementation and the expectation of punishment (even when not actually implemented) increase individual contributions in a one-shot PGG compared to standard PGGs without punishment. Implemented punishment leads to higher contributions than non-implemented punishment. Individual differences in BIS-anxiety (a measure of behavioral inhibition) predict higher contributions and less free riding when punishment is expected but not implemented. FFFS (flight, fight, freeze system) does not predict contributions. The main outcomes are behavioral (contribution levels, free riding), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that both implemented and expected (but not implemented) centralized punishment increase contributions in a one-shot PGG, with implemented punishment having a stronger effect. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency changes due to punishment. The findings are most useful for predicting changes in contribution rates under different punishment regimes and for understanding the role of individual differences in behavioral inhibition. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that enabling punishment (even if not always implemented) increases cooperation, but the magnitude of efficiency gains cannot be directly inferred from the reported outcomes.

- source: Gächter, S., Herrmann, B., & Thöni, C. (2010). Culture and cooperation. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a standard public goods game, the opportunity to punish increases average contributions in some cultures but not in others. Cultural background explains a much larger share of the variance in contributions when punishment is available than when it is not. Punishment patterns themselves (including antisocial punishment) differ strongly across cultures and are linked to differences in cooperation. The presence of punishment reduces within-group variance in contributions but has mixed effects on between-group variance. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but higher contributions in the punishment condition in some cultures suggest likely higher efficiency in those cases, while in other cultures the effect is negligible.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by implication, efficiency) is highly culture-dependent, even in otherwise identical public goods game designs. For prediction tasks, this means that game design dimensions alone (such as player count, MPCR, punishment cost, etc.) are not sufficient to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency without accounting for cultural context. The paper's results suggest that, in some environments, punishment can substantially increase cooperation (and likely efficiency), while in others it has little or no effect. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, its quantitative value for efficiency prediction is limited to inferring that higher contributions generally imply higher efficiency.

- source: Molleman, L., Kölle, F., Starmer, C., & Gächter, S. (2019). People prefer coordinated punishment in cooperative interactions. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that in a one-shot, three-player binary public goods game with a costly punishment stage, a substantial fraction of participants prefer to punish free riders only if another group member also punishes (coordinated punishment). This preference is especially prevalent among those who do not punish unconditionally. The presence of coordinated punishment is robust across two large samples and is not correlated with conditional cooperation preferences. The study provides detailed behavioral evidence on how punishment decisions are conditioned on others' actions, but does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs with or without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative about the behavioral mechanisms of punishment in public goods games, especially the prevalence and motivations for coordinated punishment. However, it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff, nor does it compare efficiency between punishment-enabled and punishment-disabled conditions. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper suggests that the structure of punishment (e.g., whether it is coordinated) may matter, but does not provide quantitative or comparative efficiency data. The game design dimensions are well specified, which may help in mapping to other studies that do report efficiency outcomes.

- source: Espín, A. M., Brañas-Garza, P., Gamella, J. F., Herrmann, B., & Martín, J. (2022). Culture and group-functional punishment behaviour. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that Gitanos punish much less than non-Gitanos in homogeneous groups, but increase punishment (especially Gitano males) in mixed groups with non-Gitanos, targeting Gitano free-riders. Non-Gitanos punish more overall, and in mixed groups, non-Gitano males punish Gitano free-riders more harshly than non-Gitano free-riders. Antisocial punishment (punishing cooperators) is generally low, but somewhat higher when outgroup members are present. Gender moderates punishment: Gitano females almost never punish, while males increase punishment in mixed groups. Contribution rates are high and do not differ significantly by ethnicity or group composition. The paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides detailed evidence on how punishment behavior varies by culture, gender, and group composition in a one-shot PGG with punishment. However, it does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design and behavioral results may be useful for understanding moderators of punishment behavior, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. If using this paper for prediction, one should note that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not measured, and that the main findings concern who punishes whom, not whether punishment increases group payoff.

- source: Schroeder, K. B., Nettle, D., & McElreath, R. (2015). Interactions between personality and institutions in cooperative behaviour in humans. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that the opportunity for decentralized punishment in a repeated public goods game increases contributions to the public good compared to a no-punishment baseline. This effect is especially pronounced for individuals high in extraversion, who tend to free-ride in the absence of punishment but increase their contributions when punishment is possible. Agreeableness is associated with higher initial contributions regardless of institution, while neuroticism is associated with lower contributions in both conditions. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the increase in contributions with punishment suggests a likely increase in efficiency, subject to the cost of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in a repeated public goods game increases contributions, particularly among certain personality types (notably extraverts). However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect on efficiency cannot be quantified from this paper alone. The game design dimensions are well specified, and the punishment mechanism is standard (costly, decentralized, 1:3 cost-to-impact ratio). The findings support the general prediction that punishment increases cooperation, but do not allow direct prediction of efficiency outcomes for the downstream task.

- source: Gächter, S., & Herrmann, B. (2009). Reciprocity, culture and human cooperation: previous insights and a new cross-cultural experiment. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that in one-shot public goods games, the introduction of punishment opportunities increases contributions in Swiss participant pools but not in Russian pools. Antisocial punishment (punishing cooperators) is much more common in Russia, leading to lower earnings and less effective cooperation. The paper reports that 80% of Russian participants earned less than the selfish prediction, compared to 33% in Switzerland. However, the study does not directly report group efficiency (payoff relative to the cooperative maximum) or compare average payoffs between punishment and no-punishment conditions. The findings highlight that the effect of punishment on cooperation and earnings is strongly moderated by cultural context, with punishment sometimes failing to improve, or even reducing, group welfare due to antisocial punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation and earnings in public goods games is highly context-dependent, especially on cultural background. While the design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost, etc.) are well specified, the key moderator in this study is culture, which is not a standard game design variable. The paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency (payoff relative to maximum) from control efficiency and design dimensions, but it does show that punishment can increase, decrease, or have no effect on group earnings depending on the prevalence of antisocial punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of antisocial punishment (which may be proxied by cultural or social variables) is a critical moderator, and that design dimensions alone may be insufficient to predict efficiency effects of punishment without accounting for such context.

- source: Espín, A. M., Brañas-Garza, P., Herrmann, B., & Gamella, J. F. (2012). Patient and impatient punishers of free-riders. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that punishment in a one-shot public goods game is implemented by two distinct types of individuals: patient cooperators (who punish free-riders, likely for moralistic/future-oriented reasons) and impatient free-riders (who punish other free-riders, likely for competitive/spiteful reasons). The likelihood of punishing is positively associated with cooperation among patient individuals and with free-riding among impatient individuals. The paper does not analyze or report the effect of punishment on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides no direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. It does, however, offer insight into the psychological heterogeneity of punishers, which may be relevant for understanding the mechanisms behind punishment behavior. For the downstream prediction task, this paper does not provide usable data on efficiency outcomes or how game design dimensions affect efficiency with or without punishment. Its main value is in highlighting that the motivations for punishment can differ (moralistic vs. competitive) depending on individual patience and contribution behavior, but this is not tied to efficiency outcomes.

- source: Bone, J., Silva, A. S., & Raihani, N. J. (2014). Defectors, not norm violators, are punished by third-parties. *Biology Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment in a one-shot public goods game is overwhelmingly directed at defectors, regardless of whether defection is the norm in the group. The manipulation of norm violation (defection being typical or atypical) does not affect the likelihood of defectors being punished. Cooperators are rarely punished, even when they are in the minority. The cost structure of punishment is fixed, so punishing multiple players is not more expensive than punishing one. Social rejection patterns mirror punishment: defectors are less preferred as future partners, regardless of norm status. The study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a one-shot PGG with third-party punishment and a fixed punishment cost, punishment is targeted at defectors rather than norm violators. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design details (one-shot, third-party punishment, fixed cost, no communication, binary contribution) are informative for mapping to other studies, but the lack of payoff outcomes limits its direct utility for efficiency prediction. The findings suggest that, under these design features, punishment is likely to be used against defectors, but the impact on group efficiency remains unmeasured.

- source: Hintze, A., & Adami, C. (2015). Punishment in public goods games leads to meta-stable phase transitions and hysteresis. *Physical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that allowing punishment in public goods games shifts the critical synergy parameter (r) required for cooperation to emerge to lower values, making cooperation possible under less favorable conditions. Punishment acts as a catalyst for cooperation, even if it is used sparingly, and can stabilize cooperation below the critical point. However, punishment can also stabilize defection above the critical point if defectors reach a critical density. The system exhibits meta-stable phase transitions and hysteresis: once cooperation is established, it can persist even as conditions worsen, and vice versa. The main reported outcomes are the probabilities of cooperation and punishment, and the critical values of the synergy parameter, not direct efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the presence and parameters of punishment (cost, effectiveness) in a public goods game can substantially lower the threshold (synergy r) at which cooperation emerges. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the probability of cooperation is a strong proxy for efficiency in this context, as full cooperation would maximize group payoff. The findings suggest that, for a given game design, enabling punishment (especially if it is effective and not too costly) will increase the likelihood of high cooperation and thus higher efficiency, particularly near the critical synergy threshold. However, the paper does not provide quantitative efficiency values, so its support is indirect and best used for qualitative prediction or mechanistic understanding.

- source: de Weerd, H., & Verbrugge, R. (2011). Evolution of altruistic punishment in heterogeneous populations. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in both infinite well-mixed and spatially structured populations, heterogeneity in the cost of punishing (and, when combined, in the effectiveness of punishment) can make it easier for altruistic punishment and cooperation to stabilize. Specialization occurs, with the most efficient punishers (those with low cost and/or high impact) carrying out most of the punishment. In the spatial model, this effect is robust and can lead to higher prevalence of altruistic strategies. However, the paper does not directly analyze or report group efficiency, welfare, or total payoff; instead, it focuses on the prevalence and stability of cooperative and punishing strategies as the main outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that heterogeneity in punishment cost and effectiveness can facilitate the evolution and maintenance of altruistic punishment and cooperation in public goods games, especially when efficient punishers are present. However, because the main outcomes are the prevalence of altruistic strategies rather than direct measures of group efficiency or payoff, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results suggest that, under certain heterogeneity conditions, punishment is more likely to support stable cooperation, which is typically associated with higher efficiency, but this link is not directly quantified in the paper. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper provides contextual and mechanistic support but not direct quantitative evidence.

- source: Odouard, V. V., Smirnova, D., & Edelman, S. (2023). Polarize, catalyze, and stabilize: how a minority of norm internalizers amplify group selection and punishment. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The presence of norm internalizers in the strategy space (even as a small minority, around 10% of the population) leads to higher mean cooperation rates, longer and more stable periods of high cooperation, and fewer long periods of low cooperation, compared to conditions with only group selection and punishment. This effect is most pronounced at intermediate benefit-to-cost ratios, where cooperation is possible but not guaranteed. The mechanism is indirect: norm internalizers polarize groups (increasing inter-group variance), catalyze spikes in cooperation when global cooperation is low, and stabilize high-cooperation states. The effect is not due to norm internalizers themselves cooperating more, but to their influence on the behavior of other agents. The paper does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare outcomes, so the direct effect on efficiency is not established.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in agent-based models of public goods games with group selection and imperfect punishment, adding a minority of norm internalizers can substantially increase mean cooperation rates, especially at intermediate benefit-to-cost ratios. However, because the reported outcomes are cooperation rates and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used as indirect evidence for predicting efficiency: higher cooperation rates likely imply higher efficiency, but the mapping is not quantified here. The model assumes punishment is imperfect (not all defections are observed), and punishment is implemented as withholding benefits rather than costly punishment. The results are robust across two different model structures (abstract and spatial/naturalistic). For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of norm internalization mechanisms can amplify the effect of punishment and group selection on cooperation, but does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency.

- source: Kaiping, G. A., Cox, S. J., & Sluckin, T. J. (2016). Cooperation and punishment in community-structured populations with migration. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in community-structured populations playing a linear PGG with punishment, the presence of group competition (where entire communities can replace each other based on average fitness) or fitness-based migration (where individuals are more likely to migrate if they are fitter) can sustain high levels of cooperation and punishment, suppressing defection in parameter regions where defection would otherwise dominate. Constant (random) migration does not have this effect. The transition between cooperative and defective regimes is sharper with migration, but only group competition and fitness-based migration expand the parameter space where cooperation and punishment are stable. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of strategies, not efficiency or group payoff, but higher frequencies of cooperation and punishment imply higher group payoffs relative to defection-dominated regimes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task: in PGG-like environments with punishment, the presence of group competition or fitness-based migration mechanisms can substantially increase the prevalence of cooperation and punishment, and thus are likely to increase efficiency or group payoff compared to control games without punishment. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so the evidence is based on the frequencies of cooperative and punishing strategies. The results suggest that, for the same game design dimensions, enabling punishment in a context with group competition or fitness-based migration will likely lead to higher efficiency than in well-mixed or constant migration settings. The findings are most relevant for models with large group sizes, high mutation and migration rates, and community structure.

- source: García, J., & Traulsen, A. (2012). Leaving the loners alone: Evolution of cooperation in the presence of antisocial punishment. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the evolutionary success of cooperation and punishment in public goods games with voluntary participation depends critically on whether loners (abstainers) can be punished or can punish others. If loners are truly isolated (neither punish nor are punished), cooperators who punish defectors become dominant in the population, even when antisocial punishment is allowed. If loners can be punished, cooperation and punishment do not evolve, and the population is split among cooperators, defectors, and loners, with no strategy dominating. The results are robust across different strategy sets and selection intensities. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff; instead, it focuses on the abundance of strategies as the main outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the ability to punish or be punished, especially for abstainers (loners), is a critical moderator of whether punishment can support cooperation in public goods games. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that if the game design allows abstainers to be punished, punishment is unlikely to increase efficiency or cooperation. If abstainers are truly isolated, punishment can support the dominance of cooperative strategies. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The results are most useful for understanding which game design features (especially the treatment of abstainers) are likely to enable or undermine the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation.

- source: Fehr, E., & Schurtenberger, I. (2018). Normative foundations of human cooperation. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that social norms, particularly conditional cooperation norms, are central to explaining cooperation in public goods games. Punishment opportunities increase cooperation rates, but the welfare (efficiency) effects are often limited or negative due to the costs of punishment and the occurrence of antisocial punishment. Normative constraints on punishment—such as those enabled by institutional or group-level norm formation—can reduce antisocial punishment and make punishment more effective, leading to higher cooperation with less collateral cost. The review emphasizes that the presence of punishment alone is not sufficient for efficiency gains; the structure and normative context of punishment matter greatly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and integrative evidence that enabling punishment in public goods games increases cooperation rates, but does not reliably increase efficiency or group payoff unless punishment is normatively constrained to reduce antisocial punishment and collateral costs. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on the presence of normative or institutional constraints, the prevalence of antisocial punishment, and the cultural context. Game design dimensions that affect the cost, targeting, and normative context of punishment are likely to be critical moderators of efficiency outcomes. The paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency changes, but it cautions against assuming that increased cooperation from punishment always translates into higher efficiency.

- source: Akdeniz, A., & van Veelen, M. (2021). The evolution of morality and the role of commitment. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper argues that commitment is a central mechanism for the evolution of cooperation, punishment, and other moral behaviors in humans. It reviews evidence and theory suggesting that commitment to punishment can sustain cooperation in public goods games and prisoners' dilemmas, even when punishment is costly. The authors contend that standard models based on population structure or repetition do not fully explain observed human behavior, and that commitment (and the ability to recognize commitment in others) is necessary for the evolution of costly punishment and conditional cooperation. The paper discusses the implications for efficiency and group welfare in a theoretical sense but does not provide quantitative predictions or empirical results on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for why punishment can sustain cooperation in public goods games and similar environments, emphasizing the role of commitment. However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper supports the idea that the presence of punishment (and the ability to commit to punishment) can change the strategic environment and potentially increase cooperation, but it does not allow for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes based on game design dimensions. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of commitment and recognizability of commitment for punishment to be effective.

- source: Fehr, E., & Fischbacher, U. (2004). Social norms and human cooperation. *Trends in Cognitive Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the opportunity to punish norm violators in public goods games leads to a dramatic increase in cooperation rates, often approaching full cooperation. Punishment is frequently administered by group members or even third parties, and is motivated by non-selfish concerns for norm enforcement rather than self-interest. The costliness of punishment reduces its frequency and strength, but punishment remains prevalent even when it is costly. The presence of punishment opportunities transforms the dynamics of cooperation, preventing the typical decline in contributions over time seen in standard public goods games without punishment. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence or quantitative analysis of efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong qualitative evidence that enabling punishment in public goods games leads to much higher and more stable cooperation rates, and that the effect is robust across various experimental designs. For the downstream prediction task, the paper supports the expectation that introducing punishment will increase efficiency, provided that efficiency is closely linked to contribution rates. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes directly, its value is primarily in establishing the behavioral mechanism and the robustness of the punishment effect on cooperation. It also highlights that the cost and effectiveness of punishment, as well as group size and information structure, are important moderators, but does not provide quantitative estimates for these effects on efficiency.

- source: Gintis, H. (2011). Gene-culture coevolution and the nature of human sociality. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper synthesizes theoretical and empirical literature to argue that human cooperation and the willingness to punish defectors in public goods games are products of gene-culture coevolution. It reviews evidence that, in public goods games, cooperation typically decays over time unless costly punishment is available, in which case cooperation is sustained or even increases. The paper references Fehr & Gächter's experiments showing that allowing punishment prevents the decay of cooperation, but does not report efficiency or group payoff data. The main findings are about the prevalence and rationality of prosocial and punitive behaviors, not about their impact on group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and qualitative support for the idea that enabling punishment in public goods games increases cooperation rates, and references classic experiments that show this effect. However, it does not provide quantitative evidence or direct analysis of efficiency or group payoff outcomes, which limits its direct utility for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions. The referenced experiments (e.g., Fehr & Gächter) are more directly relevant for such predictions, but this paper itself does not supply the necessary outcome data.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper reviews and interprets experimental evidence (notably from Fehr & Gächter) showing that in public goods games, the availability of punishment leads to higher and increasing contributions over rounds, in contrast to the decline seen without punishment. Punishment is frequently used, mostly by high contributors targeting low contributors. The authors argue that punishment can induce and stabilize cooperation, and discuss the evolutionary puzzle of why individuals punish at a cost to themselves. However, the paper does not report or analyze efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral outcomes such as contribution rates and punishment frequency.

However, it does not provide quantitative or direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it analyze how game design dimensions moderate the effect of punishment on efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative: it supports the expectation that punishment increases cooperation, which is often associated with higher efficiency, but does not provide data or analysis to calibrate this relationship or its dependence on design features.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper reviews evidence that punishment of non-cooperators in Public Goods Games and related paradigms increases cooperation and reduces free-riding, with punishment typically administered by cooperators and targeted at defectors. It discusses the neural correlates of punishment and cooperation, the evolutionary rationale for sanctioning, and the influence of individual differences (e.g., personality, genetics, psychopathology) and contextual factors (e.g., culture, gender, resource availability) on social exchange behavior. However, it does not provide quantitative or direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff effects of punishment.

- source: Fehr, E., & Rockenbach, B. (2004). Human altruism: economic, neural, and evolutionary perspectives. *Current Opinion in Neurobiology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper reviews evidence that the availability of costly punishment in public goods games leads to much higher and more stable cooperation levels compared to games without punishment. Voluntary selection into punishment regimes further increases cooperation compared to exogenous assignment. The review also highlights that punishment is only effective at promoting cooperation when it is perceived as fair or socially justified. Neuroeconomic studies show that both cooperation and the act of punishing defectors activate reward-related brain circuits, suggesting a neural basis for strong reciprocity. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence or quantitative results on efficiency or group payoff, instead focusing on behavioral outcomes and neural mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong qualitative support that enabling punishment in public goods games increases cooperation and contributions, especially when punishment is voluntary and perceived as justified. However, it does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. For prediction tasks, this review supports the expectation that punishment will increase efficiency when it increases cooperation, but the magnitude and conditions for this effect must be inferred from other sources or from the primary studies it cites.

It highlights that when punishment is available, cooperation is maintained, and when it is removed, cooperation declines. The review also discusses the emotional and neural correlates of cooperation and punishment, such as the role of negative emotions in driving punishment and the involvement of brain regions like the insula, striatum, and DLPFC. However, the paper does not provide quantitative or direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it systematically analyze how game design dimensions moderate these effects.

For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is best used as background for the general mechanism that punishment increases cooperation, but not for precise quantitative prediction or for understanding the impact of specific design features.

- source: Singer, T., & Steinbeis, N. (2009). Differential Roles of Fairness- and Compassion-Based Motivations for Cooperation, Defection, and Punishment. *Values, Empathy, and Fairness Across Social Barriers*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
