  findings: The paper finds that punishment can promote the evolution of cooperation (and thus higher group payoffs) in both well-mixed and lattice-structured populations, but only under certain conditions. In the score-dependent viability model, punishment is more effective at suppressing defectors and promoting cooperation than in the fertility model. The presence of selfish punishers (defectors who punish other defectors) can help the evolution of altruistic punishers by first eliminating pure defectors, but pure altruists (who cooperate but do not punish) can hinder the evolution of punishment by being exploited. The benefit of cooperation, the fine imposed by punishment, and the cost of punishing all interact to determine evolutionary outcomes. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus context-dependent and can be positive or negative depending on parameter values and population structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency (group payoff) in public-goods-like games depends on the interaction of several design dimensions: population structure (well-mixed vs. lattice), the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the benefit of cooperation, and the evolutionary update rule (viability vs. fertility). Punishment is more likely to increase efficiency when the punishment fine is large relative to the cost of punishing, and in spatially structured populations. However, if the cost of punishing is too high or if pure cooperators are common, punishment may not evolve or may even reduce cooperation. The findings suggest that predictions of treatment efficiency from control efficiency should account for these moderators, especially the structure of interactions and the punishment parameters. However, as the paper does not report explicit efficiency values, its support is indirect and based on evolutionary stability of cooperation.

- source: Nakamaru, M., & Dieckmann, U. (2009). Runaway selection for cooperation and strict-and-severe punishment. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in spatially structured populations, the co-evolution of cooperation and punishment (with continuous strategies for both) can lead to runaway selection for both high cooperation and strict-and-severe punishment. This dynamic is most pronounced when punishment is not too costly and cooperation is neither too cheap nor too expensive. The model shows that strict punishment norms (high strictness and severity) evolve naturally and reinforce high cooperation, resulting in much higher group-level cooperation than in the absence of punishment. In contrast, in well-mixed populations, punishment and cooperation do not evolve. The findings suggest that punishment can greatly enhance group cooperation and, by implication, group efficiency, but only under certain cost structures and spatial conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in spatially structured public-goods-like environments can substantially increase group cooperation and, by extension, efficiency—especially when punishment is strict, severe, and not too costly. The model suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on the cost structure of cooperation and punishment, and on the population structure (spatial vs. well-mixed). For prediction tasks, the findings imply that punishment will have a more positive effect on efficiency when: (1) the game is spatially structured (analogous to local interactions), (2) punishment is strict and severe, (3) costs of punishment and cooperation are not too high, and (4) baseline cooperation is low without punishment. However, the model is theoretical and does not provide direct empirical efficiency ratios, so its quantitative predictions should be interpreted with caution.

- source: Currie, T. E., Campenni, M., Flitton, A., Njagi, T., Ontiri, E., Perret, C., & Walker, L. (2021). The cultural evolution and ecology of institutions. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper argues that institutions, including punishment mechanisms, can alter the payoffs in collective action problems such as public goods games, making cooperation more stable and frequent. The agent-based model demonstrates that when agents can collectively set punishment levels, cooperation can emerge and be maintained, with higher payoffs for cooperators and a reduction in free-riders. The model is illustrative and not based on empirical data. Macro-scale analyses show that institutional complexity and inclusivity are associated with higher economic development, but these are not tested in controlled PGG-like settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that introducing punishment mechanisms in public goods games can increase cooperation and, in model terms, improve payoffs for cooperators. However, it does not provide empirical estimates of efficiency or group payoff changes due to punishment in controlled experimental settings. The agent-based model suggests that the ability to set and adjust punishment can help overcome collective action problems, but the findings are illustrative and not directly transferable to quantitative prediction tasks. The paper is useful for understanding mechanisms and for contextualizing the role of punishment, but not for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions.

- source: Couto, M. C., Pacheco, J. M., & Santos, F. C. (2020). Governance of risky public goods under graduated punishment. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Graduated institutional punishment, where the severity and cost of punishment increase with the number of defectors, is more effective than strict (fixed) punishment in promoting cooperation and public good achievement in collective-risk dilemmas. This effect is especially pronounced at low risk levels and when both fines and costs are graduated. The model shows that graduated punishment reduces the time populations spend in all-defector states and increases the prevalence of cooperation and sustainable institutions. The approach also helps solve the second-order free-rider problem associated with institutional punishment. The findings are robust across a range of parameters and extend to linear public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in threshold public goods games with risk and institutional punishment, making punishment and its costs graduated (i.e., increasing with the number of defectors) leads to higher group achievement and overall welfare than strict, fixed punishment. While the main outcomes are not reported as efficiency ratios, the close relationship between group achievement, average fitness, and efficiency means the results are highly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that, for games with similar structure (threshold, risk, institutional punishment), enabling graduated punishment will likely increase efficiency relative to strict punishment, especially at low risk and when punishment costs are also graduated. The results are robust to changes in group size, threshold, and other parameters.

- source: Gardner, A., & West, S. A. (2004). Cooperation and punishment, especially in humans. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment can promote and stabilize cooperation in public-goods-like environments, leading to higher group fitness (efficiency), provided that individuals who punish tend to receive more cooperation. The cost of punishment decreases as cooperation increases, making punishment more sustainable in cooperative populations. Relatedness between individuals is not required for punishment to be favored; instead, the key is a positive association between an individual's punishment and the cooperation she receives. The model predicts that punishment is more easily maintained than initially evolved, and that small group size and effective punishment (where the cost of being punished exceeds the cost of cooperation) facilitate the evolutionary stability of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in public-goods-like games can increase efficiency, especially when punishment is effective (i.e., the penalty for being punished is greater than the cost of cooperation) and when there is a mechanism (such as behavioral adjustment) that links punishment to increased cooperation received. The findings suggest that, all else equal, introducing punishment should increase group efficiency, particularly in smaller groups and when punishment is not too costly to the punisher. However, the results are derived from theoretical models rather than empirical data, so quantitative predictions require caution. The paper also highlights that the effect of punishment is not dependent on relatedness or repeated interactions, but on the structure of behavioral associations.

- source: Chen, S., Shi, J. C., Yang, X. L., Ye, H., & Luo, J. (2019). Modulating Activity in the Dorsolateral Prefrontal Cortex Changes Punishment in the 3-Player Prisoner's Dilemma: A Transcranial Direct Current Stimulation Study. *Frontiers in Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that modulating DLPFC activity via tDCS can significantly decrease the amount of punishment assigned by cooperators to defectors (both in second-party and third-party punishment), depending on the stimulation mode. One bilateral stimulation (left anodal/right cathodal) increases cooperation rates. However, the study does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare as outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding neural mechanisms of punishment and cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or prisoner's dilemma games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, as it does not report these outcomes. It does, however, provide detailed evidence on how punishment behavior and cooperation rates are affected by neural modulation in a 3-player prisoner's dilemma with punishment. For the prediction task, this paper suggests that punishment behavior can be modulated by factors unrelated to game design (e.g., neural stimulation), and that cooperation can be increased by certain interventions. However, without efficiency or payoff data, the paper's direct value for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions is limited.

- source: de Melo, G., & Piaggio, M. (2015). The perils of peer punishment: Evidence from a common pool resource framed field experiment. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Nonmonetary peer punishment (social disapproval) reduces resource extraction (increases cooperation) in mixed-community groups but has no significant effect in in-group (same community) groups. The effect is measured as a reduction in the average number of nets used per person. There is substantial antisocial punishment (punishing cooperators), which can undermine the effectiveness of punishment. The study does not directly report group efficiency or total payoff, but lower extraction implies higher efficiency. The presence of antisocial punishment and lack of a unique social norm limit the positive effect of punishment. The findings suggest that the context (group composition) moderates the effect of punishment on cooperation, and that coordination is needed for punishment to be effective.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that enabling peer punishment (social disapproval) can reduce resource extraction (increase cooperation) in common pool resource games, especially in mixed-group settings. However, the effect on efficiency is not directly measured; it must be inferred from extraction levels. The presence of antisocial punishment and lack of norm consensus can limit or even reverse the positive effect of punishment. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that punishment is more likely to improve efficiency in mixed-group contexts than in homogeneous groups, but the risk of antisocial punishment and unclear norms can reduce or negate efficiency gains. The specific game design (field context, nonmonetary punishment, no communication, continuous extraction choice) should be considered when generalizing.

- source: Gatiso, T. T., Vollan, B., & Nuppenau, E. A. (2015). Resource scarcity, and democratic elections in commons dilemmas: An experiment on forest use in Ethiopia. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that resource scarcity (lower initial stock) leads to less cooperative behavior (higher HR), and that the way sanctions are implemented matters: democratically elected sanctions increase cooperation (lower HR) both under abundance and scarcity, while imposed sanctions can reduce cooperation (higher HR) under abundance (crowding-out effect), but not under scarcity. The main outcome is behavioral (HR), not efficiency or group payoff. Theoretical efficiency levels are provided, but not empirically analyzed as outcomes. The findings suggest that participatory rule-making is more effective than top-down sanctions for sustaining cooperation, especially when resources are not extremely scarce.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on how the method of sanction implementation (election vs. imposition) moderates the effect of punishment on cooperation in a common-pool resource game with real-world framing. However, because the main outcome is behavioral (harvest ratio) rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results must be mapped with caution to efficiency prediction tasks. Theoretical efficiency levels are given, but empirical results focus on HR. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, no chat, continuous contribution, no explicit MPCR, probabilistic punishment, no rewards, summary info shown, anonymous punishment) are well described, supporting mapping to prediction models. The main implication is that democratically elected sanctions are more likely to increase cooperation (and thus efficiency) than imposed sanctions, especially under resource abundance. Under scarcity, the difference is smaller, but elections still outperform imposition.

- source: Kido, Y., & Takezawa, M. (2024). Coevolution of norm psychology and cooperation through exapted conformity. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model shows that cooperation can evolve in collective action dilemmas when injunctive norms strongly encourage punishment, but not when they only encourage cooperation. Conformity (descriptive norm psychology) alone can support intermediate cooperation, but is unstable and easily undermined. When conformity is exapted (pre-existing), it can expand the conditions under which punishment-based cooperation evolves, acting as a scaffold for the emergence of stable, high-cooperation societies. The model identifies two stable types of cooperative societies: those based on conformity (unstable, lower cooperation) and those based on punishment (stable, higher cooperation). However, all outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation and punishment rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the importance of punishment norms (and the cost/benefit structure of punishment) in enabling high cooperation in public-goods-like environments, especially when conformity is present. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that punishment is more effective than conformity at sustaining high cooperation, and that the presence of conformity can expand the parameter space where punishment-based cooperation emerges. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that punishment mechanisms (especially when normatively encouraged) are likely to increase cooperation, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Van Cleve, J. (2016). Cooperation, conformity, and the coevolutionary problem of trait associations. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the coevolution of cooperation, punishment, and conformist bias in a metapopulation model requires a strong initial within-group association (high F_ST) between these traits. If this threshold is not met, cooperation and punishment do not evolve to high frequency, and conformist bias does not stabilize cooperation. The threshold is around F_ST = 0.2 for large groups. The model assumes a public-goods-type group interaction with costly cooperation and punishment, and group competition is based on accumulated resources (which are higher with more cooperation). However, the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome, focusing instead on the evolutionary dynamics of the traits.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task: it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation (and thus, by implication, efficiency) in public-goods-like environments depends critically on initial population structure (within-group association). In well-mixed or unstructured populations, punishment may not evolve or persist, and thus may not improve efficiency. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in a controlled game setting. Its findings are most relevant for understanding the conditions under which punishment can support cooperation in evolutionary or long-run settings, rather than for predicting short-run efficiency changes in experimental games.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Diekmann, A. (2013). Individual heterogeneity and costly punishment: a volunteer's dilemma. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that costly punishment, when implemented as a volunteer's dilemma, is effective in deterring defection (stealing) in groups. When only one group member needs to punish and punishment costs are heterogeneous, punishment is more efficiently coordinated (mainly by the lowest-cost punisher), and deterrence of defection is as strong as with much higher penalties in a symmetric-cost setting. The introduction of punishment reduces the stealing rate by about 50 percentage points overall, with greater reductions in conditions with high penalty or with cost heterogeneity. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoffs, but the behavioral results suggest that punishment can sustain cooperation and that heterogeneity in punishment costs facilitates more effective and efficient norm enforcement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong behavioral evidence that introducing punishment in a volunteer's dilemma setting (where only one punisher is needed) greatly reduces defection rates, especially when punishment costs are heterogeneous. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that punishment will likely increase efficiency (by reducing defection) in similar settings, and that cost heterogeneity among punishers can substitute for higher penalty size in deterring defection. However, because the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff outcomes, the quantitative effect on efficiency must be inferred indirectly from the large reductions in defection rates. The findings are most applicable to step-level public goods games with punishment opportunities and cost heterogeneity among punishers.

- source: Nhim, T., Richter, A., & Zhu, X. Q. (2019). The resilience of social norms of cooperation under resource scarcity and inequality - An agent-based model on sharing water over two harvesting seasons. *Ecological Complexity*.

  findings: The model shows that strong punishment (high punishment strength) and high social capital (frequent encounters) support the emergence and resilience of cooperation in a common-pool resource dilemma. However, resource scarcity, high variability in resource inflow, and increased inequality (in land or skill) undermine cooperation, making collapse of social norms more likely. The system tends toward either full cooperation or full defection, with little evidence for stable mixed equilibria. The model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but the collapse of cooperation is interpreted as a breakdown of socially optimal resource use.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that punishment can sustain cooperation in public-goods-like resource dilemmas, but its effectiveness is strongly moderated by resource scarcity, variability, and inequality. The model is not a direct PGG but is structurally similar, with a focus on seasonal water allocation and peer punishment. The main outcome is the presence or absence of cooperation, not efficiency or group payoff, so the paper is most useful for predicting the conditions under which punishment will be effective at sustaining cooperation, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency gains. The findings suggest that in environments with high scarcity, variability, or inequality, the positive effect of punishment on efficiency (if any) is likely to be weaker or absent.

- source: Lehmann, L., Rousset, F., Roze, D., & Keller, L. (2007). Strong reciprocity or strong ferocity? A population genetic view of the evolution of altruistic punishment. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that strong reciprocity (costly helping plus costly punishment) can evolve in spatially structured populations if helping and punishment are tightly linked (no recombination) and relatedness is sufficiently high. In this case, strong reciprocity can invade even when rare. When helping and punishment are unlinked (recombination is strong), neither costly helping nor costly punishment can invade from rarity unless punishment yields a direct fitness benefit to the actor. The model shows that kin selection (genetic or cultural) is necessary for the evolution of strong reciprocity. The analysis is theoretical and does not provide direct predictions about group efficiency or payoff, but rather about the evolutionary stability and spread of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into the evolutionary preconditions for the emergence of punishment and cooperation in public-goods-like settings, especially the importance of linkage between helping and punishment, population structure (relatedness), and direct fitness effects. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions about group efficiency or payoff with and without punishment, nor does it report outcomes directly comparable to experimental public goods games. Its main value for prediction is in highlighting that punishment is unlikely to increase cooperation or efficiency in anonymous, unstructured, or one-shot settings unless there is kinship or direct benefit to punishers. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative.

- source: Henrich, J., & Henrich, N. (2006). Culture, evolution and the puzzle of human cooperation. *Cognitive Systems Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper argues that punishment, especially when combined with cultural learning mechanisms (such as conformist and prestige-biased transmission), can stabilize costly cooperative norms in human groups. It reviews theoretical models showing that the effectiveness of punishment in maintaining cooperation depends on the cost of punishing relative to the cost of being punished, and that social norms enforced by punishment can persist even when the cooperative act is individually costly. The paper also discusses how group-level selection can favor groups with more effective cooperative norms, and how cultural evolution allows for rapid adaptation of cooperation strategies. However, the paper does not provide empirical or quantitative findings on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the mechanisms and evolutionary logic.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that punishment can stabilize cooperation in public goods-like environments, especially when punishment is effective and not too costly relative to the cost of being punished. It highlights the importance of social norms, cultural learning, and group structure in moderating the effects of punishment. However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence on efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the magnitude of efficiency changes when punishment is enabled. Its value for prediction lies in its mechanistic insights and identification of key moderators (e.g., punishment cost/effectiveness, group size, norm strength), but not in providing direct outcome estimates.

- source: Ringsmuth, A. K., Lade, S. J., & Schlüter, M. (2019). Cross-scale cooperation enables sustainable use of a common-pool resource. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that even weak social sanctioning (punishment) between communities can prevent the collapse of cooperation that would otherwise occur due to biophysical coupling (resource sharing). When only biophysical coupling is present and communities do not communicate or sanction each other, increased resource access can undermine cooperation in one community. However, if communities can sanction defectors in both communities, or if they communicate when setting norms, cooperation is more robust. The model identifies parameter regions where cooperation or defection collapses, depending on the strength of biophysical and social coupling and on whether communities collaborate in norm setting. The findings highlight the importance of cross-community sanctioning and communication for sustaining cooperation in common-pool resource systems.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment (social sanctioning) in public-goods-like environments can prevent the collapse of cooperation that may occur due to resource sharing or biophysical coupling. However, the model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so its relevance to predicting efficiency outcomes is indirect. The results suggest that the presence and strength of punishment mechanisms, as well as opportunities for communication and norm setting, are important moderators of cooperation stability. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the expectation that punishment will have a more positive effect on cooperation (and likely efficiency) when social coupling is present, especially if communities can communicate and set norms collaboratively. However, quantitative predictions about efficiency cannot be extracted from this paper.

- source: Seip, E. C., van Dijk, W. W., & Rotteveel, M. (2009). On Hotheads and Dirty Harries The Primacy of Anger in Altruistic Punishment. *Values, Empathy, and Fairness Across Social Barriers*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that anger, rather than the perception of unfairness, is the primary driver of altruistic punishment in social dilemma games. Experimental evidence from a one-shot, three-person public goods game with punishment shows that lower contributions from others evoke more anger and lead to harsher punishment, and that the effect of others' contributions on punishment is fully mediated by experienced anger. Priming participants with anger increases their tendency to punish defectors. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. However, it does suggest that the presence and intensity of punishment are strongly influenced by emotional responses (especially anger) to defection or low contribution. For prediction tasks, this implies that punishment is likely to be used when players experience anger at norm violations, but the paper does not address whether this increases or decreases group efficiency. The findings are most relevant for understanding when and why punishment is deployed, not its payoff consequences.

- source: Raihani, N. J., & Bshary, R. (2015). The reputation of punishers. *Trends in Ecology & Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the reputation consequences of punishment are context-dependent. In forced play games where punishment is possible, a punitive reputation can induce fear and stabilize cooperation, potentially supporting group efficiency. However, when partner choice is available, individuals with a nonpunishing cooperative reputation are typically preferred over punishers, unless punishment is the only available signal of cooperative intent. The efficiency of punishment (fee:fine ratio) also moderates reputational outcomes: 'efficient' punishment (fee:fine > 1) may be viewed as self-serving, while 'inefficient' punishment (fee:fine < 1) is more likely to be seen as genuinely norm-enforcing. The paper synthesizes prior empirical and theoretical work but does not provide new data or direct estimates of efficiency effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is most useful for identifying moderators and mechanisms that may affect the impact of punishment on cooperation and, by extension, efficiency in public goods games and similar environments. It highlights the importance of partner choice, the efficiency of punishment, and the availability of alternative signals of cooperation. However, it does not provide quantitative estimates or direct evidence on efficiency outcomes, so its value for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions is primarily in identifying which dimensions are likely to be important moderators (e.g., partner choice, punishment efficiency, information structure).

- source: Declerck, C. H., Boone, C., & Emonds, G. (2013). When do people cooperate? The neuroeconomics of prosocial decision making. *Brain and Cognition*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper proposes a dual-process neural framework for prosocial decision-making in social dilemmas, including public goods games and related paradigms. It reviews evidence that extrinsic incentives (such as punishment) and trust signals independently increase cooperation. Neuroimaging studies show that punishment threats activate cognitive control regions and increase cooperative behavior, particularly among self-regarding or Machiavellian individuals. The review also highlights that individual differences (e.g., social value orientation, Machiavellianism) moderate the impact of punishment and incentives on cooperation. However, the paper does not directly report or synthesize efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral and neural responses.
  prediction_guidance: This review provides strong theoretical and mechanistic support for the idea that punishment increases cooperation in public-goods-game-like environments, especially for self-regarding individuals. It suggests that the effect of punishment is likely to be more pronounced when extrinsic incentives are salient and among participants with self-regarding preferences. However, because the review does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. Its value for prediction is primarily in identifying moderators (e.g., individual differences, presence of incentives, trust signals) and in supporting the general mechanism by which punishment increases cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is best used to inform the likely direction and moderators of punishment effects, but not to calibrate the magnitude of efficiency changes.

- source: Bshary, R. (2010). Cooperation between unrelated individuals - a game theoretic approach. *Animal Behaviour: Evolution and Mechanisms*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The chapter argues that a wide variety of game-theoretic mechanisms—including punishment, sanctions, partner choice, and reciprocity—are necessary to explain the diversity of cooperative behaviors observed in nature. In n-player (public goods) games, the chapter discusses the tragedy of the commons and the theoretical role of punishment in stabilizing cooperation, especially in humans. It reviews empirical examples and theoretical models but does not provide quantitative data on efficiency or group payoff. The discussion of punishment is detailed, especially in the context of human experiments (citing Fehr & Gächter 2002 and others), but the chapter itself does not present new empirical results or efficiency measurements.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is useful for understanding the conceptual and mechanistic role of punishment in public goods and related games, and for mapping the diversity of game structures and partner control mechanisms. However, it does not provide direct quantitative evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report experimental manipulations of game design dimensions. For prediction tasks, this chapter can inform the expected direction and mechanisms by which punishment may affect cooperation, but not the magnitude or efficiency outcomes under specific game parameterizations.

- source: Inglis, R. F., West, S., & Buckling, A. (2014). An experimental study of strong reciprocity in bacteria. *Biology Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in bacterial populations, cooperative punishers (who both produce a public good and punish non-cooperators) can invade a population of cheats only when the traits for cooperation and punishment are tightly linked. When the linkage is broken (by mixing punishing and non-punishing cooperators), invasion fails. Non-punishing cooperators alone also cannot invade. The main outcome is the frequency and survival/extinction of the different strains, not group efficiency or total payoff. The results support theoretical predictions that linkage is necessary for the evolutionary success of strong reciprocity in this context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation (and by implication, group efficiency) depends critically on the linkage between cooperation and punishment. However, the study does not measure efficiency or group payoff directly, and the outcomes are evolutionary (strain invasion/extinction) rather than economic payoffs. The findings suggest that in systems where punishment and cooperation are not tightly linked, punishment may not improve group outcomes. For prediction tasks, this paper is most informative about the importance of linkage or coupling between cooperation and punishment, but less so about the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency in standard public goods games.

- source: Raihani, N. J., Thornton, A., & Bshary, R. (2012). Punishment and cooperation in nature. *Trends in Ecology & Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment can promote cooperation in laboratory public goods games with humans, but evidence for similar effects in non-human animals is rare. It argues that punishment is most likely to evolve in symmetric n-player games or asymmetric two-player games, especially when cheating is a discrete event and repeated interactions are likely. The paper highlights cognitive and ecological constraints that limit the prevalence of punishment in nature. While it discusses the theoretical efficiency of punishment (e.g., when it is wasteful or beneficial), it does not provide empirical data on efficiency or group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and conceptual guidance on when punishment is likely to increase cooperation in public goods-like settings, emphasizing the importance of player number, time horizon, and the nature of cheating. However, it does not provide empirical estimates or direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency given game design dimensions and control efficiency. Its main value is in highlighting moderators and mechanisms that may inform the interpretation of empirical studies.

- source: Buckholtz, J. W., & Marois, R. (2012). The roots of modern justice: cognitive and neural foundations of social norms and their enforcement. *Nature Neuroscience*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper argues that the ability to enforce social norms through punishment is central to the evolution and maintenance of large-scale human cooperation. It reviews evidence from behavioral economics (including public goods games and ultimatum games) showing that the possibility of punishment increases cooperation and norm compliance, and that individuals are willing to incur costs to punish norm violators. The commentary also synthesizes neuroscientific findings on the brain regions involved in norm enforcement, proposing a model in which domain-general cognitive processes (such as value learning, mentalizing, and response selection) are repurposed for social norm enforcement. The paper notes that both punishment and reward can promote cooperation, but does not provide new empirical data on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and mechanistic support for the idea that punishment increases cooperation and norm compliance in public-goods-game-like environments, referencing empirical studies that show this effect. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff outcomes with and without punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper supports the general expectation that enabling punishment will increase cooperation, but does not allow for precise prediction of efficiency changes based on game design dimensions or control efficiency. Its value is primarily in contextualizing the importance of punishment and identifying relevant behavioral and neural mechanisms.

- source: Gelcich, S., Guzman, R., Rodríguez-Sickert, C., Castilla, J. C., & Cárdenas, J. C. (2013). Exploring External Validity of Common Pool Resource Experiments: Insights from Artisanal Benthic Fisheries in Chile. *Ecology and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: This paper reports a field-lab experiment with Chilean artisanal fishers playing a 20-round common pool resource game. In the first 10 rounds (open access, no enforcement), overharvesting increased and efficiency declined, especially among low-performance unions and nonunionized fishers. In rounds 11-20, a weak enforcement regime was introduced: two players per round were randomly inspected, and if caught overharvesting, their entire round's harvest was confiscated. This sanction led to a sharp reduction in overharvest and a large increase in group earnings (efficiency) among unionized fishers, especially those from high-performance unions, who sustained cooperation and high efficiency through the end of the game. Low-performance unions showed a temporary improvement but cooperation eroded over time. Nonunionized fishers did not respond to enforcement and remained inefficient. The paper provides direct evidence that even weak sanctions can improve efficiency in CPR settings, but the effect is strongly moderated by group social capital and prior cooperative norms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a punishment/sanction mechanism (even if weak and probabilistic) in a repeated CPR game with continuous contribution/extraction choices and no communication can substantially increase group efficiency, especially when baseline efficiency is not already at floor. The effect is strongest in groups with high social capital or prior cooperative norms. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling punishment in similar CPR/Pgg-like games will increase efficiency, with the magnitude depending on group composition and baseline cooperativeness. The design features—5 players, 20 rounds, no chat, continuous extraction, no rewards, and a probabilistic, high-impact punishment—are all relevant for mapping to prediction. The control (no punishment) condition yields low efficiency, while the treatment (punishment enabled) yields much higher efficiency for unionized groups.

- source: Boyd, R., & Richerson, P. J. (2009). Voting with your feet: Payoff biased migration and the evolution of group beneficial behavior. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that payoff-biased migration—where individuals move from lower-payoff to higher-payoff groups and assimilate—can lead to the spread and predominance of group-beneficial behaviors, resulting in higher average group payoffs (efficiency). This effect is strongest when migration rates are low enough to maintain between-group cultural variation, but not so low as to prevent migration from having an effect. The model is general and can represent public goods, mutualism, and punishment-based norm enforcement. The presence of punishment is discussed as a mechanism that can stabilize group-beneficial equilibria, which are associated with higher group payoffs. The model predicts that, under plausible conditions, group-beneficial (efficient) norms will predominate, but alternative (less efficient) norms may persist in a polymorphic equilibrium.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that mechanisms which stabilize group-beneficial behaviors (including punishment) can lead to higher group efficiency, especially in structured populations with migration. The model is not a direct PGG experiment, but its payoff structure and discussion of punishment-based norm enforcement are closely related. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the presence of effective punishment (or other norm-stabilizing mechanisms) is likely to increase efficiency, particularly when migration or group structure allows for between-group variation and selective migration. However, the model is abstract and does not provide quantitative estimates for specific game design parameters such as player count, MPCR, or punishment cost, so its guidance is qualitative: enabling effective punishment or norm enforcement should increase efficiency, especially when group structure and migration allow for the persistence and spread of efficient norms.

- source: Bhui, R., Chudek, M., & Henrich, J. (2019). How exploitation launched human cooperation. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that negative indirect reciprocity (NIR)—whereby individuals are allowed to exploit those with bad reputations—can suppress exploitation and support the emergence and maintenance of cooperation and public goods provision. The model shows that cooperation is most stable when exploitation is inefficient (harm to victim > gain to exploiter), and that as the frequency of opportunities for reputation improvement increases, the minimum benefit required for stable cooperation also increases. The model provides explicit conditions under which cooperation is stable, and shows that NIR can serve as a foundation for more complex forms of cooperation. The overall effect of NIR is to increase group efficiency and welfare by reducing exploitation and supporting norm adherence, especially in early or cognitively simple societies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms (here, tolerated exploitation of ill-reputed individuals) can increase group efficiency and public goods provision, especially when exploitation is inefficient and when opportunities for norm adherence are not too frequent. The model gives explicit payoff-based conditions for when cooperation is stable, which can inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. The findings suggest that punishment is more likely to improve efficiency when the cost to the victim of exploitation is high relative to the benefit to the exploiter, and when errors in reputation assignment are low. The model also highlights that as norm adherence becomes more mandatory and frequent, the requirements for stable cooperation become stricter, potentially reducing the positive effect of punishment on efficiency in some parameter regimes.

- source: Sääksvuori, L. (2014). Intergroup conflict, ostracism, and the evolution of cooperation under free migration. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: This paper experimentally studies a dynamic public goods game with endogenous group formation, free migration, and treatments varying the presence of intergroup competition and ostracism (social exclusion by majority vote). The main findings are: (1) Ostracism increases average group size and contributions to the public good, resulting in higher net payoffs and aggregate returns, both with and without intergroup competition. (2) Intergroup competition alone does not increase group size or contributions, and leads to more imbalanced group structures, which reduces aggregate returns. (3) The combination of ostracism and competition still produces higher cooperation and payoffs than competition alone. (4) The threat of ostracism reduces the number of free-agents and increases the desirability of joining groups, outweighing the effect of involuntary exclusion. (5) Intergroup competition reduces the likelihood of ostracism, as groups are less willing to expel members when facing external threats.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that enabling a social sanctioning mechanism (ostracism) in a dynamic, migration-allowed public goods environment increases both cooperation and group-level payoffs (i.e., efficiency), regardless of whether intergroup competition is present. The effect of ostracism is robust and positive for efficiency-related outcomes. Intergroup competition alone does not improve efficiency and may even reduce it by fragmenting groups. The design features—such as endogenous group formation, free migration, and ostracism by majority vote—are important moderators. The paper does not use classic costly punishment (with explicit cost per punishment point), but ostracism functions as a group-level sanction. Thus, for environments with similar group formation and migration, the presence of ostracism is a strong positive predictor for efficiency gains from sanctioning.

- source: Powers, S. T., & Lehmann, L. (2017). When is bigger better? The effects of group size on the evolution of helping behaviours. *Biological Reviews*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that as group size increases, the marginal benefits of helping, the effectiveness of reciprocity, and relatedness all tend to decrease, making cooperation less likely to evolve or be stable in large groups. Punishment can stabilize cooperation, but its effectiveness also declines with group size unless institutional mechanisms are in place. The authors argue that human societies have overcome these limitations through the evolution of institutions that coordinate sanctioning and information sharing, thereby enabling large-scale cooperation. The paper provides a conceptual framework for understanding how game design features (such as group size, punishment, and information structure) interact to affect the evolution and maintenance of cooperation, but does not provide quantitative predictions or new data on efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding the qualitative effects of group size and institutional punishment on cooperation and efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that, all else equal, increasing group size will tend to reduce the effectiveness of both reciprocity and punishment in sustaining cooperation, unless institutional mechanisms are present to coordinate sanctioning and information. The paper highlights the importance of considering diminishing marginal returns, monitoring costs, and second-order free-riding in large groups. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the expectation that punishment will be less effective at increasing efficiency in larger groups unless institutional features (e.g., centralized punishment, coordinated monitoring) are present. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates or experimental data for efficiency outcomes, so its support is primarily conceptual and mechanistic.

- source: Rankin, D. J., Bargum, K., & Kokko, H. (2007). The tragedy of the commons in evolutionary biology. *Trends in Ecology & Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper reviews the tragedy of the commons as a framework for understanding how individual-level selfishness can reduce group-level outcomes, including group fitness and productivity, in both human and non-human systems. It highlights that mechanisms such as punishment, policing, kin selection, and ecological feedbacks can prevent or reduce the severity of the tragedy, sometimes restoring group productivity or preventing collapse. The review draws analogies to public goods games and discusses the importance of distinguishing between complete collapse (collapsing tragedy) and partial reduction in group fitness (component tragedy).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual support for the idea that punishment and related mechanisms can improve group outcomes (efficiency) in public-goods-like environments by altering incentives and deterring selfish behavior. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence that would allow direct prediction of efficiency changes from game design dimensions. Its value lies in supporting the general expectation that punishment, when effective and not too costly, can prevent or mitigate efficiency losses due to free-riding, but it does not specify how this effect depends on specific game parameters.

- source: Schlüter, M., Tavoni, A., & Levin, S. (2016). Robustness of norm-driven cooperation in the commons. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that cooperation enforced by social sanctioning is robust under certain conditions but can collapse with increased resource abundance or small increases in resource variability. High resource variability or scarcity can promote cooperation. The effectiveness of sanctioning depends on the proportion of norm followers (social capital). When two groups are ecologically connected, resource leakage from a more cooperative group can destabilize cooperation in the less cooperative group. The model highlights the complex, nonlinear interplay between social and ecological factors in sustaining cooperation. However, the main outcome is the level of cooperation, not explicit efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for predicting the effects of punishment-like mechanisms (here, non-costly social disapproval) on cooperation in CPR/public goods settings, especially under varying resource conditions. However, it does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it manipulate or report most of the game design dimensions used in the prediction task. The model's focus on non-costly, endogenous social sanctioning differs from standard experimental punishment treatments. Thus, its relevance for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games is limited and mainly contextual/mechanistic.

  findings: The paper synthesizes theoretical results on how various mechanisms (iteration, spatial structure, indirect reciprocity, punishment, reward) affect the evolution and maintenance of cooperation in social dilemmas. It notes that spatial structure promotes cooperation in the Prisoner's Dilemma but can reduce cooperation in the Snowdrift game. Indirect reciprocity, punishment, and reputation can promote cooperation, especially when individuals can condition their behavior on others' reputations. The review also discusses the generalization to public goods games and the impact of group size, voluntary participation, and continuous investment. However, the paper does not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the frequency and stability of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how design dimensions such as group size, spatial structure, iteration, and the presence of punishment or reward mechanisms can affect cooperation in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that punishment and reputation mechanisms can promote cooperation, and that spatial structure can have divergent effects depending on the game. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome, it is not directly usable for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency. Its value is mainly in informing mechanistic expectations and highlighting possible moderators, not in providing quantitative effect estimates.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper argues that mechanisms such as punishment and reward, studied extensively in human public goods games, are also relevant in non-human animal cooperation. It reviews how laboratory studies (often using public goods or n-player social dilemma games) have inspired comparative research, and how features like punishment, partner choice, and communication can affect cooperation rates. The authors emphasize the need for more ecologically valid experiments and for understanding how laboratory findings translate to real-world helping and cooperation. The review notes that while punishment and reward can increase cooperation in public goods games, most evidence is about behavioral outcomes, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and comparative context for how punishment and reward mechanisms can promote cooperation in public-goods-like games, but does not provide direct empirical evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency effects. It is useful for understanding the mechanisms and moderators that may influence the effect of punishment on cooperation, but cannot be used to predict efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions or control efficiency. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of ecological validity, the role of communication, and the need to consider real-world payoff structures.

- source: Wu, B., García, J., Hauert, C., & Traulsen, A. (2013). Extrapolating Weak Selection in Evolutionary Games. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary games with finite populations, the ranking of strategies by abundance (e.g., cooperation, defection, punishment) can change as the intensity of selection increases. This is shown for public goods games with punishment as well as for general multiplayer and multi-strategy games. The main implication is that predictions based on weak selection (where payoffs have little effect on fitness) may not hold for moderate or strong selection. The paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, but rather focuses on which strategies are most common under different evolutionary conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. Instead, it shows that the abundance of strategies (including punishers) can change non-monotonically with selection intensity, and that results from weak selection may not extrapolate to stronger selection. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests caution in using weak selection results to predict efficiency effects of punishment, especially in games with more than two strategies or players. However, since efficiency or payoff outcomes are not analyzed, the paper's direct value for predicting efficiency is limited.

- source: Sigmund, K., & Hilbe, C. (2011). Darwin and the Evolution of Human Cooperation. *Principles of Evolution: from the Planck Epoch to Complex Multicellular Life*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The chapter synthesizes theoretical models showing that cooperation in public goods games is difficult to sustain without mechanisms for targeted punishment or reward. When such mechanisms are available, cooperation can be stabilized at high levels. The chapter also reviews the evolutionary logic behind kin selection, reciprocity, and group selection, and discusses the cognitive and informational demands of indirect reciprocity and moral systems. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions or empirical data on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment and reward mechanisms can stabilize cooperation in public goods games, suggesting that their presence is likely to increase cooperation rates. However, because the chapter does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it cannot directly inform quantitative predictions of efficiency changes due to punishment. Its value lies in contextualizing why and when punishment might be effective, rather than in providing parameter-specific guidance for efficiency prediction.

- source: Anderies, J. M., Janssen, M. A., Bousquet, F., Cardenas, J. C., Castillo, D., Lopez, M. C., Tobias, R., Vollan, B., & Wutich, A. (2011). The challenge of understanding decisions in experimental studies of common pool resource governance. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper reviews the literature on CPR and public goods experiments, highlighting that communication (e.g., face-to-face chat) increases cooperation and average earnings, while costly punishment is used by participants but can reduce net average returns unless chosen voluntarily by the group. The paper emphasizes the importance of micro-situational variables (such as group size, communication, and context) and the need for richer data collection to understand decision-making. It also discusses the crowding-out effect of external regulation and the mixed effects of ecological dynamics on cooperation and punishment. However, the paper does not provide new empirical estimates or direct analysis of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and methodological guidance for interpreting experimental results on punishment and cooperation in CPR and public goods settings. It suggests that communication and voluntary punishment can improve cooperation, but costly punishment may reduce net payoffs unless used sparingly or chosen by the group. The paper highlights the importance of context and micro-situational variables, which should be considered when predicting the effects of punishment on efficiency. However, because it does not report new empirical data or directly analyze efficiency outcomes, its value for quantitative prediction is limited to qualitative insights and literature synthesis.

- source: Raihani, N. J., & Power, E. A. (2021). No good deed goes unpunished: the social costs of prosocial behaviour. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that prosocial acts can incur social costs, especially when observers infer self-interested motives or feel disadvantaged by others' generosity. It reviews evidence that in public goods games and similar environments, cooperators can be punished (antisocial punishment) or evicted, and that overtly generous individuals may be derogated or disliked. The review suggests that these social costs can limit the emergence and scale of cooperation, and that reputation management strategies (such as modesty or anonymity) are often used to avoid such costs. The paper does not provide quantitative or direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, but highlights the importance of context, observer motives, and cultural variation in shaping responses to prosocial behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides important theoretical context for understanding when punishment or sanctions may have counterproductive effects in public goods games and related environments, particularly through antisocial punishment of cooperators. However, it does not provide quantitative or direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in a specific game design. It does suggest that the presence of antisocial punishment and reputational risks may reduce the positive impact of punishment on cooperation and efficiency, especially in contexts where observers feel threatened or infer strategic motives. For prediction tasks, this review supports the idea that punishment does not always increase efficiency and that context and social perception are important moderators.

- source: Vollan, B., Prediger, S., & Frölich, M. (2013). Co-managing common-pool resources: Do formal rules have to be adapted to traditional ecological norms?. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This field-lab experiment with communal farmers in southern Africa shows that the efficiency of formal, democratically chosen rules for managing a common-pool resource depends on their alignment with local ecological norms. The quota rule (limiting extraction) increases group earnings and resource availability in both high and low sustainability groups, but is unpopular. The rotation rule only increases efficiency in low sustainability groups (who previously failed to coordinate), but is ineffective or even counterproductive in high sustainability groups (who already tacitly coordinated for sustainability), due to high rates of rule breaking. The lottery rule is ineffective in both types of groups. Rule compliance is lower when rules conflict with local norms, and rule breaking is not mainly motivated by profit. Thus, the effect of rule-based interventions on efficiency is highly context-dependent.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of introducing a formal rule (akin to a punishment or restriction mechanism) on efficiency in a common-pool resource game, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect depends on the compatibility of the rule with pre-existing group norms. Rules that align with local sustainability norms (e.g., quotas in groups that value sustainability) can increase efficiency, while rules that conflict with these norms (e.g., rotation in high-norm groups) may reduce efficiency due to non-compliance. The presence of a sanctioning mechanism (imperfect monitoring and penalty) is not sufficient to ensure compliance or efficiency gains. Thus, the effect of punishment-like interventions on efficiency is moderated by group-level norms and prior behavior, not just by the formal game design. This paper is highly informative for settings where the main intervention is a formal rule or restriction, but less so for classic peer-punishment PGGs.

- source: Castillo, D., Bousquet, F., Janssen, M. A., Worrapimphong, K., & Cardenas, J. C. (2011). Context matters to explain field experiments: Results from Colombian and Thai fishing villages. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing externally imposed rules with probabilistic sanctions (confiscation of yield upon detection) in a CPR field experiment in fishing villages leads to only modest improvements in group efficiency and earnings. The effect is significant only when the quota rule is chosen and followed, but in most groups, rule breaking remains common and efficiency gains are small or absent. The context—such as economic necessity, lack of trust in authorities, and low legitimacy of rules—plays a major role in limiting the effectiveness of sanctions. The findings suggest that punishment mechanisms, as implemented here, do not reliably increase efficiency in real-world resource-dependent communities, except under specific conditions of rule choice and compliance.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in field settings with resource-dependent participants, the introduction of rules with probabilistic sanctions (punishment) does not reliably increase efficiency in public-goods-like games. Efficiency gains are observed only when the group chooses and complies with a quota rule; otherwise, rule breaking and contextual barriers (e.g., lack of trust, economic necessity) undermine the effect of punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment mechanisms alone is not sufficient to ensure higher efficiency—contextual factors and the type of rule matter. Control efficiency (without punishment) is low, and treatment efficiency (with punishment) is only modestly higher, and only in some cases. Game design dimensions such as the absence of communication, the probabilistic and relatively weak punishment, and the lack of participant buy-in to rules are important moderators.

- source: Perez, I., Yu, D. J., Janssen, M. A., & Anderies, J. M. (2015). Social roles and performance of social-ecological systems: evidence from behavioral lab experiments. *Ecology and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that group success in a lab irrigation game is best explained by the presence of certain combinations of emergent social roles, rather than any single role. All groups had leaders, knowledge generators, and followers, but successful groups also had connectors and either moralists or observers. The enforcer role (which involves punishing others' behavior, e.g., via threats to withhold investment) was not necessary for group success and was more common in unsuccessful groups. The presence of enforcers appears to be a symptom of conflict and lack of cooperation, not a cause of improved efficiency. The experiment does not include a formal punishment mechanism (e.g., costly punishment with explicit payoff consequences), so findings about 'enforcer' roles are based on communication and threats rather than actual sanctioning actions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling or disabling formal punishment mechanisms on efficiency in public goods or common-pool resource games. Instead, it provides evidence that informal enforcement roles (as expressed in chat) are not sufficient or necessary for group success, and that their presence may indicate underlying conflict. For prediction tasks about the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper suggests that the mere presence of enforcement or sanctioning behavior (in the absence of formal mechanisms) does not guarantee improved efficiency. The findings are most relevant for settings with communication and emergent informal roles, not for games with explicit, costly punishment options.

- source: Sasaki, T., & Uchida, S. (2014). Rewards and the evolution of cooperation in public good games. *Biology Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in threshold public good games, voluntary reward funds can evolve and lead to efficient cooperation, overcoming coordination problems that typically prevent the evolution of cooperation in large, anonymous groups. The model shows that, regardless of initial conditions, rewarders can invade a population of defectors if the reward fund's return is sufficiently high, leading to a cascade where cooperators eventually dominate. The resulting group payoff and average fitness are higher with rewards than without, and the system can reach full cooperation or stable mixed equilibria depending on the risk and threshold parameters. The paper contrasts this with institutional punishment, which is argued to be less effective or more difficult to initiate in large groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that introducing a reward mechanism in threshold public good games will increase group efficiency and average payoff, especially in settings where coordination is otherwise difficult. While it does not directly analyze punishment, it offers indirect evidence that reward mechanisms can be more effective than punishment in some coordination contexts. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling a reward mechanism (with sufficient effectiveness and sustainability) should increase efficiency relative to control. The model's explicit treatment of group size, threshold, risk, and reward parameters can inform how these dimensions moderate the effect of rewards on efficiency.

- source: Kurokawa, S. (2019). Three-player repeated games with an opt-out option. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a three-player repeated public goods game with an opt-out option (no explicit punishment), both tolerant and intolerant cooperators can survive, but their coexistence is not an evolutionary outcome. The equilibrium frequency of cooperators (and thus average payoff) increases with the benefit-to-cost ratio and decreases with the probability of group dissolution. The model shows that (partially) cooperative stable equilibria can exist, but the all-defector equilibrium is always asymptotically stable, making the initial evolution of cooperation difficult without additional mechanisms. The paper does not study punishment or sanctioning, so it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is relevant for understanding the effect of voluntary partner refusal (opt-out) on cooperation and efficiency in small-group public goods games, but it does not address punishment or sanctioning. It provides analytic results on how group size, benefit-to-cost ratio, and group dissolution probability affect equilibrium cooperation and payoffs. However, it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment, as no punishment mechanism is present. Its results may be useful as a baseline for comparison to punishment-enabled games, especially regarding the efficiency achievable through partner choice alone.

- source: Ohtsuki, H. (2004). Reactive strategies in indirect reciprocity. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in indirect reciprocity settings with public-goods-game-type payoffs, discriminating strategies (which conditionally cooperate based on others' past behavior, akin to punishment of defectors) are not stable evolutionary outcomes. Instead, populations tend to evolve toward unconditional cooperation (All C) or defection (All D), depending on whether the expected benefit of cooperation (modulated by information transfer and continuation probability) exceeds the cost.
