Discriminators can promote cooperation transiently by increasing the average 'discriminatoriness' in the population, but are ultimately outcompeted by more generous strategies. The paper also shows that errors (in perception or implementation) further destabilize discriminating strategies, making unconditional cooperation or defection more likely. The main outcomes are expressed in terms of average payoff (efficiency), and the model provides explicit threshold conditions for the evolution of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in public-goods-game-like environments with indirect reciprocity, strategies that punish defectors (by refusing to cooperate with them) do not lead to stable increases in group efficiency or payoff. Instead, such strategies are only transient promoters of cooperation, and populations tend to evolve toward unconditional cooperation or defection depending on the benefit-to-cost ratio and information transfer. The model does not include explicit costly punishment as in standard PGG experiments, so its direct predictive value for the effect of enabling punishment in a PGG is limited. However, it suggests that punishment-like strategies may not robustly increase efficiency unless additional mechanisms (e.g., standing, justified punishment) are present. For prediction tasks, this paper implies that simply enabling punishment (in the form of conditional cooperation) may not reliably increase efficiency, especially in the presence of errors.

- source: Castro, L., & Toro, M. A. (2008). Iterated prisoner's dilemma in an asocial world dominated by loners, not by defectors. *Theoretical Population Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated N-person Prisoner's Dilemma games, the presence of a 'loner' strategy (voluntary opt-out for a fixed payoff) changes the evolutionary dynamics: loners can displace defectors, and conditional cooperators (TFT0) can invade a population of loners if the benefit of cooperation is high enough and the number of repeated interactions is sufficient. The model demonstrates that cooperation can be established and maintained in sizable groups without the need for explicit punishment, provided that individuals can opt out. The paper does not model or test punishment directly, but discusses its potential role as an additional mechanism for sustaining cooperation in large groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that voluntary participation (the loner strategy) can enable high-efficiency outcomes in repeated public-goods-like games, even without punishment. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency, as punishment is not modeled. The findings suggest that in environments where voluntary participation is possible, the need for punishment to sustain cooperation may be reduced. For prediction, this paper is most informative about the baseline efficiency in control games with voluntary participation, but not about the incremental effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Kurokawa, S. (2023). Evolution of spite in an n-player game with an opting-out option. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated n-player games with an opting-out option, spiteful (costly harming) strategies can sometimes persist, but only under restrictive conditions: low cost-to-harm ratio, small group size, and specific opting-out rules. As group size increases, it becomes harder for spiteful strategies to achieve higher payoffs than nonspiteful strategies, and the basin of attraction for all-nonspiteful populations grows. The opting-out rule (how easily groups disband) is a key moderator: when homogeneous groups (all spiteful or all nonspiteful) are forced to disband, spite can persist more easily, but this is a somewhat artificial rule. The model is mathematically explicit about when spiteful strategies can dominate, and these conditions almost always correspond to lower group efficiency compared to all-nonspiteful populations. The analysis is directly about payoff and efficiency, not just behavior.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public-goods-like n-player games with an opting-out mechanism, the introduction of costly punishment (here, spiteful harming) generally reduces group efficiency unless the cost-to-harm ratio is extremely low and the group size is small. As group size increases, the conditions for punishment/spite to persist become more restrictive, and group efficiency is more likely to be maximized when punishment/spite is absent. The details of the opting-out rule (e.g., whether homogeneous groups are forced to disband) can moderate this effect, but in most realistic settings, punishment/spite is not efficiency-enhancing. The model is not an exact PGG with standard punishment, but the payoff structure and evolutionary logic are closely related, and the results are directly about payoff/efficiency. Thus, the paper supports the prediction that enabling punishment/spite in large groups with standard cost structures will not increase efficiency, and may reduce it.

- source: Fontanari, J. F., & Santos, M. (2024). Solving the prisoner's dilemma trap in Hamilton's model of temporarily formed random groups. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in the 'others-only' n-person prisoner's dilemma (a strong altruism scenario), cooperation cannot evolve under asexual reproduction or sexual reproduction with selfing, but can evolve and become stable under biparental sexual reproduction if the group benefit multiplier (r) is sufficiently large relative to group size (n) and the cost of cooperation (c) is not too high. The model does not include punishment or reward, and the main mechanism enabling cooperation is the genetic structure imposed by biparental reproduction. The main outcome is the probability that the population reaches the all-cooperator state (fixation probability), which is a direct measure of group efficiency or welfare. The results are summarized in phase diagrams showing regions where cooperation is stable, bistable, or unstable, as a function of c, r, and n. The findings are robust to demographic noise and extend to dioecious populations. The paper does not provide evidence about the effects of punishment, communication, or other institutional design features.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments that lack punishment or reward mechanisms, especially when the game structure is the 'others-only' variant of the n-person prisoner's dilemma. It shows that, in the absence of punishment, efficiency (measured as fixation of cooperation and group payoff) is only achievable under specific genetic and demographic conditions—namely, biparental sexual reproduction, sufficiently high group benefit multiplier (r), and not-too-large group size (n) or cost (c). The results suggest that, without punishment, efficiency is generally low unless these conditions are met. However, the paper does not provide any evidence about the effect of enabling punishment or sanctions, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency difference between control and punishment-enabled conditions. Its main value is in establishing a baseline: in the absence of punishment, efficiency is generally low except under the specified genetic/demographic conditions. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not manipulate or analyze punishment.

- source: Schonmann, R. H., & Boyd, R. (2016). A simple rule for the evolution of contingent cooperation in large groups. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that contingent cooperation in large groups can invade and persist if relatedness is above a threshold that depends sensitively on the threshold fraction of cooperators required for cooperation to be sustained. The required relatedness increases rapidly as the threshold for cooperation increases. The number of repeated interactions (iterations) required for cooperation to invade also increases rapidly with the threshold and decreases with relatedness. The model shows that, under realistic levels of relatedness, only strategies that require cooperation from a small fraction of the group can invade; strategies that require near-universal cooperation are unlikely to evolve. The paper argues that contingent punishment (not formally modeled here) is a more plausible mechanism for the evolution of widespread cooperation, because even a small fraction of punishers can induce high levels of cooperation. The results are supported by mathematical derivations and numerical simulations of evolutionary dynamics in group-structured populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how group size, relatedness, and the threshold for contingent cooperation affect the evolutionary success (and thus efficiency) of cooperation in public-goods-like environments. It does not directly model or analyze punishment, but its results suggest that punishment-based mechanisms are more likely to support high efficiency in large groups than pure contingent cooperation. For prediction tasks, the paper's formulas can be used to estimate the conditions under which cooperation (and thus high efficiency) can be sustained, given the group structure and behavioral rules. However, because the paper does not model explicit punishment or compare efficiency with and without punishment, it provides only indirect support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Foster, K. R. (2004). Diminishing returns in social evolution: the not-so-tragic commons. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper develops and analyzes mathematical models of social evolution in group-structured populations, focusing on how the functional form of returns to investment (linear vs. diminishing) affects group performance (efficiency) in the face of selfish competition. It finds that, under diminishing returns, the negative impact of competition on group efficiency is greatly reduced, and group performance can be close to the optimum even with low relatedness and no explicit enforcement. The paper provides examples from social vertebrates, insects, and microbes, arguing that diminishing returns are common in real biological systems. Explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are not modeled, but enforcement is discussed as one of several mechanisms that can limit the tragedy of the commons.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the efficiency impact of selfish competition in public-goods-like environments is highly sensitive to the shape of the returns to investment. In prediction tasks, if the underlying game or biological system features diminishing returns to cooperation or competition, the efficiency loss from selfish behavior (and by extension, the potential efficiency gain from adding punishment) may be much smaller than predicted by linear models. However, because explicit punishment is not modeled, the paper does not provide direct evidence about the marginal effect of enabling punishment in a PGG. It does, however, suggest that in environments with diminishing returns, the baseline efficiency may already be high, potentially reducing the incremental value of punishment.

- source: Richter, A., & Grasman, J. (2013). The transmission of sustainable harvesting norms when agents are conditionally cooperative. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the presence of conditional cooperators in a common-pool resource setting can lead to complex and sometimes abrupt transitions between cooperative and non-cooperative equilibria. Efficiency, measured as resource stock and group profit relative to the maximum economic yield, is highly sensitive to social dynamics. When temptation to defect increases or when group size increases, the system can shift from high-efficiency (cooperative) to low-efficiency (defective) states, sometimes with hysteresis or limit cycles. The model highlights that social norm enforcement via moral persuasion can sustain cooperation, but is vulnerable to collapse if conditional cooperators lose motivation. The absence of explicit punishment means that norm enforcement is 'soft' and collapses are possible even without changes in external parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in public-goods-like common-pool resource games, the efficiency of group outcomes is highly sensitive to the social composition (intrinsic vs. conditional cooperators vs. defectors) and to the strength of norm enforcement mechanisms. While it does not model explicit punishment, it suggests that in the absence of strong enforcement (e.g., costly punishment), efficiency is fragile and can collapse due to endogenous social dynamics. For prediction tasks, this paper implies that enabling or strengthening norm enforcement (potentially via punishment) could stabilize high-efficiency outcomes, especially in larger groups or when temptation to defect is high. However, the model also warns that even with some enforcement, efficiency can be lost if social dynamics shift. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is absent or weak, and thus provide a baseline for understanding the potential value of adding punishment.

- source: Iwasa, Y., Uchida, T., & Yokomizo, H. (2007). Nonlinear behavior of the socio-economic dynamics for lake eutrophication control. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the interplay between social pressure (which increases with both the number of cooperators and the level of pollution) and individual cost-benefit decisions can lead to complex group-level outcomes. Depending on parameter values, the system can settle into a high-cooperation/clean-water equilibrium, a low-cooperation/polluted equilibrium, or exhibit oscillatory or chaotic dynamics. The model shows that increasing the effectiveness of pollution-reducing technology can paradoxically worsen water quality if it reduces the perceived need for cooperation. Conservatism (slower behavioral change) can stabilize cooperation and maintain higher group welfare. The model does not include explicit punishment, but social pressure is discussed as a possible proxy for reputational or sanctioning effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how social feedbacks and behavioral dynamics can affect group efficiency in public-goods-like settings. While it does not directly model punishment, it suggests that mechanisms increasing social pressure (which could include punishment, reputation, or social norms) can promote cooperation and improve group outcomes, but may also lead to multiple stable states or complex dynamics. The findings support the idea that the structure and strength of social incentives (including, potentially, punishment) are critical moderators of efficiency. However, the absence of explicit punishment or experimental manipulation limits the direct applicability of the results to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in empirical PGGs.

- source: Brown, S. P., & Taylor, P. D. (2010). Joint evolution of multiple social traits: a kin selection analysis. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that when social traits such as public good production and exploitation co-evolve, their interactions can lead to complex evolutionary dynamics, including bistability between efficient (social) and inefficient (asocial) outcomes. The efficiency of the group (measured as total payoff or welfare) depends on the joint equilibrium of these traits, which is sensitive to relatedness, the presence of independent public good production, and the nature of trait interactions (synergy or interference). In some cases, higher relatedness increases both cooperation and exploitation, with ambiguous effects on efficiency. The model highlights that multidimensional social dilemmas can have multiple stable outcomes, and small changes in parameters or initial conditions can lead to large differences in group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how the design dimensions of a public goods game—especially those affecting trait interactions, relatedness, and the possibility of multiple social traits—can lead to non-linear and sometimes counterintuitive effects on group efficiency when punishment-like or enforcement traits are present. While the model does not directly analyze punishment in the standard PGG sense, it shows that enforcement or exploitation traits can interact with cooperation to produce bistability or runaway dynamics. For prediction, this suggests that enabling punishment or enforcement mechanisms may not always increase efficiency; the effect depends on the interaction structure, initial conditions, and whether the system is in a region of parameter space that supports social or asocial equilibria. The findings caution against simple extrapolation from one-dimensional models and highlight the importance of considering multidimensional trait interactions.

- source: Hauert, C. (2006). Spatial effects in social dilemmas. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that spatial structure can both promote and inhibit cooperation in social dilemmas, depending on the type of game (prisoner's dilemma, snowdrift, by-product mutualism, coordination) and the microscopic update rule. In strict prisoner's dilemma or public goods settings, spatial structure allows cooperators to survive by forming clusters, but the parameter range for this effect is limited. In snowdrift-type games, spatial structure can actually reduce cooperation. The effect of spatial structure is highly sensitive to the update rule: the death-birth process is most favorable to cooperation, while the replicator rule can lead to reduced cooperation in some parameter regions. The main outcome is the equilibrium fraction of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it is informative about how spatial structure, group size, cost-benefit ratios, and update rules affect the equilibrium level of cooperation in group social dilemmas. If the prediction task involves spatial structure or local interactions, the findings suggest that such structure can have complex, sometimes counterintuitive effects on cooperation, which may indirectly affect efficiency. The absence of punishment as a mechanism means the paper cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. The main value is in understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in spatially structured games.

- source: Billard, E. A. (1996). Evolutionary strategies of stochastic learning automata in the prisoner's dilemma. *Biosystems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper argues that cooperation in resource dilemmas (including public goods and commons dilemmas) is shaped by individual value orientation (pro-social vs egoistic), situational constraints (such as group identification, communication, and social norms), and the degree and type of uncertainty (social and environmental). It proposes that pro-social individuals are more likely to cooperate regardless of uncertainty, while egoistic individuals are more likely to defect, especially under uncertainty. Situational factors (including the possibility of sanctions or rewards) can constrain egoism and promote cooperation. The paper reviews prior findings that rewards increase cooperation and sanctions decrease defection, but notes that the costs of such interventions may outweigh collective gains and that effects may not persist when interventions are removed. The analysis is theoretical and hypothesis-generating, not empirical.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual background on the psychological mechanisms that may moderate the effect of punishment, reward, and uncertainty on cooperation in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that punishment and reward can increase cooperation, but may not always improve efficiency due to their costs, and that their effects may not persist. The paper does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions. Its main value for prediction is in highlighting the importance of situational constraints (including punishment/reward), group identification, communication, and uncertainty, and in warning that interventions may have costs that offset their benefits. However, it does not provide direct evidence for the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games.

- source: Salomonsson, M. (2010). Group selection: The quest for social preferences. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that group selection can, under certain conditions, support the evolution of cooperation and altruism in public-goods-like and Prisoner's Dilemma games. Three main approaches are discussed: fixation (requiring isolated pure groups), assortative group formation (e.g., via conformity or signalling), and reproductive externalities (where group-level payoffs affect evolutionary success). The paper notes that group selection is more likely to support cooperation when migration is low, groups are small, or positive externalities can be internalized. It also reviews how cultural mechanisms (like conformity) and group competition can foster social preferences. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence or predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, and only discusses punishment as a possible mechanism in the context of conformity and social preferences.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how group structure, migration, and mechanisms like conformity or signalling can affect the evolution of cooperation in public-goods-like games. It is useful for understanding the broader conditions under which cooperation and social preferences may arise, but it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that group structure and the ability to internalize externalities are important moderators, but it does not inform the specific effect of punishment interventions in experimental public goods games.

- source: Aktipis, A. (2016). Principles of cooperation across systems: from human sharing to multicellularity and cancer. *Evolutionary Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper identifies three general principles that facilitate cooperation: (1) the ability to 'Walk Away' from uncooperative partners, (2) need-based resource transfers, and (3) the necessity of increasingly sophisticated cheater suppression mechanisms as cooperation scales up. It reviews agent-based models (from prior work) showing that conditional movement (Walk Away) can stabilize cooperation in both dyadic and group contexts, including public goods game-like environments. The paper discusses how positive assortment (cooperators interacting more with each other) is key to the evolution of cooperation. It also highlights the challenges of cheater suppression in large-scale systems and draws analogies between human societies, multicellular organisms, and social insects. However, it does not provide new empirical or experimental results on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it directly analyze the effects of punishment mechanisms in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical background on mechanisms that support cooperation, such as partner choice (Walk Away) and cheater suppression, which are relevant to understanding the effects of punishment in public goods games. However, it does not provide direct empirical or quantitative evidence on how punishment affects efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their variants. The discussion of cheater suppression includes punishment as one possible mechanism but is not specific or detailed enough to inform quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The most relevant insight is that as cooperative systems scale up, more sophisticated and costly cheater suppression mechanisms (including punishment) are required, but the paper does not specify the efficiency consequences of these mechanisms in controlled game environments.

- source: Lee, J. H., Yamaguchi, R., Yokomizo, H., & Nakamaru, M. (2020). Preservation of the value of rice paddy fields: Investigating how to prevent farmers from abandoning the fields by means of evolutionary game theory. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation (maintenance effort) and thus group efficiency can be sustained when responsibility for collective action (maintenance) is local (among neighbors), but not when it is global (all players responsible for all facilities). Maintenance effort promotes cultivation effort, but not vice versa. Spatial structure and local interaction are key to sustaining group welfare. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are directly relevant to predicting efficiency in nonlinear public goods games with spatial structure and local cooperation, but not to games with punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that local responsibility and spatial structure can sustain cooperation and group efficiency in nonlinear public goods games, but it does not inform the effect of punishment or sanctions. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly informative. However, it is highly informative for understanding the baseline efficiency of control games (without punishment) as a function of spatial structure, local vs. global responsibility, and ecological parameters. It can help predict when efficiency will be high or low in the absence of punishment, but not the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Joshi, J., Brännström, & Dieckmann, U. (2020). Emergence of social inequality in the spatial harvesting of renewable public goods. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model shows that when harvesting efficiency is low, all agents evolve to be sedentary and harvest at low rates, resulting in equitable but inefficient resource use. As harvesting efficiency increases, agents become mobile, and resource extraction approaches the maximum sustainable yield, achieving both efficiency and equity. Further increases in harvesting efficiency or reductions in dispersal cost lead to overexploitation and the emergence of social inequality, with a minority of mobile, overexploitative agents and a majority of frugal, sedentary agents. Rapid social learning (impatience) and local information (myopia) further aggravate inequality and inefficiency. The model identifies key moderators of efficiency and equity but does not include or analyze punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding how efficiency in spatial public goods–like environments depends on ecological and behavioral parameters such as harvesting efficiency, dispersal cost, consumer density, and information structure. However, it does not provide any evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctioning, as these mechanisms are absent from the model. Therefore, while the findings are useful for predicting efficiency in control (no-punishment) conditions and for understanding the role of mobility and social learning, the paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to the introduction of punishment. It is best used for baseline efficiency estimation and for understanding moderators of overexploitation and inequality in the absence of sanctions.

- source: Bach, L. A., Helvik, T., & Christiansen, F. B. (2006). The evolution of n-player cooperation - threshold games and ESS bifurcations. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in n-player threshold public goods games, the existence and stability of cooperative equilibria depend critically on the cost-benefit ratio and the threshold required for the collective benefit. For certain parameter regions, both cooperative and defecting equilibria can coexist, and small changes in parameters can lead to sudden transitions (catastrophes) in group efficiency and cooperation. The model predicts hysteresis: once a population falls into full defection, simply restoring favorable parameters may not be sufficient to recover cooperation. The analysis provides explicit mathematical conditions for these transitions and demonstrates that such complex dynamics are only possible in games with at least three players. No punishment or reward mechanisms are included.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how group size, threshold structure, and cost-benefit parameters affect the efficiency and stability of cooperation in public-goods-like games without punishment. It is directly relevant for predicting baseline (control) efficiency in threshold public goods games, especially when the benefit is all-or-nothing and depends on a minimum number of cooperators. However, it does not inform the effect of punishment or sanctions, as these mechanisms are not modeled. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is useful for understanding how efficiency varies with game design in the absence of punishment, but provides no evidence on how enabling punishment would change efficiency.

- source: Wang, X. M., Couto, M. C., Wang, N. Y., An, X. M., Chen, B., Dong, Y. L., Hilbe, C., & Zhang, B. Y. (2023). Cooperation and coordination in heterogeneous populations. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that endowment inequality is a significant obstacle to successful coordination in threshold public goods games, reducing the proportion of groups that reach the threshold and thus receive the group reward. Productivity inequality does not have a similar negative effect. Participants tend to coordinate on equal relative contributions, even when this is not payoff-maximizing. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, so it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or cooperation. The findings highlight the importance of focal points and fairness heuristics in equilibrium selection, and show that evolutionary game theory models may mis-predict outcomes unless they account for participants' initial expectations and heuristics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the experimental design. However, it provides informative context on how inequality (in endowment or productivity) affects coordination and group success in threshold public goods games. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it suggests that baseline efficiency (without punishment) is lower under endowment inequality. This could be relevant for understanding the baseline to which punishment effects would be compared in similar asymmetric games.

- source: Gallier, C., Langbein, J., & Vance, C. (2018). Non-binding Restrictions, Cooperation, and Coral Reef Protection: Experimental Evidence from Indonesian Fishing Communities. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that non-binding recommendations, especially those from an external source (e.g., NGO or scientist), can reduce extraction effort in a CPR game, but this effect is highly context-dependent and only significant in the most diverse community (Bunaken). Democratic recommendations have a weaker effect, and leader-based recommendations have no effect. There is no punishment or sanctioning in any treatment. The main outcome is extraction effort, not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that, in the absence of enforcement, non-binding recommendations alone are often insufficient to promote cooperation, except in certain socio-cultural contexts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or CPR games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any treatment. The findings are relevant for understanding the limits of non-binding recommendations in the absence of enforcement, but do not inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. The game design dimensions are well described, but the absence of punishment means this study is not directly informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Chung, D., Yun, K., & Jeong, J. (2015). Decoding covert motivations of free riding and cooperation from multi-feature pattern analysis of EEG signals. *Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that neural oscillations measured by EEG immediately after result presentation can predict whether a participant will cooperate or free ride in the next round, across three different payoff structures that manipulate motivations (fear and greed). Behaviorally, free riding rates are highest in the standard condition, lower in the 'no fear' condition, and lowest in the 'no greed' condition. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. It may provide some indirect context about how payoff structure (fear/greed) affects cooperation rates, but is not informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Ezoe, H., & Ikegawa, Y. (2013). Coexistence of mutualists and non-mutualists in a dual-lattice model. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that spatial structure allows for the persistence and coexistence of mutualists and non-mutualists in a dual-lattice model, even when mutualists pay a cost to benefit others. The parameter range for mutualist persistence expands as the magnitude of interspecific interaction increases. Asymmetry in basic reproduction rates between species can facilitate coexistence. The model does not include punishment or sanction mechanisms, and outcomes are not reported in terms of efficiency or group payoff, but rather in terms of population densities and coexistence patterns.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include punishment or sanction mechanisms. It is relevant for understanding the effects of spatial structure and interspecific interaction on the persistence of cooperation, but does not inform predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment. The model and findings are adjacent to the prediction task, but not directly informative for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: dos Santos, M., Braithwaite, V. A., & Wedekind, C. (2014). Exposure to superfluous information reduces cooperation and increases antisocial punishment in reputation-based interactions. *Frontiers in Ecology and Evolution*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In reputation-based helping games (without punishment), cognitive disturbance (superfluous words) reduces cooperation and lowers group payoffs, indicating that efficiency is sensitive to cognitive constraints. In reputation-based punishment games, disturbance increases antisocial punishment and reduces punishment of defectors, but does not significantly change overall cooperation or mean final payoffs. The link between reputation and payoff is weakened under disturbance. The study highlights that experimental outcomes in reputation and punishment games are highly sensitive to environmental and cognitive factors, and that the expected efficiency gains from punishment or reputation mechanisms may not materialize under cognitive load.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the efficiency effects of punishment and reputation mechanisms in indirect reciprocity games are sensitive to cognitive context. For prediction tasks, it suggests that efficiency gains from punishment (or reputation) may be undermined or nullified if cognitive load is high, even when game design parameters are otherwise favorable. The results also show that the presence of punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency: the effect depends on how punishment is used (e.g., antisocial vs. prosocial punishment) and on whether players can process reputational information. When using control efficiency to predict treatment efficiency, one should consider that cognitive or informational disturbances can reduce or eliminate the positive effects of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Hilbe, C., & Sigmund, K. (2010). Incentives and opportunism: from the carrot to the stick. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The model demonstrates that negative incentives (punishment) can stabilize cooperation and lead to higher average payoffs (efficiency) in the population, provided that players have enough information about others' reputations. Rewarding (positive incentives) is not stable in the long run but is important for catalyzing the initial emergence of cooperation, especially when information is limited. When both punishment and reward are available, the population transitions from defection to cooperation more quickly, and the stable outcome is a cooperative, high-payoff equilibrium enforced by punishment. The results are robust to population size and mutation structure, and the authors discuss how the findings generalize to n-person public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in a public-goods-like environment will increase efficiency (average group payoff), especially when players can observe or infer others' incentive-providing behavior. The model suggests that the effect of punishment is more pronounced when information about others is available, and that the presence of reward mechanisms can accelerate the transition to cooperation but does not affect the long-run efficiency as much as punishment does. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player, two-stage games but are argued to generalize to n-person PGGs. For prediction tasks, the paper supports a positive effect of punishment on efficiency, especially when information/reputation is high, and suggests that the marginal benefit of adding reward is mainly in catalyzing cooperation rather than sustaining higher efficiency in the long run.

- source: Yaman, A., Leibo, J. Z., Iacca, G., & Wan Lee, S. (2023). The emergence of division of labour through decentralized social sanctioning. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that decentralized social sanctioning, implemented as a matrix of role-based rules for reward and punishment, enables groups of self-interested agents to learn and maintain efficient divisions of labour. This leads to substantially higher average group payoff compared to groups without sanctioning, or with only altruistic or selfish learning. The effect is robust across different environmental conditions and is close to the theoretical upper bound. Complexity regularization can promote the evolution of simpler, convergent social norms without sacrificing group payoff. The model shows that sanctioning transforms the incentive structure, making it individually rational to take on critical but otherwise unrewarding roles, thus solving the social dilemma.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and computational evidence that enabling decentralized social sanctioning (punishment and/or reward) in public-goods-like environments can substantially increase group efficiency, especially when critical roles are otherwise unrewarding. The effect is robust across a range of environmental parameters and is close to the optimal achievable by global optimization. The model is not a direct PGG but is structurally adjacent, with payoff interdependence and social dilemmas. The findings support the prediction that, all else equal, enabling punishment (and/or reward) will increase efficiency relative to a control with sanctioning disabled, especially in settings where division of labour is required. The model also highlights the importance of norm complexity and the potential for simple sanctioning rules to be highly effective.

- source: Jordan, J. J., & Rand, D. G. (2017). Third-party punishment as a costly signal of high continuation probabilities in repeated games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This paper develops a formal model in which third-party punishment (TPP) acts as a costly signal of trustworthiness in repeated games. The model shows that when individuals differ in their probability of future interaction (continuation probability), only those with high continuation probability (trustworthy types) find it worthwhile to engage in TPP, as they are more likely to receive future reciprocal benefits. The signaling value of TPP can make punishment equilibrium behavior in parameter regions where, absent signaling, punishment would not occur. The model provides explicit payoff formulas and equilibrium conditions, and demonstrates via evolutionary simulations that the punishment signaling equilibrium is dynamically stable. The presence of TPP as a signal increases the set of parameter values for which cooperation and higher group payoffs are sustained, thus improving efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the availability of punishment as a costly signal can increase group efficiency in repeated games, especially when individuals differ in continuation probability and when signaling is possible. The model gives explicit conditions (in terms of cost/benefit ratios, continuation probabilities, and punishment costs) under which punishment increases efficiency. While the model is not a direct PGG or lab experiment, its structure is closely related to repeated social dilemma games and provides mechanistic insight into when and why punishment will improve efficiency. The findings suggest that, in environments where signaling is possible and individuals can benefit from future interactions, enabling punishment can lead to higher efficiency than disabling it, provided the cost of punishment is not too high.

- source: dos Santos, M., Rankin, D. J., & Wedekind, C. (2011). The evolution of punishment through reputation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that punishment, when reputationally visible, can evolve and be stable because it increases the likelihood of receiving help in the future, offsetting its immediate cost. Analytical and simulation results show that punishing discriminators (who punish defectors and are recognized for it) achieve higher payoffs and can dominate, leading to high cooperation and group efficiency. The effect is robust to a range of costs and benefits, but is sensitive to the accuracy of reputation information and the mutation rate. The findings suggest that reputation is a key moderator of punishment's efficiency-enhancing effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the efficiency gains from punishment depend critically on whether punishment is reputationally visible and can influence future interactions. In environments where reputation for punishing is tracked and used by others to condition their cooperation, punishment can increase group efficiency and stabilize cooperation. The results are robust across a range of costs and benefits, but efficiency gains are reduced if reputation information is noisy or mutation rates are low. For prediction, the presence of a reputation system that makes punishment visible is a key moderator for positive efficiency effects of punishment.

- source: Murase, Y. (2025). Costly punishment sustains indirect reciprocity under low defection detectability. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the efficiency of costly punishment in sustaining cooperation depends critically on the detectability of defection. When defection is difficult to detect (i.e., high probability that defection is misperceived as cooperation), social norms that include costly punishment (CP norms) can be the most efficient ESS, achieving high cooperation and high average payoff. When defection is easy to detect, punishment is rarely efficient and often reduces payoffs. The model provides explicit boundaries for when punishment is efficient, and shows that punishment is most effective when it is rarely executed but strongly deters defection. Execution errors and high punishment costs shrink the parameter space where punishment is efficient. The results challenge previous theoretical work that found only a narrow efficiency margin for punishment, showing that under asymmetric detection errors, punishment can be broadly efficient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, parameterized theoretical predictions for the effect of punishment on efficiency in indirect reciprocity games with pairwise donation structure. While not a standard public goods game, the model is adjacent and the efficiency results are highly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment in environments where defection is hard to detect. The explicit formulas and phase diagrams allow mapping from game design dimensions (punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, error rates) and baseline efficiency to the expected efficiency with punishment enabled. The main moderator is the detectability of defection: when it is low, punishment can substantially increase efficiency; when high, punishment is unnecessary or even harmful. The results are most transferable to settings with pairwise interactions, reputation, and indirect reciprocity, but provide strong mechanistic insight for broader PGG-like environments.

- source: Okamoto, K., & Matsumura, S. (2000). The evolution of punishment and apology: an iterated prisoner's dilemma model. *Evolutionary Ecology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that strategies incorporating both punishment and apology signals (especially the Pavlov-like pPAS strategy) can maintain high levels of cooperation and average payoffs in the iterated prisoner's dilemma, even in the presence of errors and deceptive strategies. The average payoff in a pPAS-dominated population is nearly as high as in a fully cooperative population, and much higher than in populations dominated by tit-for-tat (TFT) or defectors. The success of pPAS depends on the costs of punishment and apology: pPAS dominates when the cost of giving punishment is low, the cost of receiving punishment is high, or the cost of giving apology is high. The model also shows that pPAS can remain stable even when there is a dominance asymmetry in costs between players. The findings suggest that punishment and apology mechanisms can evolve to support efficient cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated social dilemmas with error, the availability of punishment (and apology) mechanisms can substantially increase group efficiency, provided the costs of punishment and apology are within certain bounds. The results are most directly applicable to iterated prisoner's dilemma games, which are adjacent but not identical to public goods games. The findings suggest that, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, one should expect a positive effect if the punishment cost is not too high, the punishment is effective, and the apology cost is not too low (to prevent deception). The paper also highlights the importance of considering the cost structure of punishment and apology when making such predictions. However, the results are based on theoretical models and evolutionary simulations, not empirical experiments.

- source: Leimar, O. (1997). Repeated games: A state space approach. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated games like the IPD and dominance games, the ability to punish can support the evolutionary stability of cooperative strategies, leading to higher efficiency (i.e., higher expected payoffs, closer to full cooperation) when the probability of continuation is high and punishment is sufficiently severe and/or effective. However, the effect of punishment on efficiency is parameter-dependent: if the probability of continuation is too low or punishment is too costly/ineffective, cooperation cannot be sustained and efficiency is low. The analysis also shows that there are many possible stable strategies, some of which are more efficient than others, and that the structure of punishment (e.g., how many rounds of mutual defection follow a defection) affects the range of stability and efficiency. In dominance games, punishment by a dominant can enforce resource control and higher efficiency for the dominant, but alternative equilibria exist where the subdominant prevails. In alternating PD, reciprocal strategies can be stable and efficient if the benefit-to-cost ratio is high enough. The findings are theoretical and based on formal analysis of payoffs and stability conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated games depends on game design dimensions such as the probability of continuation (analogous to number of rounds), the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the structure of the game (simultaneous vs. alternating moves, role asymmetry). It shows that punishment can increase efficiency by supporting cooperation, but only when parameters are favorable (e.g., high continuation probability, effective punishment). The results suggest that, for prediction tasks, one should expect punishment to improve efficiency in repeated games when the game is long enough and punishment is not too costly or weak, but that the effect can be neutral or negative if these conditions are not met. The paper also highlights that many different punishment strategies can be stable, so the specific implementation of punishment matters. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency in repeated, two-player, payoff-based games with punishment.

- source: Voelkl, B. (2015). The evolution of generalized reciprocity in social interaction networks. *Theoretical Population Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: Theoretical analysis demonstrates that introducing an effective penalty for unfaithfulness (selling outside the cooperative) leads to stable, efficient cooperative outcomes where all members sell to the cooperative. If the penalty is limited (not strong enough), full cooperation is unstable. When the penalty is strong (exceeds the extra profit from defection), the system converges to full cooperation, maximizing group payoff. Redistribution of penalties among faithful members further strengthens this effect, leading to a unique, stable Nash equilibrium with high efficiency. The model provides explicit conditions under which punishment stabilizes cooperation and maximizes group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in public-goods-like settings with continuous contribution and the possibility of punishment, increasing the effectiveness and/or strength of punishment increases group efficiency and stabilizes cooperation. The results are directly relevant for predicting that enabling or strengthening punishment will increase efficiency, especially when the punishment is strong enough to outweigh the gains from defection. The model also highlights that redistributing penalties among cooperators can further enhance efficiency and stability. However, the results are derived from a stylized, theoretical model rather than empirical data, and the setting is a marketing cooperative with continuous contribution rather than a canonical PGG, though the structure is closely related.

- source: Nakamaru, M., Shimura, H., Kitakaji, Y., & Ohnuma, S. (2018). The effect of sanctions on the evolution of cooperation in linear division of labor. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing sanctions—especially the producer responsibility system—promotes the evolution of cooperation and increases group-level efficiency in a linear division of labor game. The actor responsibility system only promotes cooperation if monitoring and detection are sufficiently effective, which is often not the case in real-world settings. The producer responsibility system, which sanctions the generator if the manifest is not returned, is robust to low monitoring probabilities and is more effective at promoting cooperation and reducing illegal dumping. The model provides explicit conditions under which cooperation is stable and shows that the three-role model captures more realistic dynamics than simpler two-role models. The findings are supported by both analytical results and numerical simulations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that introducing a well-designed punishment system (especially one that does not rely on costly monitoring, like the producer responsibility system) will increase group efficiency in public-goods-like environments with linear division of labor. The model is adjacent to standard PGGs but is directly relevant for settings with sequential roles and externalities. The results suggest that, for games with similar structure, enabling punishment (especially with high effectiveness and low monitoring requirements) should be predicted to increase efficiency relative to control. The paper also highlights the importance of the effectiveness of monitoring and the structure of the sanction system as moderators. However, the model is theoretical and does not provide direct empirical estimates of effect sizes.

- source: Jaffe, K. (2004). Altruism, altruistic punishment and social investment. *Acta Biotheoretica*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper uses agent-based simulations to examine the effects of altruistic punishment on aggregate wealth (efficiency) in artificial societies. It finds that while punishment increases generosity and norm compliance, it reduces aggregate wealth compared to societies without punishment, unless the punished behaviors trigger synergistic benefits for society. The effect is observed in both structured (with division of labor) and unstructured societies, though structured societies are more susceptible to social control via punishment. The study concludes that punishment is a strong mechanism for norm enforcement but is not, by itself, efficiency-enhancing.
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper provides evidence that enabling punishment in a public-goods-like environment (here, an agent-based resource-sharing simulation) increases norm compliance and generosity but reduces group efficiency (aggregate wealth), unless there are additional synergistic benefits from the enforced behaviors. The negative effect on efficiency is robust to variations in social structure and punishment intensity. Thus, when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper suggests that, in the absence of explicit synergistic returns, punishment is likely to reduce efficiency relative to the control. The findings are most directly relevant to settings where punishment is costly and does not directly generate group-level surplus.

- source: Ubeda, F., & Duéñez-Guzmán, E. A. (2011). POWER AND CORRUPTION. *Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a Prisoner's Dilemma with costly punishment, allowing for corrupt punishers and power asymmetries enables the evolution of stable states with both cooperation and corruption. When the social cost of punishing a punisher (q+d) is less than the cost of punishing a civilian (p), and within a certain range, a mixed equilibrium emerges with both cooperating civilians and corrupt policers. In this equilibrium, the population mean payoff (efficiency) is higher than in the all-defect equilibrium, and can approach the fully cooperative payoff as power asymmetries increase. The model demonstrates that costly punishment can increase both cooperation and efficiency, especially when punishers are protected by power asymmetries. The likelihood of reaching the efficient equilibrium depends on the relative costs and effectiveness of punishment. The results challenge the view that costly punishment is always inefficient, showing that under the right conditions, it can benefit the group even when punishers are themselves corrupt.
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency depends critically on the structure of punishment costs and power asymmetries. If punishment is costly but power asymmetries exist (punishers are less vulnerable to punishment or punishment is more effective against non-punishers), then enabling punishment can increase efficiency above the control (no-punishment) baseline. The model suggests that the efficiency gain is larger when the punishment of defectors is strong and the punishment of punishers is weak. The findings are most directly applicable to Prisoner's Dilemma settings, but the logic is relevant for public goods games with similar punishment structures. The paper does not address all game design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, communication), but provides direct, mechanistic insight into how punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and power asymmetries moderate the efficiency impact of punishment.

- source: Watve, M. G., Damle, A., Ganguly, B., Kale, A., & Dahanukar, N. (2011). Blackmailing: the keystone in the human mating system. *Bmc Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a hidden-strategy game modeling human mating, the combination of punishment (social sanctions) and policing (gossip/observation), when stabilized by the possibility of blackmailing, leads to the stable coexistence of monogamy and other strategies, with monogamy maintained at high frequency. This results in higher average fitness (efficiency) for the population. If punishment is present but blackmailing is not possible, policing collapses due to second-order free-riding, and monogamy cannot be maintained. Thus, the introduction of punishment (with blackmailing) increases efficiency relative to the baseline with no punishment or with punishment alone. The model is robust to variations in the opportunity for blackmailing and to gender differences in payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment (with sufficient severity and the possibility of side-payments/blackmailing) in a public-goods-like environment increases group efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition. The model is not an exact PGG but is structurally analogous, with cooperation (monogamy) as the group-beneficial act and defection (EPM) as the selfish act. The results suggest that punishment alone may not be sufficient unless stabilized by additional incentives (e.g., blackmailing), but when these are present, efficiency is higher. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is costly and subject to second-order free-riding, and where additional mechanisms (like blackmailing) can offset these costs.

- source: Larson, J. M. (2016). The evolutionary advantage of limited network knowledge. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated prisoner's dilemma with gossip-based punishment, the ability to sustain full cooperation (and thus maximize group efficiency/fitness) depends on both the structure of the social network and the precision of individuals' knowledge about that structure. When all individuals have precise knowledge of the network, it is harder to sustain full cooperation because some individuals can be identified as weakly connected and thus are tempting targets for defection. When individuals have only coarse knowledge (e.g., know only the average neighborhood size), it is easier to sustain full cooperation, as targeting is not possible. The minimum discount factor required to sustain cooperation is lower under coarse knowledge than under precise knowledge, making full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency) more robust. The model thus predicts that limited network knowledge facilitates the enforcement of cooperation through punishment, leading to higher group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining high efficiency in repeated social dilemmas depends not only on the presence of punishment but also on the information structure—specifically, the precision of network knowledge. In environments where individuals have only coarse knowledge of the network, punishment is more effective at sustaining full cooperation and thus maximizing efficiency. If a public goods game or similar environment allows only limited information about the network or about others' positions, the model predicts a more positive effect of punishment on efficiency. Conversely, if players have precise knowledge of the network, the positive effect of punishment on efficiency may be reduced. The paper does not provide direct empirical estimates but offers clear theoretical mechanisms and comparative statics relevant to prediction.

- source: Vincent, J. R. (2007). Spatial dynamics, social norms, and the opportunity of the commons. *Ecological Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that economic incentives—specifically, the use of sanctions (punishments for non-cooperation and rewards for cooperation)—are critical for achieving efficient and sustainable management of common pool resources. Theoretical models (e.g., repeated games, evolutionary game theory) and empirical case studies (e.g., Ostrom 1990) show that monitoring and enforcement mechanisms enable groups to avoid the tragedy of the commons, leading to higher group profits and resource stocks. The author is skeptical that social norms alone, without sanctions, can achieve similar efficiency, especially when rapid or large-scale behavioral change is needed. The paper also notes that public goods (as opposed to common pool resources) face similar free-rider problems and require external enforcement (e.g., taxes, quotas) to achieve efficient provision.
  prediction_guidance: This paper supports the prediction that enabling punishment or sanctions in public-goods-game-like environments will increase efficiency, group payoff, and resource sustainability, especially when compared to environments without such mechanisms. The presence of monitoring and enforcement is highlighted as a key moderator: systems with sanctions are much more likely to achieve efficient outcomes than those relying on norms alone. The paper does not provide quantitative estimates but offers strong theoretical and qualitative support for the positive effect of punishment on efficiency. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment/sanctions should be coded as a strong positive moderator of efficiency, especially in settings analogous to common pool resources.

- source: dos Santos, M., Rankin, D. J., & Wedekind, C. (2013). HUMAN COOPERATION BASED ON PUNISHMENT REPUTATION. *Evolution*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the ability to punish defectors, combined with the visibility of punishment reputation, leads to higher cooperation and higher group payoffs. Punishers are compensated for their costs through increased cooperation from others, and in some groups, punishers even achieve net benefits. When punishment or its reputation is removed, cooperation and payoffs decline. The effect is driven by the threat of punishment and the reputational benefits it brings, not by the mere display of past punishment behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment, especially when punishment reputation is visible, increases group payoffs and compensates punishers, thus improving efficiency-related outcomes. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment and the visibility of punishment reputation are key moderators. The game is not a standard public goods game but a helping game with punishment and reputation, so direct quantitative transfer to PGGs should be cautious, but the qualitative effect of punishment on efficiency is robustly positive when reputation is present.

- source: Wubs, M., Bshary, R., & Lehmann, L. (2016). Coevolution between positive reciprocity, punishment, and partner switching in repeated interactions. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games, the evolutionary success of punishment, positive reciprocity, and partner switching depends on the number of rounds, group size, and the costs/effectiveness of punishment and switching. Punishment is favored in small and moderately sized groups, especially when punishment is efficient and its behavioral effect is long-lasting. Partner switching dominates in large groups or when the number of rounds is high, unless switching is costly. Positive reciprocity is rarely the dominant mechanism, except when punishment is less effective or more costly. The model does not directly report group efficiency or total payoff, but evolutionary success is determined by aggregate payoff, so the results indirectly inform about conditions under which punishment or other mechanisms would lead to higher average payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma-like environments. It suggests that punishment is more likely to improve group outcomes (in terms of evolutionary success/payoff) in small or moderately sized groups, especially when punishment is efficient and its behavioral effect is long-lasting. In large groups or with many rounds, partner switching may be more effective. The findings are most relevant for adjacent settings to public goods games, especially repeated dyadic interactions, and should be interpreted with caution when generalizing to standard PGGs. The paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency, but the evolutionary dominance of strategies is determined by payoff, so the results can be used to infer the likely direction of punishment's effect on efficiency under different design dimensions.

- source: Rand, D. G., Ohtsuki, H., & Nowak, M. A. (2009). Direct reciprocity with costly punishment: Generous tit-for-tat prevails. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper shows that in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games with costly punishment, the addition of a punishment option does not increase cooperation or group payoffs in evolutionary simulations. Even when Nash equilibrium analysis allows for cooperative equilibria using punishment, evolutionary selection in finite populations favors generous tit-for-tat strategies that do not use costly punishment. Costly punishment is rarely used, and when it is, it is associated with lower payoffs. The presence of costly punishment does not allow cooperation to evolve in parameter regions where it would not already be supported by direct reciprocity alone. These findings are robust to variations in game parameters and are supported by experimental data from Dreber et al. (2008).
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper suggests that enabling costly punishment in a repeated two-player PD-like environment does not increase efficiency or group payoff, regardless of the punishment cost or effectiveness. Evolutionary dynamics select against strategies that use costly punishment, and the most successful strategies are those that reciprocate cooperation and defect against defectors (generous tit-for-tat), not those that punish. Thus, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment in similar repeated games, one should not expect an increase in efficiency or group payoff, and may even expect a negative association between punishment use and payoff. However, the results are specific to repeated two-player PDs and may not generalize to larger group public goods games.

- source: Cui, P. B., & Wu, Z. X. (2014). Selfish punishment with avoiding mechanism can alleviate both first-order and second-order social dilemma. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that introducing selfish punishment with an avoiding mechanism into a spatial prisoner's dilemma game can alleviate both first-order and second-order social dilemmas, depending on the punishment cost, fine, and identification probability.
