The presence of selfish punishers allows for the stable coexistence of cooperators and punishers, and under certain parameter regimes, reduces the prevalence of defectors and second-order free riders. Theoretical predictions using extended pair approximation and interaction web analysis match well with simulation results. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of each strategy type, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment (especially when including mechanisms for selfish punishment and avoidance) can promote cooperation and reduce free riding in spatially structured social dilemmas. However, because the model is a prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game) and the main outcomes are strategy frequencies rather than group efficiency or total payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that the effectiveness of punishment depends on the cost/fine ratio and the ability to identify defectors, but do not provide direct quantitative predictions for efficiency outcomes in public goods games. The paper is most useful for understanding mechanisms and qualitative moderators of punishment effects, rather than for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Roos, P., Gelfand, M., Nau, D., & Carr, R. (2014). High strength-of-ties and low mobility enable the evolution of third-party punishment. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that responsible third-party punishment (3PP) can evolve and sustain cooperation in structured populations when social-structural constraints are high (i.e., high strength-of-ties and low mobility). Under these conditions, clusters of responsible punishers can induce cooperation in their local neighborhoods, leading to higher local pay-offs and the spread of both cooperation and punishment. In contrast, in well-mixed or highly mobile populations, responsible 3PP does not evolve, and cooperation fails to emerge. The model shows that 3PP is pivotal for the emergence of cooperation and increased pay-off, as neither population structure alone nor direct punishment alone suffice. The findings are robust to variations in network structure and to the presence of antisocial punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation and increasing pay-off (and thus efficiency) depends critically on the social structure of the population. Specifically, punishment is more likely to increase efficiency in games with high strength-of-ties (low average degree, i.e., players interact repeatedly with a small set of neighbors) and low mobility (players do not frequently change partners). In well-mixed or highly mobile settings, punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that game designs with fixed, repeated local interactions and low partner switching will see greater efficiency gains from punishment mechanisms, while designs with random matching or high mobility will not. The model is adjacent to public goods games (it uses a two-player cooperation game with third-party punishment), so the transfer to standard PGGs is not exact but likely informative.

- source: Gao, S. P., Wu, T., Nie, S. L., & Wang, L. (2015). Promote or hinder? The role of punishment in the emergence of cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation depends critically on the pattern of punishment and the presence of anti-social punishment. Centralized (pool) pro-social punishment is more effective than decentralized (peer) punishment at promoting cooperation. When anti-social punishment is possible and both pro- and anti-social punishments are in the same pattern, the positive effect of punishment is largely neutralized. However, if pro-social punishment is centralized and anti-social punishment is decentralized, cooperation can still be promoted. The results are derived from theoretical evolutionary game models and focus on the stationary abundance of strategies, not direct efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative theoretical evidence about how punishment design (centralized vs decentralized, presence of anti-social punishment) can affect the evolutionary success of cooperation in social dilemmas. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it shows that centralized punishment mechanisms are more robust to anti-social punishment and more likely to sustain cooperation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that games with centralized punishment (pool) are more likely to see positive effects of punishment on efficiency, especially when anti-social punishment is possible. However, the results are based on evolutionary dynamics and strategy abundance, not direct efficiency measures, and the model is a prisoner's dilemma rather than a public goods game.

- source: Ågren, J. A., Davies, N. G., & Foster, K. R. (2019). Enforcement is central to the evolution of cooperation. *Nature Ecology & Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that enforcement mechanisms—defined as actions that reduce selfishness within cooperative alliances—are predicted by evolutionary models to evolve across a wide range of biological systems, from genomes to human societies. The models show that, in the absence of enforcement, selfishness undermines cooperation and reduces group fitness. When enforcement is possible, it evolves under many parameter regimes and leads to higher cooperation and improved collective outcomes. Empirical examples from animal societies, mutualisms, and cellular systems support the theoretical predictions. The effectiveness of enforcement depends on its cost and the structure of the system, but its presence is generally associated with higher group efficiency or fitness.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment or enforcement in public-goods-like environments will increase group efficiency or collective payoff, relative to control conditions without enforcement. The models are not direct PGGs but are structurally analogous, and the mechanisms (punishment, policing, sanctions) map closely to punishment in PGGs. The effect is generally more positive, but the magnitude depends on the cost of enforcement and system details. The paper also highlights that enforcement is most critical when relatedness is low and that high enforcement costs can limit its effectiveness. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that adding punishment will increase efficiency, especially when enforcement is not prohibitively costly.

- source: Batistoni, T., Barclay, P., & Raihani, N. J. (2022). Third-party punishers do not compete to be chosen as partners in an experimental game. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment and helping are both used as signals of cooperative intent, but helping is a stronger and less ambiguous signal than punishment. Observability increases investments in both, but the possibility of partner choice does not further escalate investments. Higher investments in punishment or helping increase the likelihood of being chosen as a partner and being trusted, but this effect is much stronger for helping. Investments in both are positively associated with actual trustworthiness in a Trust Game. The study does not address group efficiency or total payoff in a public goods game, but provides evidence on the reputational and signaling functions of punishment and helping.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on group efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is adjacent to PGGs, using a Dictator Game with third-party punishment/helping and a subsequent Trust Game. The main outcomes are individual-level signaling, partner choice, and trust/trustworthiness, not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that punishment is a weaker and more ambiguous signal of cooperative intent than helping, and that reputational incentives may increase costly punishment/helping, but do not escalate under partner choice. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in PGGs, this paper provides only indirect, contextual evidence about the social and reputational motivations for punishment, not about its effect on group payoff or efficiency.

- source: Pedersen, E. J., Kurzban, R., & McCullough, M. E. (2013). Do humans really punish altruistically? A closer look. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that, in a lab setting where third-party punishment is possible but demand characteristics and audience effects are minimized, witnesses of unfairness toward others rarely engage in costly punishment. When they do, it is not motivated by moralistic anger but by envy. Victims of unfairness (those personally harmed) do punish at nontrivial rates. In hypothetical scenarios, people forecast much more punishment and anger than is observed in real behavior. The results challenge the robustness of altruistic third-party punishment in humans and suggest that previous findings may be due to experimental artefacts. No efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in dictator/third-party punishment games where the punisher is not personally harmed, actual costly punishment is rare and mild, and not motivated by norm-enforcing anger. This suggests that enabling punishment in similar games is unlikely to increase efficiency or group payoff, since punishment is rarely used and not directed at norm enforcement. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, and the findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is only available to third parties who are not personally affected. The results caution against assuming that punishment will improve efficiency in such designs.

- source: Konishi, N., & Ohtsubo, Y. (2015). Does dishonesty really invite third-party punishment? Results of a more stringent test. *Biology Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that, even after removing five key methodological confounds (including adding a reward option and ensuring anonymity), a significant minority of third-party observers are willing to incur a personal cost to punish a dishonest trustee, even when the trustee's allocation is fair. The rate of punishment is lower than in previous studies (27% vs. 53%), but still significantly higher in the dishonesty condition than in the honesty condition. Participants were more likely to punish than reward in response to dishonesty. The main outcomes are the frequency and cost of punishment/reward, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that third-party punishment of norm violations (specifically dishonesty) can occur even when methodological confounds are controlled. However, the design is a modified trust game with third-party punishment and reward, not a public goods game or direct group efficiency context. The outcomes are behavioral (punishment/reward rates and costs), not efficiency or group payoff. Thus, while the paper supports the robustness of third-party punishment as a behavioral phenomenon, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments. Its main value is in showing that punishment can persist even when alternative explanations are minimized, but it does not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on group efficiency.

- source: Gneezy, A., & Fessler, D. M. T. (2012). Conflict, sticks and carrots: war increases prosocial punishments and rewards. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that during periods of violent intergroup conflict, individuals are more willing to incur costs to punish non-cooperative group members (as measured by higher rejection rates of unfair offers in the Ultimatum Game) and to reward cooperative group members (as measured by higher returns in the Trust Game when the first mover is generous). These effects are observed by comparing behavior before, during, and after the 2006 Israel-Hezbollah war among Israeli senior citizens. The findings support the hypothesis that intergroup conflict elevates sanctioning behaviors that promote within-group cooperation. However, the study does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs, focusing instead on the behavioral propensity to punish and reward.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that real-world intergroup conflict increases individuals' willingness to punish non-cooperators and reward cooperators in economic games structurally related to public goods dilemmas. However, because the games used (Ultimatum and Trust Games) are not public goods games and the outcomes are behavioral (punishment/reward rates) rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The results suggest that contextual factors (such as intergroup conflict) can increase sanctioning behaviors, which, in public goods games, are often associated with higher cooperation and sometimes higher efficiency, but this link is not directly tested here. The study does not manipulate or report on most game design dimensions relevant to the prediction task (e.g., MPCR, player count, punishment cost/tech, etc.), so its direct predictive value for efficiency outcomes is limited.

- source: Baumgartner, T., Götte, L., Gügler, R., & Fehr, E. (2012). The mentalizing network orchestrates the impact of parochial altruism on social norm enforcement. *Human Brain Mapping*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment is strongly modulated by group membership: outgroup members who defect are punished much more than ingroup members who commit the same norm violation. This parochial punishment pattern is supported by increased activation and connectivity in a neural network associated with punishment-related decision processes (right orbitofrontal gyrus, right lateral prefrontal cortex, right dorsal caudatus) for outgroup punishment, and increased activation/connectivity in the mentalizing network (dorsomedial prefrontal cortex, bilateral temporo-parietal junction) for lenient ingroup punishment. The study provides evidence for neural mechanisms underlying parochial altruism in norm enforcement, but does not analyze or report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of a punishment option leads to parochial (group-biased) punishment behavior in a third-party punishment context, with outgroup norm violators punished more than ingroup violators. However, it does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The design is a one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma with third-party punishment, which is adjacent to but not the same as a repeated public goods game. The main value for prediction is in understanding that punishment can be highly group-biased, and that the behavioral effect of punishment may depend on group context, but the paper does not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Crockett, M. J., Apergis-Schoute, A., Herrmann, B., Lieberman, M., Müller, U., Robbins, T. W., & Clark, L. (2013). Serotonin Modulates Striatal Responses to Fairness and Retaliation in Humans. *Journal of Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that lowering serotonin via ATD increases costly punishment (rejection of unfair offers) in the ultimatum game (second-party context), but tends to decrease costly punishment in third-party punishment games. The behavioral effects are accompanied by neural changes: ATD reduces ventral striatal responses to fairness and increases dorsal striatal responses during retaliation. The results suggest that serotonin modulates the drive for retaliation rather than fairness preferences. The games are one-shot, with no repeated interaction, and the main outcomes are behavioral and neural, not payoff-based. There is no direct analysis of efficiency or group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological and neurobiological mechanisms underlying costly punishment, showing that serotonin depletion increases second-party punishment but decreases third-party punishment. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, and the games are not public goods games but rather ultimatum and third-party punishment games. The findings may inform predictions about the psychological drivers of punishment in similar environments, but do not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design features (one-shot, no communication, binary accept/reject, known costs of punishment) are informative for mapping to game dimensions, but the lack of efficiency outcomes limits direct predictive value for the downstream task.

- source: Li, S. Y., Ma, S. M., Wang, D. Y., Zhang, H. J., Li, Y. Z., Wang, J. X., Li, J. Y., Zhang, B. Y., Gross, J., De Dreu, C. K. W., Wang, W. X., & Ma, Y. N. (2022). Oxytocin and the Punitive Hub-Dynamic Spread of Cooperation in Human Social Networks. *Journal of Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in repeated networked games (trust game, ultimatum bargaining, and two-stage prisoner's dilemma with punishment), oxytocin administration to central (hub) players increases their willingness to enforce cooperation norms via costly punishment. This enforcement, rather than mere cooperation or trust, is what enables cooperation to spread throughout the network. Agent-based simulations confirm that high punishment rates by central nodes are more effective than high cooperation rates alone in promoting network-wide cooperation. Manipulating peripheral nodes' punishment or cooperation has much weaker effects. The main outcomes are cooperation and punishment rates, not group efficiency or total payoff, though some payoff equality measures are reported in the ultimatum game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence and effectiveness of costly punishment, especially when wielded by central/influential players, is a key moderator of cooperation in repeated networked social dilemmas. However, the main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation, punishment, norm enforcement), not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that, in networked repeated games with punishment, enabling effective punishment by central players can substantially increase cooperation rates, and by extension, may improve efficiency, but this is not directly measured. The results are most relevant for predicting behavioral outcomes (cooperation, norm enforcement) rather than efficiency as defined by group payoff relative to full cooperation. The design dimensions most strongly supported are network structure (heterogeneity, centrality), punishment existence and effectiveness, and repeated interaction. The study does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency change due to punishment, so its use for predicting efficiency outcomes is indirect and should be supplemented with studies that report group payoff or efficiency directly.

- source: Baumgartner, T., Schiller, B., Rieskamp, J., Gianotti, L. R., & Knoch, D. (2014). Diminishing parochialism in intergroup conflict by disrupting the right temporo-parietal junction. *Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment is more severe toward outgroup norm violators than ingroup violators (parochial punishment), especially in cases where the outgroup member defects against an ingroup member. Disrupting the right TPJ with rTMS reduces this parochial punishment, making third-parties treat outgroup and ingroup violators more similarly. This effect is mediated by a reduction in the motive to retaliate and a reduction in mentalizing bias (the tendency to take the perspective of ingroup more than outgroup members). The manipulation does not affect punishment in intragroup constellations or in non-defection cases. The study does not measure or report group efficiency or total payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the psychological and neural mechanisms underlying parochial punishment in third-party punishment games, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a Prisoner's Dilemma for norm violation and a third-party punishment stage, but does not involve repeated public goods contributions or measure group efficiency. The findings are relevant for understanding when and why punishment is more severe toward outgroup members, and how neural interventions can reduce this bias. However, the results cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no efficiency or payoff data are reported. The punishment technology (1 point spent reduces target's payoff by 3) is specified, but the main outcomes are behavioral (punishment assigned) and motivational (retaliation, perspective-taking), not payoff-based.

- source: Deutchman, P., Aellen, M., Bogese, M., Bshary, R., Drayton, L., Gil, D., Martin, J., Pretot, L., Raihani, N., Santos, L. R., & Mcauliffe, K. (2023). Punishment is sensitive to outside options in humans but not in cleaner fish (Labroides dimidiatus). *Animal Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that human participants are sensitive to the intent behind cheating when deciding to punish: they punish more when a partner chooses to cheat despite having a cooperative option. Cleaner fish, in contrast, do not show this sensitivity; male fish punish female partners at similar rates regardless of whether the female had a choice to cooperate or not. The main outcome is the likelihood or amount of punishment, not efficiency or group payoff. The human experiment is a novel economic game inspired by cleaner fish mutualism, not a standard public goods game, and does not report efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants, as it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its main contribution is to show that human punishment is sensitive to intent (alternative options), while cleaner fish punishment is not. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only indirectly relevant: it suggests that the psychological basis for punishment in humans includes sensitivity to intent, which may affect when and how punishment is used, but does not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.

- source: Xie, E. H., Liu, M. D., Liu, J. Q., Gao, X. X., & Li, X. C. (2022). Neural mechanisms of the mood effects on third-party responses to injustice after unfair experiences. *Human Brain Mapping*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that individuals who experience disadvantageous unfairness (receiving less than others) are more likely to choose third-party punishment in a subsequent justice game, while those who experience advantageous unfairness (receiving more than others) are more likely to choose third-party compensation. These effects are mediated by mood (negative emotion increases punishment, positive emotion increases compensation) and sense of unfairness. Neural data show that increased DLPFC activity is associated with compensation after advantageous unfairness, while decreased DLPFC activity is associated with punishment after disadvantageous unfairness. The study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and the design is not a standard public goods game but a third-party justice game with costly punishment and compensation options.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The design is adjacent to PGGs, focusing on third-party responses to unfairness rather than first-party cooperation or group provision. However, it provides informative evidence about the psychological and neural mechanisms that may underlie punishment and compensation choices, and how prior experiences and mood can shift the likelihood of punishment versus compensation. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not address how punishment affects group payoff or efficiency.

- source: Spadaro, G., Molho, C., Van Prooijen, J. W., Romano, A., Mosso, C. O., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2023). Corrupt third parties undermine trust and prosocial behaviour between people. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across five experimental studies, participants exposed to a corrupt (dishonest) third-party punisher (TPP) trusted strangers less and cooperated less in subsequent economic games (trust game, prisoner's dilemma, dictator game) with TPP present. The effect is robust to both second-hand and first-hand exposure to corruption, and to whether the TPP's corruption harms only the experiment or also a collective good. The negative effect of perceived TPP corruption on cooperation is mediated by reduced interpersonal trust. The studies do not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the direct effect of TPP corruption on efficiency is not measured. The design always includes a TPP; there is no comparison to a no-punishment baseline, so the effect of enabling punishment per se on efficiency cannot be determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the perceived integrity of a third-party punisher moderates the effectiveness of punishment institutions in promoting trust and cooperation. However, it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling punishment (vs. no punishment) on group efficiency or payoff, nor does it report efficiency outcomes. The findings suggest that if punishment is administered by corrupt or untrusted third parties, its positive effect on cooperation may be undermined or even reversed, but the paper does not quantify efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper is most informative about the moderating role of institutional trustworthiness, not about the baseline effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games.

- source: Zhang, Y., Yu, H. B., Yin, Y., & Zhou, X. L. (2016). Intention Modulates the Effect of Punishment Threat in Norm Enforcement via the Lateral Orbitofrontal Cortex. *Journal of Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that the intention behind punishment threat critically shapes norm compliance: when a partner intentionally retains the right to punish, participants allocate less to the partner (reduced norm compliance), but when the partner intentionally waives the right, participants allocate more (increased norm compliance and positive affect). When the punishment threat is unintentional (computer-decided), these effects are weaker or absent. The rLOFC is shown to mediate these effects, as brain stimulation modulates the behavioral response to intention. However, the study does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes, and the game is a modified dictator game rather than a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task: it shows that the social context and perceived intention behind punishment can strongly affect norm compliance (proxied by allocation), but does not report efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that in environments where punishment is perceived as self-serving or intentional, punishment may reduce cooperation or norm compliance, while unintentional or third-party punishment may be less detrimental or even neutral. For prediction, this implies that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency may depend on whether punishment is perceived as legitimate or self-interested, but the lack of efficiency data and the use of a dictator game limit direct applicability to public goods game efficiency predictions.

- source: Schlaepfer, A. (2018). The emergence and selection of reputation systems that drive cooperative behaviour. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a population of initially non-cooperative individuals, a reputation system based on punishment is likely to emerge first and drive the initial evolution of cooperation. However, once cooperation is established, it is mainly sustained by a reputation system based on cooperative actions (social image). The model shows that cooperation rates are highest when both punishment-based and cooperation-based reputation systems are available. The emergence and stability of these systems depend on the costs of information acquisition (gossip) and the relevance of active gossip channels. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but focuses on cooperation rates and the use of reputation systems.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment-based reputation systems can help establish cooperation in repeated social dilemmas, especially when information about others' behavior is costly to acquire. However, it does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it use a standard public goods game (it uses a dyadic Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment stage). The findings suggest that punishment may be most important for the initial emergence of cooperation, but less so for its maintenance. For prediction tasks about efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly by highlighting the role of punishment-based reputation in overcoming initial defection.

- source: Li, J. B., Pan, J. J., Zhu, C. K., & Wang, Y. W. (2022). Inter-brain synchronization is weakened by the introduction of external punishment. *Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of external punishment (i.e., making responder rejection payoff-relevant in the rrUG) leads to significantly higher mean offers from proposers compared to the rrDG (no punishment). However, the main focus is on neural outcomes: inter-brain synchronization is significantly lower in the punishment condition (rrUG) than in the no-punishment condition (rrDG). There is no significant effect of punishment on the rejection rate of responders. The study interprets the neural results as evidence that punishment crowds out moral motivation, reducing shared cognitive/emotional processing between players. No direct measures of group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that introducing punishment (via responder rejection in a two-player ultimatum game) increases proposer generosity (mean offer size), which could be associated with higher group payoffs if responders accept. However, the study does not report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games but is not a PGG or close variant. The main outcome is neural (inter-brain synchronization), not payoff-based. Thus, the paper offers only weak, indirect support for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Sánchez, A., & Cuesta, J. A. (2005). Altruism may arise from individual selection. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that strong reciprocity (altruistic punishment) can arise and persist in agent-based evolutionary models of the Ultimatum Game under individual selection, without the need for group or cultural selection, repeated interactions, or reputation. The key mechanism is the role of fluctuations and finite selection intervals, which allow altruistic punishers to survive and proliferate. The model reproduces the empirical finding that offers and acceptance thresholds are much higher than the self-interested Nash equilibrium. However, the paper does not analyze or report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, focusing instead on the distribution and evolution of behavioral strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the possibility that punishment (in the form of rejection of unfair offers) can evolve and persist under individual selection in one-shot, anonymous games. However, it does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings are most relevant for understanding the evolutionary plausibility of punishment behaviors, not for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment. Thus, its direct value for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions is limited, though it may inform mechanistic expectations about the robustness of punishment behaviors.

- source: Baum, W. M., Paciotti, B., Richerson, P., Lubell, M., & McElreath, R. (2012). Cooperation due to cultural norms, not individual reputation. *Behavioural Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that in a trust game with a conditional fine, trustees return more to investors from their own ethnic group when no fine is available. When a fine is available and imposed, trustworthiness increases in cross-ethnic pairs, eliminating the in-group advantage. In same-ethnic pairs, the fine has no effect or may crowd out trustworthiness. The effect is similar when the fine is available but not used. The study does not report group efficiency or total earnings as a main outcome, but the behavioral results suggest that punishment is more effective at increasing trustworthiness across ethnic lines than within them.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation (measured as trustworthiness) depends on group identity: punishment increases trustworthiness in cross-ethnic pairs but not in same-ethnic pairs. However, because the outcome is not group efficiency or total payoff, the results should be used with caution for predicting efficiency. The design is a modified trust game, not a public goods game, so transferability to PGGs is limited but informative for settings where group identity and punishment interact. The punishment is costless to the punisher and has a fixed impact, which should be considered when mapping to other designs.

- source: Güney, S., & Newell, B. R. (2013). Fairness overrides reputation: the importance of fairness considerations in altruistic cooperation. *Frontiers in Human Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments show that rejection of unfair offers (costly punishment) in the Mini Ultimatum Game is primarily driven by fairness considerations, particularly the perceived intentions of the proposer. When intentions are not assessable, outcome fairness still motivates some punishment, but to a lesser degree. The opportunity for reputation building and future interaction (i.e., repeated, non-anonymous play) does not significantly increase costly punishment when fairness considerations are strong, but can increase punishment when intentions are ambiguous. The study does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the psychological drivers of costly punishment in ultimatum-like bargaining games are primarily fairness-based, not reputation-based, and that the presence of repeated interaction or reputation opportunities does not necessarily increase punishment or change behavioral outcomes when fairness motives are strong. However, because the study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The findings are most relevant for understanding when and why punishment occurs, not its aggregate payoff consequences.

- source: Marlowe, F. W., Berbesque, J. C., Barrett, C., Bolyanatz, A., Gurven, M., & Tracer, D. (2011). The 'spiteful' origins of human cooperation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that: (1) Small-scale societies are less generous in the Dictator Game and less likely to engage in third-party punishment, but are equally likely to engage in second-party punishment (Ultimatum Game) as large societies. (2) In large societies, third-party punishment is more common and even exceeds second-party punishment, suggesting a greater role for norm enforcement. (3) Generosity and punishment behaviors are correlated across games and societies. The findings challenge the view that strong reciprocity (including third-party punishment) is universal, suggesting that 'spiteful' second-party punishment is more fundamental in small-scale societies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment, as it does not use a PGG or report efficiency-related outcomes. However, it provides informative context about the prevalence and type of punishment (second- vs third-party) across societies, which may be relevant for understanding the social and cultural moderators of punishment effects in related game environments. The results suggest that the willingness to punish (especially second-party) is widespread, but the emergence of third-party punishment and higher generosity is more characteristic of large, complex societies. For prediction tasks, this implies that the social context (e.g., group size, societal complexity) may moderate the type and frequency of punishment, but the direct impact on efficiency in PGG-like games is not addressed.

- source: Marlowe, F. W., Berbesque, J. C., Barr, A., Barrett, C., Bolyanatz, A., Cardenas, J. C., Ensminger, J., Gurven, M., Gwako, E., Henrich, J., Henrich, N., Lesorogol, C., McElreath, R., & Tracer, D. (2008). More 'altruistic' punishment in larger societies. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the level of third-party punishment (as measured by MAO) is significantly higher in larger and more complex societies. Both local group size and ethnic group size are strong predictors of the willingness to punish unfair offers in a third-party punishment game. The results suggest that third-party punishment is more prevalent in larger societies, possibly as a response to increased anonymity and collective action problems. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the relationship between group size and the prevalence of third-party punishment in dictator-like games, which are adjacent to public goods games. It does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it suggests that the willingness to punish norm violations increases with group size and social complexity, which may be relevant for understanding the conditions under which punishment is more likely to be used or effective. For the prediction task, this paper is mainly informative about the social and cultural moderators of punishment behavior, not about efficiency effects.

- source: Lo Gerfo, E., Gallucci, A., Morese, R., Vergallito, A., Ottone, S., Ponzano, F., Locatelli, G., Bosco, F., & Lauro, L. J. R. (2019). The role of ventromedial prefrontal cortex and temporo-parietal junction in third-party punishment behavior. *Neuroimage*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that modulating neural activity in the VMPFC increases the tendency to punish unfair behavior (altruistic punishment), while modulating the TPJ increases the tendency to punish fair behavior (antisocial punishment) in a third-party punishment game. The behavioral outcomes are measured as the decision to punish and the amount of punishment, not as group efficiency or total payoff. The study does not report on the effect of punishment on group welfare, efficiency, or aggregate earnings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. Its findings are limited to individual punishment behavior in a third-party context, with no analysis of how punishment affects group outcomes or efficiency. The game is a modified Dictator Game, not a standard PGG or repeated PGG, and the outcomes are not payoff-based. Thus, the paper offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task, except as context for the psychological and neural mechanisms underlying punishment behavior.

- source: Wen, Y. J., He, Z. X., Shen, C., & Tanimoto, J. (2025). Indirect punishment can outperform direct punishment in promoting cooperation in structured populations. *Plos Computational Biology*.

  findings: The study finds that, in structured populations modeled as a square lattice, indirect punishment (punishing defectors who harm one's neighbors) can outperform direct punishment (punishing one's own defectors) in promoting cooperation, but only when both the cost and fine of punishment are low. Outside this narrow parameter region, direct punishment may be more effective, both may be equally effective, or neither may improve cooperation. The mechanism is that indirect punishment can impose more total fines on defectors without greatly increasing the cost to punishers, but only when punishment is cheap. The main reported outcomes are cooperation rates and the distribution of strategies; group efficiency or total payoff is not directly measured.

While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it suggests that indirect punishment can be more effective at promoting cooperation than direct punishment under low-cost, low-fine conditions, but not otherwise. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results are only adjacent, as the main outcomes are behavioral. The findings may inform expectations about when punishment is likely to increase efficiency, but direct quantitative mapping is not possible from this paper alone.

- source: Vukov, J., Pinheiro, F. L., Santos, F. C., & Pacheco, J. M. (2013). Reward from Punishment Does Not Emerge at All Costs. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in structured (scale-free network) populations, the introduction of peer punishment does not always promote cooperation. When the cost-to-impact ratio (q/p) is high, punishment can actually reduce the prevalence of cooperation compared to the no-punishment baseline. Only when the cost of punishing is sufficiently low relative to its impact does punishment enhance cooperation. The effect is mapped across a range of game parameters (T, S) and punishment parameters (p, q), showing regions where punishment is beneficial or detrimental for cooperation. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It does not report efficiency or group payoff, but shows that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by implication, on efficiency in adjacent games) is highly sensitive to the cost-effectiveness of punishment (q/p) and to the network structure. In structured populations, punishment can backfire unless costs are low. For prediction, this suggests that the efficiency impact of punishment in public-goods-like games may be negative or mixed if punishment is costly relative to its impact, especially in networked or heterogeneous settings. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff outcomes limits the direct applicability of these findings to the prediction task.

- source: Zhang, C. Y., Zhu, Y. Y., Chen, Z. Q., & Zhang, J. L. (2017). Punishment in the form of shared cost promotes altruism in the cooperative dilemma games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a multi-player Prisoner's Dilemma with punishment (where only cooperators punish and share the cost), the evolutionary outcome depends on the punishment parameters and group size. Higher punishment effectiveness (greater penalty to defectors) and lower cost to punishers promote the spread and fixation of cooperation. Larger group size and higher temptation to defect make cooperation harder to sustain. The model provides explicit conditions under which cooperation can dominate, coexist with defection, or be eliminated. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators at equilibrium, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it shows that punishment with shared cost can promote the fixation of cooperation under certain parameter regimes, especially when punishment is effective and not too costly. The effect of group size (player_count) and temptation to defect (analogous to MPCR) are also analyzed. The results suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment with shared cost is likely to increase cooperation rates (and thus efficiency) when punishment is strong and not too costly, but the effect is weaker or negative when punishment is weak or costly, or when group size is large. However, because the model outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency, the translation to efficiency must be inferred.

- source: Sekiguchi, T., & Nakamaru, M. (2009). Effect of the presence of empty sites on the evolution of cooperation by costly punishment in spatial games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The presence of empty sites in spatially structured populations (lattices) promotes the evolution of cooperation and does not reduce the effect of costly punishment. In contrast, in completely mixing populations, empty sites reduce the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation. The evolutionary success of strategies depends on the benefit of cooperation, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the density of empty sites. The model does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but rather which strategies dominate under different parameter regimes. The findings suggest that spatial structure and population density (via empty sites) are important moderators of the evolutionary dynamics of punishment and cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It shows that spatial structure (analogous to networked or local interaction in PGGs) and the presence of empty sites (analogous to non-participation or dropout) can strongly moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation. In spatial settings, punishment remains effective even with empty sites, while in well-mixed settings, empty sites undermine punishment's effectiveness. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The results are most useful for understanding when punishment is likely to promote cooperation (and by extension, possibly efficiency) depending on spatial structure and participation rates, but do not provide quantitative efficiency effects.

- source: Raihani, N. J., Grutter, A. S., & Bshary, R. (2012). Female cleaner fish cooperate more with unfamiliar males. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that female cleaner fish behave more cooperatively (feed more against their preference) when paired with unfamiliar males, who are more aggressive in punishing cheating than familiar males. Preventing male punishment reduces female cooperation. The effect of punishment on female cheating is short-lived, with no significant long-term effect. Male cooperative behavior is not affected by partner familiarity, likely because females do not punish males. The results suggest that in asymmetric interactions, harsher punishment from unfamiliar partners can induce greater cooperation from subordinates, even when familiarity would otherwise be expected to promote cooperation. However, the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral proxies for cooperation and punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence and severity of punishment can increase cooperative behavior in asymmetric dyadic interactions, even when the punisher is unfamiliar. However, because the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games. The findings are most relevant for understanding the behavioral mechanisms by which punishment increases cooperation, especially in settings with asymmetric power and punishment ability. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that enabling punishment (especially effective, low-cost punishment by dominants) can increase cooperation rates, but does not provide direct evidence about the impact on group efficiency or welfare.

- source: Wang, Y. F., & Meng, X. Z. (2023). Evolutionary game dynamics of cooperation in prisoner?s dilemma with time delay. *Mathematical Biosciences and Engineering*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a 2-player prisoner's dilemma, adding a penalty for mutual defection (punishment) leads to stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors when the penalty exceeds the cost of cooperation (a > c). The equilibrium proportion of cooperators increases with the penalty size. When time delays are introduced, the system can undergo a Hopf bifurcation: for small delays, the equilibrium is stable, but for delays above a critical threshold (which decreases as penalty increases), the system oscillates between cooperation and defection. Adding mutation (random switching between strategies) slightly reduces the equilibrium proportion of cooperators, but does not fundamentally change the qualitative dynamics. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the frequency of cooperation and the stability of these frequencies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that increasing the severity of punishment for defection in a prisoner's dilemma increases the equilibrium proportion of cooperators, which is typically associated with higher group payoff in such games. However, the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so the findings must be interpreted as indirect evidence. The results suggest that, in similar 2-player games, enabling punishment (with sufficient severity) will increase cooperation rates, and thus likely improve efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline. However, if time delays are present and large, the system may become unstable and oscillate, which could reduce average efficiency. The findings are most relevant for settings with 2 players, no communication, continuous contribution/strategy choice, and deterministic evolutionary dynamics. The results are less directly transferable to standard public goods games with more players, explicit payoff reporting, or experimental data.

- source: Raihani, N. J., Pinto, A. I., Grutter, A. S., Wismer, S., & Bshary, R. (2012). Male cleaner wrasses adjust punishment of female partners according to the stakes. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that male cleaner wrasses adjust the severity of punishment towards female partners based on the stakes of the cheating event (client value) and the relative size of the female. More severe punishment is delivered when cheating is more costly to the male (high-value client) and when the female is similar in size to the male. In turn, females respond to more severe punishment by behaving more cooperatively (cheating less in subsequent opportunities). The results support the idea that punishment can be flexibly adjusted to circumstances and that such flexibility can influence cooperative behavior. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment can be flexibly and contextually applied in cooperative dilemmas, and that increased punishment can reduce cheating and increase cooperative behavior. However, because the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot directly inform quantitative predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings are most useful for understanding the behavioral mechanisms and moderators (e.g., cost of cheating, partner asymmetry) that may influence the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation, but not for direct efficiency prediction.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent

  findings: The paper provides a decision-tree framework linking specific game structures (defined by investment, payoff structure, symmetry, repetition, and partner choice) to the partner control mechanisms that can stabilize cooperation. Punishment is predicted to be a viable control mechanism in repeated interactions without partner choice, especially when both players can invest and defect. The framework also highlights the roles of reward, sanctions, partner switching, and indirect reciprocity in other game structures. The review does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence on efficiency or payoff effects of punishment, but it does clarify the conditions under which punishment is theoretically expected to promote cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is useful for identifying which game structures are theoretically compatible with punishment as a mechanism for stabilizing cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence on the efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in public goods or similar games. For the downstream prediction task, the framework can help map which design dimensions (e.g., repeated interactions, partner choice, symmetry) are necessary for punishment to be a relevant mechanism, but it cannot inform the magnitude or direction of efficiency changes. The paper is most useful for contextualizing when punishment is likely to be effective, not for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Bingham, P. M. (1999). Human uniqueness: A general theory. *Quarterly Review of Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The central finding is that coalitional enforcement—cooperative, low-cost punishment of defectors—was necessary and sufficient for the evolution of large-scale, stable cooperation among unrelated individuals in humans. The theory predicts that as the efficiency and scale of coalitional enforcement increased (via technological and social monitoring advances), so did the extent and complexity of human cooperation. The paper discusses how this mechanism underlies the emergence of language, culture, large political units, and rapid technological progress. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence or quantitative findings about efficiency or payoffs in public goods game-like environments; rather, it offers a theoretical framework for understanding the evolutionary roots of cooperation and punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that punishment (specifically, coalitional or group-based punishment) can enable and stabilize high levels of cooperation among non-kin, by reducing the individual cost of enforcement and making defection unprofitable. For the downstream prediction task, the paper suggests that the presence and efficiency of punishment mechanisms are critical moderators of group cooperation and, by extension, group efficiency. However, because the paper does not report empirical or experimental data on efficiency or payoffs in public goods games, it cannot be used to calibrate quantitative predictions. Its main value is in supporting the general principle that more efficient, lower-cost, and more widely distributed punishment mechanisms should be associated with higher group cooperation and efficiency, all else equal.

  findings: The model shows that the introduction of punishment (policing) as a third strategy in a repeated PD game can lead to stable coexistence or oscillatory dominance cycles among cooperators, defectors, and punishers, depending on ecological and game parameters. The strength of punishment, temptation to defect, and ecological benefits (from 'free space') all modulate which strategies persist. Moderate punishment can suppress defectors and promote cooperation, but high temptation to defect can still undermine cooperation. The model also demonstrates that initial conditions and parameter choices can lead to multistability, with different attractors (e.g., dominance by one strategy, coexistence, or extinction). The focus is on the eco-evolutionary dynamics of strategy frequencies, not on group efficiency or total welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that punishment can promote the persistence of cooperation in social dilemma settings, but the results are framed in terms of population dynamics (strategy frequencies and coexistence) rather than efficiency or group payoff. The model is based on a repeated PD game, not a public goods game, and does not report efficiency as defined by total group payoff relative to full cooperation. Therefore, while the findings support the idea that punishment can help sustain cooperation under certain conditions, they do not provide direct quantitative guidance for predicting efficiency changes in PGG-like environments with or without punishment. The results may be useful for understanding qualitative moderators (e.g., the importance of punishment strength, temptation, ecological context), but are not directly transferable to the efficiency prediction task.

Heterogeneity in individual values and costs leads to diverse group outcomes. Punishment stabilizes cooperative equilibria and is mainly administered by those with high norm internalization. The models are general and can be applied to a range of social norm contexts, including public-goods-like settings, but do not directly model or report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.

  findings: The paper finds that in mobile hunter-gatherer societies, group punishment—ranging from shaming and ostracism to capital punishment—has been a central mechanism for enforcing cooperation and curbing antisocial behaviors such as bullying, theft, and cheating. These sanctions are argued to have shaped the evolution of human conscience and moral norms, favoring individuals who are generous, honest, and humble. The author suggests that such punitive social selection has been critical for the emergence of egalitarian, cooperative societies, and for the evolutionary success of altruistic traits. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence or quantitative analysis of efficiency or group payoffs in public goods game-like settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and ethnographic context for the role of punishment in promoting cooperation and suppressing free-riding in small-scale societies. It supports the idea that punishment can be a powerful tool for maintaining cooperation, but does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or their variants. For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper offers indirect support for the positive role of punishment in sustaining cooperation, especially in small, face-to-face groups, but does not allow for parameter-specific predictions based on game design dimensions.

- source: Molho, C., & Wu, J. H. (2021). Direct punishment and indirect reputation-based tactics to intervene against offences. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper argues that both direct punishment and indirect, reputation-based tactics (gossip, ostracism) are important for maintaining cooperation and enforcing norms. Direct punishment is more effective at recalibrating offenders' behavior but is costly and risky, while indirect tactics are less costly and reduce retaliation risk but are less effective at changing behavior. The choice of tactic depends on factors such as the self-relevance of the offense, relationship to the offender, power dynamics, and emotional responses (anger vs. disgust). The paper highlights that indirect tactics are more prevalent in real-world settings than direct punishment as operationalized in laboratory public goods games. It also notes that punishment can sometimes reduce efficiency, and that the ecological validity of lab findings is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it offers theoretical context suggesting that the effectiveness and efficiency of punishment depend on its cost, the risk of retaliation, and the availability of alternative (indirect) sanctioning mechanisms. It cautions that lab-based punishment games may overestimate the prevalence and impact of direct punishment compared to real-world settings, where indirect tactics are more common. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of communication, relationship context, and the type of punishment available may moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation and efficiency, but it does not provide quantitative or directly predictive evidence.

- source: Cofnas, N. (2018). POWER IN CULTURAL EVOLUTION AND THE SPREAD OF PROSOCIAL NORMS. *Quarterly Review of Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The paper argues that mainstream cultural evolutionary models, which treat punishment as an individual-level, uncoordinated action, fail to capture the collective, power-based enforcement of prosocial norms observed in real-world societies, especially among hunter-gatherers. It contends that collective punishment and coordinated norm enforcement are central to the evolution of morality and prosociality, and that these processes are not well-modeled by standard public goods game frameworks. The paper reviews anthropological evidence for collective punishment and discusses how this shapes moral psychology, but does not report or synthesize efficiency or payoff outcomes from experimental games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides important theoretical context for interpreting the role of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments, emphasizing that real-world punishment is often collective and coordinated, not merely the sum of individual actions. However, it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it analyze how game design dimensions (such as player count, rounds, or punishment cost) moderate these effects. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that models which ignore collective decision-making and power dynamics may misrepresent the real impact of punishment, but it does not provide data or direct evidence to calibrate predictions of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Bshary, A., & Bshary, R. (2010). Self-Serving Punishment of a Common Enemy Creates a Public Good in Reef Fishes. *Current Biology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that (1) punishment by victim fish (chasing the blenny) reduces the likelihood of being attacked again, (2) in shoaling species, this punishment increases the probability that the blenny switches to another species, creating a public good for the group, and (3) blennies can learn to discriminate between punishers and nonpunishers, which selects against free riding. The findings are based on both field observations and controlled laboratory experiments using artificial plates to simulate fish. The outcomes are behavioral and do not include direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff, but the results are relevant to understanding how punishment can create public goods in repeated natural interactions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that in repeated, naturalistic interactions, self-serving punishment can create public good benefits for a group, and that the ability of the 'cheater' (blenny) to identify and target nonpunishers can sustain punishment even in the absence of kin structure. However, the study does not use a formal public goods game, does not manipulate standard game design dimensions (e.g., MPCR, player count, explicit payoff structure), and does not report efficiency or group payoff. Thus, while the behavioral mechanisms are informative for understanding the conditions under which punishment can sustain cooperation and public goods, the findings cannot be directly mapped to predictions about efficiency in laboratory public goods games with or without punishment. The main value is in highlighting the importance of repeated interactions, individual identification, and self-serving motives in the emergence of public goods via punishment.

  findings: The study finds that cleaner fish, when exposed to simulated punishment (chasing) or partner switching (fleeing) after eating their preferred food, learn to avoid the preferred food and instead eat more of the less preferred food. This behavioral shift is interpreted as evidence that punishment and partner switching can promote cooperative behavior in mutualistic interactions. The control group, which experienced no negative consequences, increased their preference for the preferred food. The findings support the idea that both punishment and the threat of partner switching can be effective partner control mechanisms, but the outcomes are measured in terms of behavioral change rather than direct payoffs or efficiency.

For prediction tasks about efficiency in PGG-like environments, this paper suggests that punishment and partner switching can change behavior toward more cooperative outcomes, but does not quantify the effect on efficiency or provide data on group-level payoffs. Its main value is in supporting the behavioral mechanism by which punishment can promote cooperation.
