  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in the survivor's dilemma, cooperation can be evolutionarily stable if the benefit-to-cost ratio is high and/or environmental volatility is low. In these cases, mutual cooperation leads to higher expected lifespan (efficiency) for all. However, if the benefit-to-cost ratio is low or volatility is high, defection dominates and efficiency is lower. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; instead, indirect punishment occurs because defectors lose partners and thus reduce their own survival prospects. The findings are presented for both 2- and 3-player games, with detailed analysis of parameter regions where cooperation or defection is stable. The results are directly about efficiency (expected lifespan) and its dependence on game parameters, but do not address the effect of enabling or disabling explicit punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence about how efficiency (expected lifespan) in repeated social dilemmas depends on group size, benefit-to-cost ratio, and environmental volatility. It does not directly address the effect of enabling or disabling punishment, as no explicit punishment mechanism is present. However, it does show that indirect forms of 'punishment' (loss of cooperative partners) can sustain cooperation and efficiency under certain conditions. For prediction tasks involving explicit punishment in public goods games, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that efficiency gains from cooperation depend strongly on the benefit-to-cost ratio and the likelihood of future interaction (volatility), and that even without explicit punishment, cooperation can sometimes be stable. The model is adjacent to PGGs and does not manipulate or analyze punishment as a treatment.

- source: Johnstone, R. A., & Bshary, R. (2007). Indirect reciprocity in asymmetric interactions: when apparent altruism facilitates profitable exploitation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in asymmetric games where only one party (the actor) can help or exploit, and the other (the recipient) can only choose whether to interact, a form of indirect reciprocity can evolve. Actors help when the gains from exploitation are low to maintain a positive image, which encourages recipients to approach them in the future, allowing profitable exploitation when the gains are high. This strategy is stable and increases the long-term average payoff for both actors and recipients, provided certain conditions on observability, state-switching, and payoff parameters are met. The model shows that efficiency (average group payoff) is higher under these conditions than if actors always exploit or if recipients always avoid interaction.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in asymmetric games with image scoring and conditional approach, mechanisms analogous to punishment (here, avoidance of exploiters) can increase efficiency by supporting conditional cooperation. The findings suggest that when harmful behavior is observable and actors' states vary, efficiency is maximized for intermediate values of key parameters (cost of harm, switching probability, observability). While not a PGG or direct punishment setting, the model is adjacent and supports the idea that social mechanisms that deter exploitation (even without direct punishment) can improve group efficiency. For prediction, the paper suggests that enabling mechanisms for reputation-based avoidance or indirect sanctions can increase efficiency, especially when exploitation is not always profitable and can be detected.

- source: Johnstone, R. A., & Bshary, R. (2002). From parasitism to mutualism: partner control in asymmetric interactions. *Ecology Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The model demonstrates that in asymmetric interactions, the ability of the exploited party to terminate the interaction acts as a deterrent to exploitation, increasing the efficiency (joint payoff) of the system. As the exploiter's power to prolong the interaction increases, exploitation rises, the victim's payoff falls (eventually becoming negative), and the system shifts from mutualism to parasitism. The temptation to cheat (benefit/cost ratio) and the rate at which mutualistic benefits diminish also modulate these outcomes. The model is supported qualitatively by empirical data from cleaner-fish systems, where larger clients (with more to gain from cleaning) experience less exploitation and longer interactions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that mechanisms allowing the exploited party to control the duration of interaction (analogous to exit, partner choice, or punishment in PGG-like games) can increase efficiency by deterring exploitation. While not a PGG or direct punishment experiment, the model is relevant for predicting that increasing the ability of group members to sanction or exit (or otherwise control exploiters) will generally increase group efficiency, especially when the temptation to cheat is not overwhelming. The model also suggests that efficiency gains from punishment or partner control are greatest when the benefits of cooperation are high and the costs of cheating are high relative to the exploiter's gain. However, the model does not address repeated group interactions, explicit punishment costs, or the full range of PGG design dimensions, so its predictive value is indirect.

- source: Peña, J., Heifetz, A., & Nöldeke, G. (2024). The shirker's dilemma and the prospect of cooperation in large groups. *Theoretical Population Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in the shirker's dilemma, as group size increases, the proportion of volunteers at the stable equilibrium increases (for low enough costs), but the expected payoff (efficiency) decreases. The probability that the public good is provided can either increase or decrease with group size, depending on the cost of cooperation. In the limit of very large groups and low costs, the proportion of volunteers approaches one, and the expected payoff and success probability converge to positive values. However, the basin of attraction for the cooperative equilibrium shrinks with group size, making cooperation less likely to be reached from random initial conditions. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in a binary-action threshold collective action game (shirker's dilemma), increasing group size generally reduces efficiency (expected payoff at equilibrium), even though the proportion of volunteers may increase. The findings suggest that, in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, larger groups are less efficient, and the probability of successful collective action may not improve with group size. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that, in similar threshold public goods environments without punishment, efficiency will decrease as group size increases. However, because the model does not include punishment, it does not directly inform the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, but it does provide a theoretical baseline for control (no-punishment) conditions in threshold games.

- source: Kroumi, D. (2025). The role of selective extinction in promoting cooperation in a many demes model. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that structuring a population into demes and introducing selective extinction—whereby groups with more defectors are more likely to go extinct—can dramatically increase the evolutionary success (fixation probability) of cooperation in the Prisoner's Dilemma. Without selective extinction (\alpha=0), defection is always favored, and cooperation cannot persist. As the selective extinction parameter \alpha increases, the fixation probability of cooperation increases, and that of defection decreases. There are explicit threshold values of \alpha (functions of game payoffs and demographic parameters) above which cooperation is favored by selection, and above which it is more likely to fix than defection. The model also shows how the baseline extinction rate (v) and the structure of payoffs (T-R, P-S) modulate these thresholds. The results are robust to several alternative life-cycle assumptions. The main outcome is that group-level demographic feedback (selective extinction) can overcome the individual-level advantage of defection and promote group-level efficiency (cooperation).
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in structured populations with group-level demographic feedback (selective extinction of defect-dominated groups), the efficiency of the system (measured by the fixation probability of cooperation and thus long-run group payoff) can be substantially increased by increasing the strength of selective extinction (\alpha). In the absence of such feedback, efficiency remains low (defection dominates). The model gives explicit formulas for the critical value of \alpha needed to shift the system from defection-dominated to cooperation-dominated, as a function of game payoffs and demographic parameters (deme size, number of demes, baseline extinction rate). While the model is not a direct public goods game and does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, the selective extinction process is functionally analogous to a group-level sanction. Thus, the findings support the prediction that introducing or strengthening group-level sanctions (even if not individual punishment) can increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments, especially when the control (no-sanction) efficiency is low.

- source: Garay, J., Csiszár, V., & Móri, T. F. (2014). Under multilevel selection: When shall you be neither spiteful nor envious?. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in structured populations with multilevel selection, the evolutionary stability of strategies depends on the relative strength of group-level survival effects versus within-group competition. When group survival (due to predation or intergroup conflict) is highly sensitive to group size (i.e., total fecundity), strategies that maximize group fecundity (neutral or donator) are favored, leading to higher group efficiency. When group-level effects are weak, spiteful or envious strategies can dominate, reducing group efficiency. The model shows that increasing the benefit of donation or the cost of spite/envious behavior further promotes efficient, cooperative strategies. The results are robust to parameter changes and apply to both predation and intergroup conflict scenarios.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in environments where group-level survival or payoff is highly sensitive to group size or total contribution, mechanisms that increase group efficiency (such as donation or cooperation) will be favored over spiteful or envious (punitive) strategies. While the model does not directly implement a public goods game with explicit punishment, it is adjacent: 'spiteful' and 'envious' strategies are analogous to costly punishment, and 'donator' is analogous to cooperation or reward. The findings suggest that increasing the importance of group-level outcomes (e.g., by increasing the marginal per-capita return or the impact of group payoff on survival) will make punishment less stable and cooperation more stable, thus increasing efficiency. However, the model is not a direct PGG and does not include explicit punishment technology or cost parameters as in standard PGG experiments.

- source: Sasaki, T., Okada, I., & Nakai, Y. (2016). Indirect reciprocity can overcome free-rider problems on costly moral assessment. *Biology Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in indirect reciprocity models with costly assessment, cooperation is not stable unless there is a mechanism to sanction or exclude those who evade the cost of assessment. By introducing a pre-assessment stage that labels non-payers as 'nasty' and excludes them from receiving help, the model shows that a cooperative equilibrium (where everyone pays for assessment and helps others) can be locally stable if the benefit of helping exceeds the cost of assessment. This mechanism is analogous to pool punishment in public goods games and is effective under leading social norms. The findings are theoretical but directly address efficiency and the conditions for stable cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that institutional mechanisms to sanction or exclude cost-evaders (such as pre-assessment or pool punishment) can significantly improve efficiency in social dilemma games with costly monitoring or punishment. For prediction tasks, the presence of a pre-assessment or institutionalized punishment mechanism is likely to shift efficiency from low (collapse of cooperation) to high (stable cooperation), provided the net benefit of cooperation is positive. The results are robust across different social norms and suggest that the design of the punishment/sanctioning mechanism is a key moderator of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Fletcher, J. A., & Zwick, M. (2006). Unifying the theories of inclusive fitness and reciprocal altruism. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper demonstrates that the evolution of cooperation (altruism) in both kin and non-kin contexts can be predicted by a generalized Hamilton's rule that incorporates the covariance between genotypes and the cooperative behaviors of others, as well as nonadditive (synergistic) effects. In iterated prisoner's dilemma models, conditional strategies like tit-for-tat can increase in frequency when the benefit received from others' cooperation exceeds the cost, as predicted by Queller's rule. The same logic applies to mutualistic interactions between species. The paper shows that synergy (nonadditivity) and conditional behavior can both facilitate the evolution of cooperation, even in the absence of kinship or repeated interactions. Punishment is discussed as a possible mechanism for positive assortment but is not modeled. The findings are theoretical and do not provide empirical estimates of efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical background for understanding how mechanisms like punishment, synergy, and conditional behavior can affect the evolution of cooperation and group efficiency, but it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It suggests that any mechanism (including punishment) that increases positive assortment or synergy can increase the likelihood that cooperation evolves, but does not specify the magnitude or direction of efficiency changes in experimental settings. For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper is useful for mechanistic context but not for direct quantitative prediction.

- source: Golubski, A. J., & Klausmeier, C. A. (2010). Control in mutualisms Combined implications of partner choice and bargaining roles. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the ability to appropriate surplus (analogous to control over punishment/reward) and the ability to choose partners (partner choice) interact to determine the evolution of mutualism and the distribution of payoffs in mutualistic associations. When the guild with partner choice also receives the surplus, mutualistic strategies are favored and average payoffs are high. However, the presence of empty slots or high frequencies of exploiters can lead to cyclical dynamics where exploitative strategies temporarily increase, reducing average payoffs. The model predicts that partner choice is most effective at selecting against moderately exploitative partners, but highly exploitative partners can persist under some conditions. The overall effect on efficiency is mixed: partner choice and surplus control can increase efficiency, but not always, especially if exploiters are present or if association opportunities are limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that mechanisms analogous to punishment (partner choice, surplus appropriation) can increase efficiency (group payoff) in mutualism-like bargaining games, especially when combined. However, the effect is not universally positive: the presence of exploiters and the structure of association opportunities (e.g., empty slots) can lead to reduced efficiency or cyclical dynamics. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling partner choice and surplus control generally increases efficiency, but the effect may be moderated or reversed if exploiters can persist or if association opportunities are limited. The model is adjacent to public goods games, so its predictions should be transferred with caution, but it provides informative indirect evidence about the conditions under which punishment-like mechanisms improve or fail to improve efficiency.

- source: Wang, R. W., & Shi, L. (2010). The evolution of cooperation in asymmetric systems. *Science China-life Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in asymmetric cooperative systems, the net benefit (efficiency) to the recipient depends critically on the intrinsic growth rates of benefit and cost, the strength of density-dependent interference competition among cooperative actors, and the availability of common resources. Stronger density-dependent interference competition among cooperative actors and higher initial relatedness (intrinsic contribution ratio) increase the probability of maintaining cooperation (positive efficiency). However, changes in these parameters or resource saturation can lead to a transition from cooperation to conflict, with net benefit becoming negative. The model also shows that recipient-enforced discrimination (punishing cheaters, rewarding cooperators) can increase initial relatedness and favor cooperation, but does not guarantee global stability. The system can exhibit oscillatory or chaotic dynamics between cooperation and conflict.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how efficiency in public-goods-like asymmetric systems is affected by the number of actors, resource saturation, and density-dependent competition. It suggests that efficiency gains from cooperation are not guaranteed by punishment or sanctions alone, but depend on ecological and structural parameters such as the strength of interference competition and resource availability. For prediction tasks, the model implies that enabling punishment (in the form of recipient-enforced discrimination) can increase efficiency only when it effectively increases initial relatedness and when density-dependent competition among actors is strong enough to prevent resource overexploitation. However, efficiency can decrease or oscillate if these conditions are not met. The findings are most relevant for systems with asymmetric roles and may be less directly transferable to standard symmetric PGG lab games.

- source: Lotem, A., Fishman, M. A., & Stone, L. (2003). From reciprocity to unconditional altruism through signalling benefits. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated cooperation games with individual variation in quality, the addition of signalling benefits to helping can lead to stable unconditional altruism among high-quality individuals. This occurs when the signalling benefit (S) is greater than the cost of helping (C) for high-quality individuals but not for low-quality individuals. Under these conditions, high-quality individuals cooperate unconditionally, while low-quality individuals defect or play tit-for-tat. The result is a higher average payoff (efficiency) for high-quality cooperators, and the overall level of cooperation and efficiency in the population is increased compared to models without signalling benefits. The model also shows that if signalling benefits become too cheap (affordable to all), the correlation between quality and cooperation disappears, undermining the signalling function.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that adding signalling benefits to cooperation games can increase efficiency, especially among high-quality individuals. For prediction tasks, it suggests that in environments where helping can serve as a costly signal (and where the signalling benefit exceeds the cost for some but not all), enabling such signalling will increase the efficiency of cooperation relative to control. The model is adjacent to public goods games and punishment, but the payoff logic and ESS analysis are directly relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes when game design allows for signalling or reputation effects. However, the model does not directly manipulate punishment or public goods game parameters, so its predictive value is strongest for settings where signalling is a plausible mechanism.

- source: Fehr, E., & Schneider, F. (2010). Eyes are on us, but nobody cares: are eye cues relevant for strong reciprocity?. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that subtle, implicit reputation cues (eyespots) do not increase trustees' altruistic behavior in a trust game, nor do they affect trustors' willingness to invest. In contrast, explicit pecuniary reputation incentives—where trustees' past back-transfers are revealed to future trustors—lead to a large and significant increase in both trustees' back-transfers and trustors' investments. This effect is particularly pronounced among individuals with high Machiavellianism scores. The results suggest that explicit, material reputation incentives are powerful drivers of prosocial behavior, while implicit cues without real consequences are ineffective in this context. The findings cast doubt on the hypothesis that most prosocial behavior in anonymous experiments is due to uncontrolled implicit reputation cues.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that explicit, material reputation incentives (a form of positive sanctioning or reward) can substantially increase prosocial behavior and, by implication, group efficiency in trust-based economic games. However, it also shows that implicit cues (such as eyespots) have no such effect. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of explicit reputation or reward mechanisms is a key moderator of efficiency, while subtle cues without real payoff consequences are not. The results are adjacent to public goods games: the trust game is a two-player, sequential, one-shot game repeated with random rematching, not a standard PGG, and there is no punishment mechanism. The findings are most relevant for settings where reputation or reward is at stake, and less so for punishment-based designs. The paper does not provide direct evidence on punishment, but supports the importance of explicit incentive structures for efficiency.

- source: Pacheco, J. M., Santos, F. C., & Chalub, F. A. C. C. (2006). Stern-judging: A simple, successful norm which promotes cooperation under indirect reciprocity. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that, in a multilevel evolutionary model of indirect reciprocity, the stern-judging norm—where helping good individuals or refusing help to bad individuals is rewarded, and the opposite is punished—emerges as the dominant and most robust social norm. This norm leads to maximized average payoffs for groups, indicating high efficiency in sustaining cooperation. The result is robust to different forms of group selection and inter-tribe conflict. The model suggests that simple, reputation-based punishment mechanisms are sufficient to promote and maintain high levels of cooperation and group welfare, even in the presence of errors and migration.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that simple, reputation-based punishment mechanisms (like stern-judging) can robustly increase group efficiency and cooperation in indirect reciprocity settings. While the model is not a public goods game per se, it is adjacent: it involves costly helping, reputation, and punishment via social norms. The findings suggest that, in environments where indirect reciprocity and reputation are salient, enabling punishment (in the form of social norm enforcement) is likely to increase group efficiency relative to no-punishment baselines. However, the model is abstract and does not manipulate most game design dimensions directly, so its predictive value is strongest for settings with similar indirect reciprocity structure and less for standard PGGs.

- source: Morsky, B., Plotkin, J. B., & Akcay, E. (2024). Indirect reciprocity with Bayesian reasoning and biases. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that Bayesian reasoning about errors in action and observation generally reduces the equilibrium level of cooperation and group payoff in indirect reciprocity games, compared to models where agents do not reason about errors. This effect is robust across most social norms (Shunning, Simple Standing, Staying, Stern Judging), but under the Scoring norm, Bayesian reasoning can actually promote cooperation and support a mixed equilibrium with all strategy types. The impact of Bayesian reasoning is strongest when error rates are low. Optimism bias (overestimating the frequency of good individuals) generally undermines cooperation, while mild pessimism bias can sometimes promote it, but strong biases of either type destroy cooperation. The model provides heatmaps and bifurcation diagrams showing how cooperation and payoffs change with error rates and biases. The findings are relevant for predicting how changes in information processing, error rates, and social norms affect group efficiency in reputation-based cooperation games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting efficiency in public-goods-like games with punishment/reward mechanisms based on reputation. It shows that the cognitive sophistication of agents (Bayesian reasoning about errors) can reduce group efficiency, except under certain social norms (Scoring). The results suggest that, in environments where punishment/reward is reputation-based and subject to errors, enabling more sophisticated reasoning may not always increase efficiency. The effect of punishment (via conditional defection) on efficiency is highly sensitive to the social norm, error rates, and the presence of optimism/pessimism bias. For downstream prediction, the paper supports the view that punishment mechanisms relying on reputation can backfire or be less effective if agents reason about errors, unless the norm is simple (Scoring). The findings are most transferable to settings where punishment is implemented as conditional cooperation/defection based on reputation, rather than direct costly punishment.

- source: Graser, C., Fujiwara-Greve, T., García, J., & van Veelen, M. (2025). Repeated games with partner choice. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing the option to leave one's partner in repeated prisoner's dilemma games (i.e., partner choice) leads to a substantial increase in average cooperation rates across a wide range of benefit-to-cost ratios and continuation probabilities. This is because partner choice enables endogenous assortment: cooperators can avoid defectors by leaving, which stabilizes cooperation. Theoretical analysis and agent-based simulations both show that, with partner choice, the population spends more time in cooperative states and escapes from universal defection more quickly. The paper compares strategies that punish by defecting (grim trigger) to those that punish by leaving, and finds that punishing by leaving is more robust to invasion and more likely to support cooperation. Although the main outcome is cooperation rate, higher cooperation implies higher average payoffs, so efficiency is indirectly improved by partner choice.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that enabling partner choice (the ability to leave and rematch) in repeated prisoner's dilemma games increases cooperation and, by implication, group efficiency/payoff. While the setting is not a public goods game per se, the mechanism is closely related, and the results are likely to transfer to PGG-like environments where partner choice is possible. The paper does not study costly punishment or explicit sanctions, but rather 'punishment' by leaving. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling partner choice should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a control with no partner choice, especially in repeated dyadic games. The results are most directly relevant when the game is dyadic, repeated, and partner choice is the main intervention.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., Iwasa, Y., & Nowak, M. A. (2015). Reputation Effects in Public and Private Interactions. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the stability of cooperative strategies in indirect reciprocity depends critically on the observability of private actions (q), the frequency of public interactions (p), and the benefit-to-cost ratio (b/c). Honest cooperation (leading to full group efficiency) is only stable when private actions are sufficiently observable and b/c is high. When private actions are rarely observed or b/c is low, only partial cooperation or defection is stable, resulting in lower group efficiency. The model provides explicit analytical thresholds for these transitions. The presence of public observation increases the incentive for cooperation, but if private actions are unobserved, individuals may defect in private, reducing overall efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on how observability (analogous to information feedback or monitoring in public goods games) and the benefit-to-cost ratio moderate the effect of reputation-based incentives on group efficiency. While not a direct PGG or punishment study, its results suggest that increasing the probability that actions are observed (or reputationally consequential) increases the likelihood of stable, high-efficiency cooperation. In prediction tasks, similar mechanisms (e.g., transparency, monitoring, or reputation updating) should be expected to increase the efficiency gains from reputation or sanctioning systems, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is high. However, if observability is low, the efficiency impact of such systems will be limited.

- source: Brandt, H., & Sigmund, K. (2004). The logic of reprobation: assessment and action rules for indirect reciprocation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost
  findings: The study shows that indirect reciprocity models with assessment rules that distinguish justified from unjustified defections (STANDING) are more effective at sustaining cooperation and suppressing defectors than simple scoring rules, especially in the presence of errors. However, even simple scoring can support cooperation if the benefit-to-cost ratio is high and players have enough interactions. The model demonstrates that the structure of the population, the number of observed interactions, and the error rates in action and perception all influence the stability of cooperation. Analytical results show that, under certain assumptions about the distribution of the number of rounds, stable mixtures of discriminating and non-discriminating altruists can exist, leading to high average payoffs. The findings suggest that costly punishment (refusing to help) can sustain cooperation, but is less robust than non-costly forms (STANDING).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment-like mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation (and thus efficiency) depends on whether punishment is costly, the benefit-to-cost ratio, error rates, and the number of interactions. While the model is not a standard PGG, the parallels are strong: refusing to help is analogous to punishing defectors, and the group payoff is analogous to efficiency. The results imply that costly punishment can increase efficiency, but is less robust than non-costly punishment, and that the details of the assessment and action rules, as well as the game structure (e.g., number of rounds, observation probability), are important moderators. For prediction, the paper supports the view that enabling punishment can increase efficiency, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is high and errors are not too frequent, but the effect is sensitive to the costliness and implementation of punishment.

- source: Aktipis, C. A. (2004). Know when to walk away: contingent movement and the evolution of cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that a simple contingent movement rule ('Walk Away') enables cooperation to evolve and persist in spatial prisoner's dilemma environments, outperforming more cognitively complex strategies like TFT and PAVLOV under many conditions. Walk Away is robust to invasion, can invade populations of defectors at lower initial frequencies than other strategies, and is most successful when movement costs, error rates, and agent density are low. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and outcomes are measured by the evolutionary success of strategies (frequency in the population), not by direct group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that contingent movement (exit) can substitute for more complex memory-based or punishment-based strategies in promoting cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It does not study public goods games or explicit punishment, but shows that contingent movement (exit after defection) can promote cooperation and evolutionary success in spatial prisoner's dilemma environments. The findings suggest that mechanisms allowing agents to avoid defectors (analogous to exit or partner choice) can be highly effective, even without explicit punishment or memory. However, because the model does not report group efficiency or total payoff, and does not manipulate punishment cost or technology, its direct applicability to predicting efficiency in public goods games with punishment is limited. The results are most relevant for understanding the role of partner choice, mobility, and spatial structure as alternatives or complements to punishment.

- source: Romano, A., Saral, A. S., & De Dreu, C. K. W. (2025). Reciprocity evolves more readily in competitive than cooperative socio-ecologies. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: Reciprocal matching strategies, which reward cooperation and punish defection, are more likely to evolve and persist in competitive socio-ecologies where the risk of exploitation is high (e.g., Chicken/Hawk-Dove/Attack-Defend games) than in cooperative ones (Stag Hunt). In cooperative environments, unconditional cooperation dominates, while in competitive environments, anti-reciprocity (mismatching) and defection are more successful. The success of reciprocity increases with the probability of repeated interaction. The paper does not directly report on group efficiency or total payoff, but the evolutionary success of reciprocity implies it is adaptive in competitive settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the effectiveness of punishment (as part of reciprocal strategies) in promoting cooperation depends on the competitiveness of the environment and the risk of exploitation. While it does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes directly, it suggests that punishment (as part of reciprocity) is more likely to be adaptive and prevalent in competitive, high-risk environments. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effect of punishment on efficiency may be more positive in competitive, high-exploitation-risk games (e.g., Chicken, Hawk-Dove) than in cooperative games (e.g., Stag Hunt). However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits quantitative prediction.

- source: Wu, J. H., Számadó, S., Barclay, P., Beersma, B., Cruz, T. D. D., Lo Iacono, S., Nieper, A. S., Peters, K., Przepiorka, W., Tiokhin, L., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2021). Honesty and dishonesty in gossip strategies: a fitness interdependence analysis. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the honesty of gossip is determined by the gossiper's fitness interdependence with the receiver and target, and by the marginal cost/benefit structure of the underlying game between receiver and target. A simple matching rule is proposed: gossipers are honest when their fitness interdependence aligns with the effect of honest gossip, and dishonest when it mismatches. The model is applied to four game types, including a punishment game, but the focus is on the strategic choice of gossip honesty rather than on direct efficiency or payoff outcomes. The paper suggests that honest gossip can support cooperation, but that dishonest gossip can undermine reputation-based cooperation, especially when fitness interdependence is negative or mixed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how information transmission (gossip) can act as a low-cost reputational punishment mechanism in social dilemmas, including punishment games. However, it does not provide empirical or simulated data on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it manipulate or analyze the standard public goods game dimensions. Its main contribution is to clarify when gossip will be honest or dishonest, which can indirectly affect cooperation and efficiency, but the paper does not quantify these effects. For prediction tasks about punishment effects on efficiency in public goods games, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly by highlighting the importance of information accuracy and fitness interdependence in the effectiveness of reputational sanctions.

- source: Watson, R., Morgan, T. J. H., Kendal, R. L., van de Vyver, J., & Kendal, J. (2024). Investigating the effects of social information on spite in an online game. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Participants overwhelmingly chose altruism over spite when given a costly choice to increase or decrease an anonymous partner's payoff. Exposure to social information indicating others had behaved spitefully (vs. altruistically) reduced the degree of altruism, but did not substantially increase spiteful acts. The source of social information (majority vs. most successful) had no effect. The study does not report group efficiency, welfare, or total payoff, and the design is a one-shot, two-player, non-iterated game with a continuous choice from costly spite to costly altruism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar repeated group games. The design is a one-shot, two-player, continuous-choice game where participants can pay to harm or help an anonymous partner, and the main outcome is a behavioral slider, not group efficiency or payoff. There is no repeated interaction, no group context, and no explicit public goods structure. The findings suggest that when both costly spite and costly altruism are available, spite is rare and social information can reduce altruism, but this is not directly transferable to the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Burum, B., Nowak, M. A., & Hoffman, M. (2020). An evolutionary explanation for ineffective altruism. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that people are generally insensitive to the efficacy (impact) of their charitable giving, in contrast to their sensitivity when making decisions for themselves or family. Social rewards (reputation, praise, monetary reward from third parties) are given for the act of giving, not for giving effectively or giving more. This insensitivity is not due to cognitive or emotional limitations, but rather to the evolutionary logic that social rewards are easier to coordinate around observable, well-defined actions (like giving) than around less observable or harder-to-define features (like efficacy or amount). The studies do not use public goods games or punishment, but the reward mechanism is conceptually adjacent to sanctioning. No efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it offers indirect evidence that social incentives (rewards) are most effective when they are based on observable, well-defined actions, and that people are not motivated by efficiency or impact when giving is socially rewarded. The findings suggest that, in environments where social rewards or punishments are based on easily observed behaviors (like contributing at all), these incentives may not improve efficiency if they do not target effective or high-impact contributions. The lack of sensitivity to efficacy in social rewards may limit the effectiveness of punishment or reward mechanisms in increasing group efficiency, unless the mechanisms are designed to be sensitive to impact. The paper is most relevant for understanding the limitations of social incentives in promoting efficient cooperation, rather than for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGGs with punishment.

- source: Geschwind, S., & Lambsdorff, J. G. (2025). Does scarcity induce hostility? An experimental investigation of common-pool resources. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that when scarcity is perceived as human-induced (overexploitation), participants are more likely to destroy each other's resources in the subsequent stage, indicating increased hostility. When scarcity is exogenously imposed (low reproduction), destruction is lower, suggesting that environmental scarcity can foster restraint and cooperation. The destruction mechanism is not a standard punishment stage: destruction is costless, partially hidden (due to random 'bear' destruction), and not directly strategic. The study finds that reciprocity (retaliation for overexploitation) is a key driver of destruction, while inequality aversion plays a limited role. Overharvesting is substantial in the CPR game, leading to welfare losses, but the main focus is on behavioral responses (hostility) rather than efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report efficiency outcomes for a standard PGG with or without punishment, it shows that the presence of a destruction (punishment-like) stage increases hostile behavior when scarcity is human-induced, and decreases it when scarcity is exogenous. The destruction mechanism differs from standard costly punishment: it is costless, partially hidden, and not designed to change future behavior. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms on efficiency may depend on the perceived cause of scarcity and the design of the punishment stage. The paper is most informative for understanding how the framing and cause of scarcity moderate the impact of punishment-like options on group behavior, but does not provide direct data for predicting efficiency changes from enabling punishment in standard PGGs.

- source: Miraghaie, A. M., Pouretemad, H., Villa, A. E. P., Mazaheri, M. A., Khosrowabadi, R., & Lintas, A. (2022). Electrophysiological Markers of Fairness and Selfishness Revealed by a Combination of Dictator and Ultimatum Games. *Frontiers in Systems Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that distinct neural markers (N1, P2, MFN, LPC) are associated with behavioral profiles of fairness and selfishness in economic games. Selfish participants show neural signatures consistent with spiteful punishment (e.g., rejecting low offers in UG, resulting in zero payoffs for both), while fair participants show markers consistent with costly punishment (e.g., rejecting selfish allocations in DG). However, the paper does not report or analyze group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, and does not manipulate or compare the presence/absence of punishment as a treatment. The findings are relevant for understanding the neural basis of punishment and fairness, but do not provide direct evidence on the efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The games studied (DG and UG) are adjacent to PGGs but lack the group structure, repeated interaction, and explicit public goods provision. Punishment in UG is endogenous (rejection of unfair offers) and always results in zero payoff for both, but the study does not compare efficiency with and without punishment, nor does it report group-level payoff outcomes. The main value for prediction is in the mechanistic and neural understanding of individual differences in punishment behavior, not in quantitative efficiency effects.

- source: Xiang, T., Lohrenz, T., & Montague, P. R. (2013). Computational Substrates of Norms and Their Violations during Social Exchange. *Journal of Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that social norm expectations, manipulated via preconditioning to low, medium, or high offers in a repeated ultimatum game, strongly affect rejection rates for identical offers. Subjects preconditioned to low offers (unfair) are less likely to reject medium offers than those preconditioned to high offers (hyper-fair), even though the offers are objectively the same. Subjective feelings about offers and neural responses (in regions such as vmPFC, striatum, and anterior insula) track these norm prediction errors. The results support a model in which norm violations, rather than simple payoff inequality, drive punishment behavior (rejection), and that these processes are reflected in both behavior and brain activity. However, the study does not report on group efficiency or total payoff, and the outcome of interest is not efficiency but rather the psychological and neural mechanisms of norm-based punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction of punishment effects in public-goods-like environments, specifically regarding how expectations and norm adaptation modulate punishment (rejection) behavior. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment (here, costly rejection) on behavior is highly sensitive to context and prior expectations, which may inform models of punishment in other social dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is best used as contextual or mechanistic evidence about the psychological drivers of punishment, not as a quantitative source for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Samu, F., & Takács, K. (2021). Evaluating mechanisms that could support credible reputations and cooperation: cross-checking and social bonding. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment found that allowing for cross-checking of gossip or opportunities for social bonding did not significantly improve cooperation rates compared to a control. High competition for reputation increased cooperation, but overall cooperation declined over time in all conditions. Gossip was generally honest but became more negative about rivals under high competition. Reputation scores and gossip valence influenced conditional cooperation, but the mechanisms tested did not create a reliable path to sustained high cooperation. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of communication, reputation, and competition on cooperation in repeated dyadic games, but does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. The design is adjacent to PGGs and does not include explicit costly punishment or group-level payoff analysis. Findings suggest that mechanisms to improve the credibility of social information (cross-checking, social bonding) may not be sufficient to sustain cooperation, and that competition for reputation can increase cooperation rates. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper offers only contextual or indirect guidance.

- source: Bednarik, P., Fehl, K., & Semmann, D. (2014). Costs for switching partners reduce network dynamics but not cooperative behaviour. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that introducing costs for forming new links in dynamic networks reduces the rate at which participants break links (i.e., switch partners). However, cooperation rates in the repeated Prisoner's Dilemma remain high across all dynamic treatments, regardless of the cost level, and are significantly higher than in static networks where no partner switching is allowed. The ability to break links—even if rarely used—appears to be enough to maintain high cooperation. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of these dynamics on efficiency cannot be directly determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games with dynamic partner networks, the introduction of costs for switching partners reduces network dynamism but does not reduce cooperation rates. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment or partner-switching mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings suggest that the mere threat of partner switching can sustain cooperation, but the efficiency implications (e.g., total group payoff relative to maximum possible) remain unaddressed.

- source: Rilling, J. K., Goldsmith, D. R., Glenn, A. L., Jairam, M. R., Elfenbein, H. A., Dagenais, J. E., Murdock, C. D., & Pagnoni, G. (2008). The neural correlates of the affective response to unreciprocated cooperation. *Neuropsychologia*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that unreciprocated cooperation (being defected on after cooperating) in the iterated Prisoner's Dilemma elicits strong negative emotions and increased neural activity in regions associated with aversive and social pain (anterior insula, hippocampus, amygdala). These neural responses are stronger when the unreciprocated cooperation follows a cooperative move by the subject, suggesting sensitivity to perceived partner intent. Functional connectivity between the anterior insula and lateral orbitofrontal cortex predicts whether the subject will defect in the next round. The paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods or Prisoner's Dilemma games. It does, however, provide evidence that unreciprocated cooperation (partner defection) triggers strong negative affect and neural responses that bias subjects toward defection in subsequent rounds. This suggests a psychological mechanism by which cooperation can unravel in the absence of punishment or sanctioning. For the downstream prediction task, this paper supports the idea that, without punishment, negative experiences with defectors can lead to reduced cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative or comparative data on efficiency or payoff, nor does it test the effect of introducing punishment.

- source: Manapat, M. L., Rand, D. G., Pawlowitsch, C., & Nowak, M. A. (2012). Stochastic evolutionary dynamics resolve the Traveler's Dilemma. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in the Traveler's Dilemma, evolutionary dynamics favor higher (more cooperative) claims when the reward/penalty R is small relative to the maximum claim M, and lower (less cooperative) claims when R is large. The model reproduces the empirical finding that average claims decrease as R increases. The analysis is focused on the distribution of claims, not on group efficiency or total payoff, and the Traveler's Dilemma is structurally different from public goods games: it is a two-player, competitive undercutting game with a specific bonus/penalty mechanism, not a multi-player contribution game. The findings are relevant for understanding how incentive structures (like R) shape cooperative behavior, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency or punishment effects in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, adjacent evidence for the prediction task. It shows that in a two-player social dilemma with a reward/penalty for undercutting (Traveler's Dilemma), increasing the size of the penalty/reward (R) leads to less cooperative behavior (lower claims). However, the game structure is not a public goods game, and the outcomes are not group efficiency or total payoff, but rather the average claim. There is no direct manipulation or analysis of punishment in a PGG or direct efficiency outcomes. Thus, the paper may inform general intuitions about how incentive structures affect cooperation, but should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.

- source: West, S. A., Gardner, A., Shuker, D. M., Reynolds, T., Burton-Chellow, M., Sykes, E. M., Guinnee, M. A., & Griffin, A. S. (2006). Cooperation and the scale of competition in humans. *Current Biology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that human participants cooperate more frequently in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games when rewards are based on global competition (top scorers in the whole class) than when rewards are based on local competition (top scorer in each small group). This effect is robust across individuals, groups, and classes. The experiment does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and the main outcome is the proportion of cooperative choices, not efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the scale of competition (local vs global) can strongly affect cooperation rates in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games, which are adjacent to public goods games. However, it does not manipulate or include punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Therefore, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, but it does highlight the importance of competitive structure as a moderator of cooperation. The findings suggest that contextual factors like competition scale may interact with other mechanisms (such as punishment) in affecting cooperation, but no direct evidence is provided for punishment or efficiency outcomes.

- source: Müller-Leinss, J. M., Enzi, B., Flasbeck, V., & Brüne, M. (2018). Retaliation or selfishness? An rTMS investigation of the role of the dorsolateral prefrontal cortex in prosocial motives. *Social Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: Inhibiting the right DLPFC via rTMS increases both retaliation against previously unfair opponents and selfish behavior (less fairness) towards previously fair opponents in a Dictator Game following an Ultimatum Game. The manipulation did not affect rejection rates in the UG. The findings suggest the right DLPFC is involved in controlling selfish motives, but the design does not distinguish between retaliation and pure selfishness. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and is not a public goods game or direct punishment paradigm.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is adjacent: it uses sequential UG and DG with role reversal and rTMS manipulation, focusing on individual-level retaliation/selfishness rather than group-level cooperation or efficiency. There is no manipulation of punishment institutions, costs, or group payoff structure as in PGGs. The main outcomes are behavioral (fair/unfair offers), not efficiency or payoff. Thus, the paper is not directly informative for the prediction task, except as context for the neural basis of retaliation/selfishness in economic games.

- source: Townsend, C., Aktipis, A., Balliet, D., & Cronk, L. (2020). Generosity among the Ik of Uganda. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that Ik participants in unframed Dictator Games give on average 23.3% of their endowment, which is within the expected range for similar societies. Framing the game to invoke both need and supernatural punishment significantly increases giving (to 43.2%), while either frame alone does not have a significant effect. The results challenge the claim that the Ik are uniquely selfish and show that cultural norms and beliefs can increase generosity. However, the study does not use a public goods game, does not include punishment as a costly sanctioning option, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is a Dictator Game, not a PGG or its variant, and the 'punishment' manipulation is a framing invoking supernatural punishment, not a real, costly, player-administered sanction. The outcome is individual giving, not group efficiency. The findings may be weakly informative for understanding how cultural beliefs about punishment can increase generosity, but they do not provide evidence about the efficiency effects of actual punishment mechanisms in PGG-like environments.

- source: Tanaka, H., Ohtsuki, H., & Ohtsubo, Y. (2016). The price of being seen to be just: an intention signalling strategy for indirect reciprocity. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that participants in a dyadic donation game use a costly signalling option (abandoning a resource) to manage their reputation, especially after justified defection or implementation error. Others interpret this signal as a sign of cooperative intent and are more likely to cooperate with signallers. The signalling option allows players to maintain a good reputation without requiring second-order information. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that such signalling can stabilize cooperation in indirect reciprocity settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The experimental design is a dyadic donation game, not a multi-player PGG, and the 'punishment' is a costly self-imposed signal (abandoning a resource) rather than a punishment directed at others. The main outcomes are behavioral (signalling and cooperation rates), not efficiency or payoff. However, the findings suggest that providing players with a costly signalling option can help maintain cooperation in the absence of second-order information, which may be relevant for understanding mechanisms that stabilize cooperation in related environments. The results do not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Rankin, D. J., & Eggimann, F. (2009). The evolution of judgement bias in indirect reciprocity. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that judgement bias—weighting bad acts more heavily than good acts in reputation updating—can evolve in indirect reciprocity models, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is low. Under 'standing' reputation rules, negative judgement bias is favored over a wider range of parameters than under 'scoring.' Allowing judgement bias to evolve increases the level of cooperation in the population. The model does not include explicit costly punishment, but negative judgement bias acts as a form of social sanctioning by reducing the likelihood of future help to defectors. The model does not report group efficiency or total payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it suggests that mechanisms that increase the reputational cost of defection (such as negative judgement bias) can promote cooperation, especially when the cost of cooperation is high. The findings are most relevant for settings where reputation-based indirect reciprocity is the main mechanism, rather than explicit costly punishment. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Yuan, H. R., Meng, X. Z., & Alzahrani, A. K. (2023). Analysis of dynamic evolution process of the N-player division of labor game model. *Mathematical Modelling of Natural Phenomena*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that high toxicity rates (especially against cheaters), high toxin levels, and smaller group sizes promote the disappearance of cheaters and the persistence of cooperation (cooperators and/or toxin producers). The system exhibits bistability: depending on initial conditions and parameters, the population may converge to all cooperators, coexistence of cooperators and toxin producers, or coexistence of cooperators and cheaters. The interior equilibrium (all three strategies coexisting) is always unstable. Bifurcation analysis reveals thresholds in absorption rates and toxin parameters that determine which strategies persist. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of each strategy at equilibrium, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how costly sanctioning (here, toxin production) can suppress free riding and promote cooperation in N-player games. However, the model is not a standard public goods game with explicit peer punishment, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome. The findings suggest that increasing the effectiveness or prevalence of costly sanctions (analogous to punishment) can reduce free riding and increase the fraction of task-performers, especially in smaller groups and with higher sanction effectiveness. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that punishment-like mechanisms can increase cooperation, but does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency changes or direct mapping to standard PGG dimensions.

- source: Bruner, J. P., & Smead, R. (2022). Tag-based spite with correlated interactions. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of observable tags and the possibility of conditioning behavior on tags (e.g., only harming out-group members) fundamentally changes the evolutionary dynamics of costly harming (spite) in the Prisoner's Delight game. Tags make pure (unconditional) spite less likely to evolve even when anti-correlation would otherwise favor it, but make conditional (tag-based) spite more likely in some regimes. Several stable equilibria are identified, including cooperative polymorphisms (mostly cooperation, little spite), pure spite, and a novel 'treacherous polymorphism' where spite is directed within groups. The presence of tags generally reduces the prevalence of widespread spiteful behavior, even under conditions that would otherwise favor it. The results are framed in terms of evolutionary stability and behavioral frequencies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. The model is a two-player game (Prisoner's Delight) with costly harming, not a multi-player public goods game. The main outcomes are the evolutionary frequencies of behavioral types, not group efficiency or total payoff. However, the analysis is relevant for understanding how the structure of punishment-like mechanisms (costly harming, conditional on observable tags) and population structure (correlated/anti-correlated interactions) can affect the prevalence of cooperation and spite. The findings suggest that mechanisms allowing for conditional punishment (e.g., based on group identity) can both promote and suppress harmful behavior, depending on the context. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides indirect theoretical context about how punishment-like behaviors may interact with population structure and signaling, but does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence for efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Koike, S., Nakamaru, M., & Tsujimoto, M. (2010). Evolution of cooperation in rotating indivisible goods game. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a rotating indivisible goods game (a model of ROSCAs), cooperation collapses unless both a peer selection rule (based on reputation) and a forfeiture rule (excluding non-contributors from receiving goods) are present. Peer selection alone or forfeiture alone is insufficient. The model tracks the evolution of cooperation probabilities before and after receiving goods, showing that only the combination of both rules leads to sustained high cooperation. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it shows that in a public-goods-adjacent environment with indivisible goods, the presence of both peer selection (reputation-based group formation) and a forfeiture rule (exclusion of non-contributors) is necessary to sustain high cooperation rates. For prediction, this suggests that punishment-like mechanisms (here, exclusion and peer selection) may only be effective in combination, and that the structure of the game (indivisible goods, rotation, exclusion) is critical. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits its direct applicability to efficiency prediction.

- source: Chen, M. K., & Hauser, M. (2005). Modeling reciprocation and cooperation in primates: evidence for a punishing strategy. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that tamarins' cooperative behavior in repeated food-exchange games is best described by 'punish-2' and 'punish-3' strategies, which are more punishing than standard tit-for-tat. These strategies require two or more consecutive cooperative acts to restart cooperation after a breakdown, leading to stable but sometimes brittle cooperation. The analysis uses maximum-likelihood fitting of a suite of behavioral models, showing that these punishing strategies account for over 90% of the posterior model weight. The paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes, and all results are based on behavioral patterns (pulling rates and model fits).
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. However, it does provide indirect evidence that more punishing strategies (requiring multiple acts of cooperation to restore cooperation) can stabilize cooperation in repeated dyadic interactions. The findings may inform mechanistic expectations about how punishment-like strategies affect cooperation dynamics, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency changes due to punishment in PGGs. The game is not a standard PGG, and the outcomes are not payoff-based.

- source: Lu, S. N., & Wang, Y. (2024). Adjustment of link weights based on tolerance promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Biosystems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that allowing individuals to reduce interaction intensity with neighbors who defect beyond a tolerance threshold increases the frequency of cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. However, if the tolerance threshold is too high (i.e., individuals are too forgiving), cooperation is undermined. The effect is stronger when more individuals in the population are 'environmental adaptors' who adjust their interaction intensity. The main outcome is the equilibrium frequency of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency. The findings are robust to changes in network size and initial cooperator fraction.
