  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about mechanisms that may promote cooperation in repeated social dilemmas, specifically through adaptive partner selection or ostracism based on tolerance for defection. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, and the game is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game. The results suggest that mechanisms allowing individuals to reduce interaction with defectors (after a tolerance threshold) can increase cooperation rates, but excessive tolerance undermines this effect. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper is only weakly informative, as it does not analyze punishment costs, efficiency, or public goods structures.

- source: Page, K. M., Nowak, M. A., & Sigmund, K. (2000). The spatial ultimatum game. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that spatial structure in the ultimatum game leads to the evolution of fairer offers and higher acceptance thresholds compared to non-spatial (random encounter) settings. In well-mixed populations, average offers and acceptance levels are low, approaching zero as mutation error decreases. In spatially structured populations (1D and 2D lattices), average offers and acceptance levels are much higher, approaching a fair split in some cases. The paper interprets rejection of low offers as a punishment mechanism that, when combined with spatial structure, supports the evolution of fairness. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to payoff-based prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that spatial structure and local competition can support the evolution of fairer, more cooperative behavior in bargaining games where punishment is endogenous (via rejection). However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of spatial structure and local interactions for the evolution of fairness and the effectiveness of endogenous punishment mechanisms.

- source: Wang, R. W., He, J. Z., Wang, Y. Q., Shi, L., & Li, Y. T. (2010). Asymmetric interaction will facilitate the evolution of cooperation. *Science China-life Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper develops a theoretical model of the asymmetric Hawk-Dove game, showing that the frequency of cooperation increases with greater asymmetry between players (i.e., when one player is much stronger or more dominant than the other). The model also shows that a lower cost-to-benefit ratio of cooperation increases cooperation frequency. The discussion links these findings to real-world systems where dominant individuals can punish non-cooperators, suggesting that credible punishment and asymmetry can stabilize cooperation. However, the paper does not provide direct predictions or data on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the probability of cooperation and conflict.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect theoretical support for the idea that asymmetry and credible punishment can increase cooperation rates in social dilemmas, but it does not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff. Its relevance to predicting efficiency in public goods games with punishment is limited, as it analyzes a two-player Hawk-Dove game rather than a multi-player public goods game, and focuses on cooperation frequency rather than efficiency. The discussion of punishment is conceptual, not formalized as a game design dimension. Thus, while the paper suggests that punishment and asymmetry can facilitate cooperation, it does not provide direct evidence for the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.

- source: Glowacki, L., & von Rueden, C. (2015). Leadership solves collective action problems in small-scale societies. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in both the Tsimane and Nyangatom societies, leaders tend to have more 'capital' (age, strength, social connections), and that leadership is more likely to become institutionalized in larger, denser, or more conflict-prone communities. Leadership is associated with improved group coordination and conflict mediation, and leaders often incur higher costs (e.g., risk in battle, time spent mediating). However, there is no direct evidence that leaders or leadership increase group efficiency or total payoffs in a way that maps onto public goods game outcomes. The paper notes that leader-based monitoring and sanctioning may reduce free-riding and improve the efficiency of reward or punishment allocation, but this is discussed in general terms and not measured as a primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is informative for understanding the social and ecological conditions under which leadership, monitoring, and sanctioning emerge to solve collective action problems. It provides indirect support for the idea that leadership (and by extension, centralized punishment or monitoring) can improve group coordination and reduce free-riding, especially as group size and conflict increase. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games, nor does it report group payoff or efficiency outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on the efficiency impact of punishment in controlled game environments, this paper offers only contextual and theoretical guidance, not direct empirical input.

- source: Powers, S. T., van Schaik, C. P., & Lehmann, L. (2016). How institutions shaped the last major evolutionary transition to large-scale human societies. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper proposes that the ability to create and enforce institutional rules, including sanctioning mechanisms, is a key driver of large-scale human cooperation and division of labor. Institutions can transform the default equilibrium of social dilemmas (such as public goods games) to more cooperative, higher-payoff outcomes by enabling coordination, information sharing, and self-enforcing sanctioning. Theoretical arguments and historical/anthropological examples are used to support the claim that institutions can increase group efficiency and welfare, but the paper does not provide new empirical evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency changes due to punishment or institutional design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual support for the idea that enabling punishment or sanctioning mechanisms within an institutional framework can increase efficiency and group payoffs in public-goods-like environments, especially as group size increases. However, it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence that can be directly used to predict efficiency changes as a function of game design dimensions or control efficiency. Its value for prediction is in supporting the plausibility and mechanism of positive effects of institutions (including punishment) on efficiency, but it does not inform the magnitude or specific moderators of these effects.

- source: Gardner, A., West, S. A., & Barton, N. H. (2007). The relation between multilocus population genetics and social evolution theory. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper demonstrates that multilocus population genetics provides a general framework for modeling the evolution of social behaviors, including cooperation and punishment. In a simple two-locus model (one for cooperation, one for punishment), it finds that cooperation and punishment cannot invade unless cooperation is already favored by relatedness (i.e., punishment does not expand the parameter space for cooperation). Moreover, increased relatedness directly selects against punishment, as punishment is mutually costly. The only benefit to punishment comes from its association with cooperation, but this is insufficient to overcome the direct costs. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on evolutionary stability and gene frequency change.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are relevant for understanding the evolutionary logic of cooperation and punishment, and suggest that costly punishment may not increase cooperation or group benefit except under restrictive conditions. However, because it does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it should not be used as a primary source for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in experimental public goods games. It may be useful for mechanistic or theoretical context, especially regarding the evolutionary logic of punishment and cooperation.

- source: Pandula, N., Akcay, E., & Morsky, B. (2024). Indirect reciprocity with abductive reasoning. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that abductive reasoning in indirect reciprocity models can, under certain social norms and low error rates, promote higher levels of cooperation than non-reasoning models. For the Scoring norm, abductive reasoning enables stable cooperative states that are not possible without reasoning. For other norms (Simple Standing, Staying, Stern Judging), abductive reasoning can outperform or underperform non-reasoning depending on the error rates and the degree of confirmation/falsification bias. Under the Shunning norm, abductive reasoning leads to only defection. The model is more robust to errors in observation/assessment than to errors in action. The main reported outcomes are the equilibrium frequencies of cooperative strategies and intended cooperation rates, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how reasoning about errors and reputation assignment can affect the evolution of cooperation in indirect reciprocity settings. While it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it suggests that the cognitive process for assigning reputations (abductive reasoning) and the social norm in use can be critical moderators of cooperation rates. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in PGG-like games with punishment, this paper is only adjacent: it does not model explicit PGGs, group-level payoffs, or direct costly punishment, but it does highlight the importance of error handling and norm choice for sustaining cooperation. Its findings may inform mechanistic priors about the robustness of cooperation to errors and the role of cognitive biases, but cannot be used for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Taylor, C., & Nowak, M. A. (2007). Transforming the dilemma. *Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper provides a unified framework for understanding how different mechanisms (direct reciprocity, indirect reciprocity, kin selection, group selection, network reciprocity) transform the payoff structure of the Prisoner's Dilemma to allow for the evolution of cooperation. For each mechanism, it derives the threshold conditions under which cooperation can be stable or invade. The analysis is mathematical and does not include empirical or simulation results. Punishment is discussed only as an extension, not as a primary focus, and is said to be subsumed under direct or indirect reciprocity. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment on these outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It is a theoretical synthesis of mechanisms for cooperation in the Prisoner's Dilemma, with only a brief, conceptual discussion of punishment. The paper does not analyze or model the effect of punishment on group payoff, efficiency, or related outcomes, and does not provide parameterized results for game design dimensions relevant to the prediction task. Its main value is in clarifying that punishment is not a separate mechanism but an extension of reciprocity, and that efficiency or payoff outcomes are not directly addressed.

- source: West, S. A., Griffin, A. S., & Gardner, A. (2007). Social semantics: altruism, cooperation, mutualism, strong reciprocity and group selection. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that much confusion in the literature on cooperation, altruism, and punishment arises from inconsistent or ambiguous use of key terms. It clarifies that in many models (including public goods games), behaviors labeled as 'altruistic' or 'strong reciprocity' may actually be mutually beneficial, providing direct fitness benefits to the actor as well as to the group. Punishment, often called 'altruistic punishment,' can also be selfish or spiteful, depending on the context. The paper argues that clear definitions and careful distinction between proximate and ultimate explanations are essential for progress in the field. It also notes that the group selection and kin selection frameworks are mathematically equivalent in most cases relevant to cooperation and public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it offers important theoretical context: it cautions that the effect of punishment on cooperation and efficiency depends on the underlying fitness consequences and the structure of the game. The paper suggests that punishment can increase cooperation and group benefit if it provides a direct or indirect fitness advantage, but the terminology used in the literature may obscure whether the effect is truly altruistic or mutually beneficial. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the need for precise definitions of efficiency and careful mapping of model assumptions to experimental designs. It also highlights that the effect of punishment may depend on whether it is costly, whether it increases group survival or productivity, and how benefits are distributed.

- source: Santos, F. P., Pacheco, J. M., & Santos, F. C. (2021). The complexity of human cooperation under indirect reciprocity. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper reviews the literature on indirect reciprocity, emphasizing how the complexity of social norms and strategies affects the evolution and maintenance of cooperation. It synthesizes theoretical and computational results showing that: (1) more complex social norms can, in principle, sustain higher cooperation, but are harder for individuals to implement; (2) the costliness of reputation spreading, the observability of interactions, and empathy are key moderators of cooperation under indirect reciprocity; (3) when complexity costs are introduced, simpler norms may outperform more complex ones in sustaining cooperation. The paper provides a proof-of-concept simulation showing that cooperation rates under different social norms decline as the cost of implementing complex strategies increases. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or direct welfare outcomes, and does not manipulate or analyze punishment in a public goods context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not report or synthesize results on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, nor does it manipulate punishment or sanctions. Its main relevance is in highlighting that the complexity of social norms and the cognitive costs of implementing strategies can moderate cooperation rates in reputation-based systems. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff outcomes in PGG-like games with punishment, this paper provides only indirect, contextual background, not quantitative or directly transferable evidence.

- source: McNally, L., & Jackson, A. L. (2013). Cooperation creates selection for tactical deception. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that conditional cooperation mechanisms (including punishment and reward) can select for the evolution of tactical deception, as deceivers can exploit conditional cooperators by hiding their defection. Theoretical results show that, under certain conditions (notably negative frequency dependence in deception detection), cooperation and deception can coexist at equilibrium. Comparative analysis in primates supports the prediction that more cooperative species exhibit more tactical deception. However, the paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, nor does it experimentally manipulate punishment or public goods game parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its theoretical model is based on the iterated prisoner's dilemma and focuses on the evolutionary dynamics of deception and cooperation, not on payoff or efficiency outcomes. While it discusses punishment as one of several conditional cooperation mechanisms, it does not analyze the quantitative impact of punishment on group efficiency or payoff. Therefore, it offers only indirect, contextual support for the idea that enforcement mechanisms can have complex evolutionary consequences, but it does not inform the prediction task regarding efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.

- source: Wismer, S., Pinto, A. I., Vail, A. L., Grutter, A. S., & Bshary, R. (2014). Variation in Cleaner Wrasse Cooperation and Cognition: Influence of the Developmental Environment?. *Ethology*.

Punishment is argued to be essential for maintaining cooperation and enforcing social norms in humans, and psychological spite is seen as a byproduct of selection for punishment. The paper reviews animal experiments (e.g., with jays, tamarins, chimpanzees) and human studies (including children), showing that only humans reliably exhibit the full suite of behaviors required for sustained reciprocity and norm-enforcing punishment.

The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not manipulate punishment as a treatment variable. This effect is strongest for offers that are not minimal or maximal, and is associated with increased activation in brain regions related to mentalizing and reward. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment mechanisms. The findings are relevant for understanding how competition changes bargaining behavior, but not for predicting efficiency in public goods games with or without punishment.

- source: Singh, M., & Garfield, Z. H. (2022). Evidence for third-party mediation but not punishment in Mentawai justice. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that, in the Mentawai context, third-party punishment is absent: no cases were found where third parties (outside the victim, their close kin, or spouse) directly punished or demanded penalties from transgressors. Indirect or second-order sanctions by third parties are also not observed. Instead, penalties (tulou) are negotiated and paid primarily to restore dyadic cooperation, with kinship playing a major role in the likelihood and size of payments. Mediation by third parties (especially shamans and elders) is common, particularly in more severe or between-clan disputes, but the mediator's role is to facilitate negotiation, not to enforce punishment. The study suggests that, in this small-scale society, institutionalized penalties serve to repair relationships rather than to enforce group-level norms through punishment. The findings are based on retrospective interviews and ethnographic observation of 302 reliable cases of transgression and response.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The studied context is not a PGG or close variant, and the outcomes are not efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that, in small-scale, kin-based societies, third-party punishment is rare or absent, and penalties serve to restore dyadic cooperation rather than to enforce group norms or increase group efficiency. If extrapolating to PGG-like settings, the results would suggest that the presence or absence of third-party punishment may depend on the social structure and the nature of cooperation (private vs. public goods), but the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency outcomes. Thus, it is of limited use for the downstream prediction task, except as a caution that punishment mechanisms may not always function as assumed in small-scale, kin-based societies.

  findings: The paper finds that among the leading eight indirect reciprocity norms, only some (notably Simple Standing and L1) are stable against small errors in reputation, meaning that cooperation can recover after perturbations. This stability is linked to how the norm treats justified punishment and cooperation with ill-reputed players. The analysis is entirely theoretical and does not simulate or report group efficiency, payoff, or welfare. The findings are about the mathematical stability of cooperation, not about payoffs or efficiency in a public goods or donation game.

- source: Raihani, N. J. (2014). Hidden altruism in a real-world setting. *Biology Letters*.

  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak

  findings: The main empirical finding is that donors to an online fundraising platform are more likely to conceal their identity when making donations that are either much lower or much higher than the norm for a given appeal. The paper interprets this as evidence that individuals may hide norm-violating cooperative behavior (especially high donations) to avoid social costs such as punishment or ostracism, as has been observed in public goods game experiments. However, the study does not directly measure punishment, sanctions, or efficiency, and the context is real-world charitable giving rather than a controlled public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence that social punishment or ostracism can target both low and high contributors in cooperative settings, which may limit the extent of visible over-contribution. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment mechanisms on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. For the prediction task, this paper suggests that social costs for norm-violating contributions (including over-contribution) may moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency outcomes. Its main value is in highlighting the potential for negative social responses to both under- and over-contribution.

- source: André, J. B., & Morin, O. (2011). Questioning the cultural evolution of altruism. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that models of cultural evolution can, in principle, allow for the spread of biologically altruistic behaviors (including costly punishment) among nonkin, but only because of maladaptive features of social learning such as conformist or payoff-biased imitation. These mechanisms do not change the fundamental selective pressures on altruism; rather, they introduce constraints or errors that allow maladaptive behaviors to persist. The paper critiques the literature for sometimes conflating mutualistic cooperation (which can be adaptive) with genuine altruism (which is maladaptive by definition), and for overstating the explanatory power of cultural group selection. The authors argue that the prevalence of genuine altruism in human societies is likely limited and always reflects some form of maladaptation, whether cognitive or cultural.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence relevant to predicting the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games or their variants. It does, however, offer theoretical caution: the presence of punishment or helping behaviors in cultural evolutionary models does not guarantee group-level efficiency or welfare gains, as these behaviors may be maladaptive and persist only due to constraints in learning or imitation. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the mere presence of punishment mechanisms (especially when costly) does not ensure improved efficiency, and that claims about the benefits of punishment should be scrutinized for underlying assumptions about adaptation and learning. However, the paper does not analyze or report efficiency, payoff, or welfare outcomes, so its direct value for quantitative prediction is limited.

  findings: The paper argues that cooperation and helping are underpinned by distinct psychological mechanisms, with parental care mechanisms playing a key role in both asymmetrical helping and in supporting stable cooperation among autonomous agents (such as humans). It suggests that mechanisms for punishment, aggression, and power interact with care-based mechanisms to regulate cooperation and prevent exploitation. The paper critiques the abstraction of game-theoretical models and calls for more mechanistic, multi-agent modeling approaches. While it references public-goods-game-like environments (e.g., the prisoner's dilemma), it does not report empirical findings or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence to inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its value for the prediction task is limited to theoretical insights about the psychological mechanisms that may underlie cooperation, helping, and punishment. It may be useful for contextualizing why certain design features (e.g., opportunities for punishment, cues of vulnerability, or mechanisms for care) could influence cooperation, but it does not provide data or direct evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes based on game design dimensions.

- source: Isern-Mas, C., & Gomila, A. (2022). A Second-Personal Approach to the Evolution of Morality. *Biological Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that moral motivation evolved in two forms: partial (toward close others) and impartial (toward the group or community at large). It proposes that social norms and sanctions (including third-party punishment and internalized sanctions such as guilt and shame) emerged at the community level and were later internalized, giving rise to the feeling of moral obligation. The authors critique and build on the accounts of Tomasello and Darwall, emphasizing the group-level origins of normativity and the psychological mechanisms that support norm compliance. The paper does not address efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it present experimental or empirical evidence.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its relevance is limited to theoretical insights about the evolutionary function of sanctions and norm internalization in supporting norm compliance and cooperation. It does not address game design dimensions, payoff structures, or efficiency outcomes, and thus should not be weighted heavily in quantitative prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff. However, it may provide useful conceptual background on why sanctions (including internalized ones) can support norm compliance.

- source: Shimura, H., & Nakamaru, M. (2018). Large group size promotes the evolution of cooperation in the mutual-aid game. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in the mutual-aid game (an 'all for one' group helping structure), larger group size and more repeated interactions (rounds) both promote the evolution and stability of cooperation, as measured by the expected payoff of conditional cooperators relative to defectors. This is in contrast to the standard public goods game, where larger groups typically undermine cooperation. The effect is robust to implementation and perception errors, and is strongest under certain reputation assessment rules. The paper does not include punishment or costly sanctioning mechanisms; all incentives are mediated by reputation and conditional cooperation. The findings are directly about efficiency (payoff) outcomes, not just cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in mutual-aid games with indirect reciprocity and reputation-based conditional cooperation (but no explicit punishment), increasing group size and number of rounds both increase the likelihood that cooperation (and thus high efficiency) will evolve and persist. The results are not directly transferable to standard public goods games with punishment, but they suggest that the structure of the game (e.g., 'all for one' vs 'all for all') and the presence of reputation-based mechanisms can strongly moderate the effect of group size and repetition on efficiency. Since punishment is not present, the paper does not inform the effect of enabling punishment per se, but it does provide a baseline for efficiency in a related class of games without punishment.

- source: Roberts, G. (2020). Honest signaling of cooperative intentions. *Behavioral Ecology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that costly signaling of cooperative intent can evolve as an honest signal when the long-term benefits of repeated cooperation outweigh the costs of signaling. In such environments, agents who signal are more likely to be chosen as partners, and those who are chosen are more likely to cooperate, leading to higher payoffs for both signalers and choosers. The model predicts that efficiency (total payoff) is maximized when signaling costs are moderate and the number of repeated interactions is high. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning, and is based on dyadic rather than group interactions, but the findings are relevant for understanding how reputation mechanisms can support efficiency in social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that mechanisms allowing individuals to signal cooperative intent and choose partners based on such signals can increase efficiency in repeated social dilemmas. While not a public goods game per se, the model is closely related and suggests that, in environments where reputation-building is possible and partner choice is allowed, efficiency will be higher than in environments without these mechanisms. The absence of punishment in the model means it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does highlight the importance of costly signaling and partner choice as alternative or complementary mechanisms. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling reputation-building and partner choice (analogous to some forms of communication or signaling in PGGs) can increase efficiency, especially when the cost of signaling is not prohibitive and the benefits of cooperation are high.

- source: Szabó, G., Szolnoki, A., & Czakó, L. (2013). Coexistence of fraternity and egoism for spatial social dilemmas. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that allowing for fraternal (other-regarding) player types in spatial social dilemmas leads to the coexistence of egoist and fraternal strategies, often in spatially ordered patterns. The presence of fraternal players reduces the parameter region where only defectors survive, thus supporting higher levels of cooperation and higher average payoffs. The model demonstrates that spatial structure and personality diversity can promote cooperation and mitigate the tragedy of the commons, even without explicit punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms introducing other-regarding preferences (such as fraternity) can increase average group payoff and reduce the prevalence of inefficient, all-defect outcomes in spatial social dilemmas. However, the model is not a public goods game and does not include punishment or reward treatments. The findings suggest that, in environments where player types or preferences can vary, the introduction of pro-social types can have a positive effect on efficiency, analogous to the effect of some institutional interventions. For direct prediction of punishment effects in PGGs, this paper is only weakly informative, but it supports the general principle that mechanisms reducing pure self-interest can improve efficiency.

  findings: The main finding is that for traits inherited both genetically and culturally, cultural selection can drive the evolution of a trait even when genetic selection opposes it, provided the variance in culture is high relative to genetic variance. The model is applied to altruism, showing that cultural selection can favor the spread of altruistic behaviors even when they are genetically maladaptive. The framework also demonstrates that cultural transmission can modify the effect of genetic selection itself. The paper does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency in public goods games, nor does it report empirical or simulated outcomes.

Its main relevance is in highlighting that cultural mechanisms (such as social learning) can enable the spread of cooperative or altruistic behaviors even when they are costly in terms of genetic fitness, especially when cultural variance is high.   findings: The meta-analysis finds that making prosocial actions observable increases prosocial behavior (r = 0.141), with stronger effects in repeated interactions, social dilemmas (including public goods games), and when decisions have consequences for the actor. The effect is also stronger in laboratory settings and with passive observers. However, the outcome is always a behavioral measure (e.g., contribution, donation, volunteering) or intention, not efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or the effect of punishment, this paper is only weakly informative, suggesting that increasing observability may increase contributions, which could in turn affect efficiency, but without direct evidence.   findings: The study demonstrates that certain LLMs (notably text-davinci-003 and GPT-4) generate completions that simulate both payoff-maximizing behavior in non-social tasks and altruistic sharing in dictator game scenarios. The simulated sharing rates for text-davinci-003 are close to those observed in human dictator game experiments, while less advanced models do not consistently maximize payoffs or share resources. The findings are robust to prompt variations, model parameters, and resource framing. No punishment, sanctioning, or public goods game (PGG) structure is present; the focus is on one-shot dictator games and non-social maximization tasks.

- source: Lie-Panis, J., & André, J. B. (2022). Cooperation as a signal of time preferences. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper develops a formal model in which cooperation is a signal of time preferences in a repeated trust game. Individuals with lower discount rates (more future-oriented) are more likely to cooperate, and populations with lower average discount rates can sustain cooperation in a wider range of parameter settings (e.g., lower cost-benefit ratios, less reliable observation). The model provides payoff-based conditions for the evolutionary stability of cooperation and explains variability in cooperation and trust as a function of time preferences. No punishment, sanctioning, or public goods game is modeled. The main outcome is the equilibrium pay-off for different strategies and the conditions under which cooperation is stable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include punishment or a public goods game. However, it is relevant for understanding how individual and population-level time preferences can affect the stability and variability of cooperation and aggregate pay-offs in repeated trust-based interactions. For prediction tasks involving punishment or public goods games, this paper is only indirectly informative, suggesting that time preferences and the structure of observation and reputation can moderate cooperation and efficiency, but it does not address punishment or sanctions.

- source: McClung, J. S., Placì, S., Bangerter, A., Clément, F., & Bshary, R. (2017). The language of cooperation: shared intentionality drives variation in helping as a function of group membership. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: This paper introduces a joint foraging game (the 'egg hunt') in which pairs of participants can help each other at a cost to themselves, with helping not explicitly instructed. The design manipulates group membership (in-group vs out-group) and the ability to talk (talking vs no talking). Both in-group membership and the ability to talk significantly increased costly helping and overall group success (total screws collected, which determined monetary payoffs). Shared intentionality in conversation was identified as the key psychological mechanism mediating the effect of group membership on helping. Costly helping was costly to the helper but increased total group payoff. There were no punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. The game is adjacent to public goods or prisoner's dilemma games, but differs in that helping is not explicitly structured and there is no punishment or reward system.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that enabling communication (chat) and making group membership salient can increase efficiency (total group payoff) in joint action games where helping is possible but not enforced. The effect is mediated by shared intentionality in communication. While the game is not a standard public goods game and does not include punishment, the findings are relevant for predicting efficiency in games where communication and group identity are manipulated. The absence of punishment means the paper does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does show that communication alone can have a strong positive effect on efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the importance of chat/communication and group identity, but does not provide evidence about punishment, punishment cost, or related dimensions.

- source: Laird, R. A. (2018). Sequential interactions-in which one player plays first and another responds-promote cooperation in evolutionary-dynamical simulations of single-shot Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Sequential interactions in single-shot Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift games allow for the emergence of conditional strategies, which can increase the average payoff of the population compared to simultaneous play. In the spatial Prisoner's Dilemma, sequential play greatly expands the parameter range (cost-benefit ratio) over which cooperation and higher payoffs are possible. In the Snowdrift game, sequential play leads to high average payoffs across the full parameter range. The effect is due to the structure of the game and the possibility of conditional responses, not to punishment, reward, or repeated interaction.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. However, it is highly informative about the effect of sequential versus simultaneous play in two-player social dilemmas (Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift), showing that sequential play can increase efficiency by enabling conditional strategies. Since the paper does not include punishment or sanctions, it should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it may be useful for understanding the role of game structure and timing in cooperation and efficiency.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., & Nowak, M. A. (2007). Direct reciprocity on graphs. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the evolution of cooperation via direct reciprocity is affected by the structure of the population (graph degree k) and the update rule. For birth-death and pairwise comparison updating, structured populations make it harder for cooperation to be evolutionarily stable, but easier for cooperators to be advantageous. For death-birth and imitation updating, structured populations always facilitate cooperation. The critical benefit-to-cost ratio for cooperation to be favored depends on the probability of repeated interaction (w) and the number of neighbors (k). Errors in strategy execution, which increase with the number of neighbors, make cooperation harder to sustain unless the network is sparse (small k). The paper does not consider punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. However, it provides theoretical results on how network structure and repeated interaction affect the evolution of cooperation and group payoffs in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games. The absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms means it cannot inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. Its results may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency in repeated dyadic games on networks, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of imperfect information on the evolution of reciprocity (and thus on group efficiency/payoff) depends on the structure of payoffs. When payoffs are linear, imperfect information always makes reciprocity (cooperation) less likely to evolve, reducing group efficiency. However, when payoffs are nonlinear (synergetic effects), imperfect information can sometimes make reciprocity more likely to evolve, increasing group efficiency. The analysis is theoretical, based on evolutionary stability conditions for tit-for-tat (TFT) versus always-defect (ALLD) strategies in the iterated prisoner's dilemma with mistakes and imperfect information. The results do not generalize directly to public goods games or to settings with punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how information structure and payoff nonlinearity can affect the evolution of cooperation and group efficiency in repeated dyadic games. However, it does not address public goods games, punishment, or sanctions, and thus is only indirectly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The main lesson is that the effect of information and error structure on efficiency can be non-monotonic and depend on payoff nonlinearity, but this does not directly inform the effect of punishment in multi-player public goods games.

For death-birth and imitation updating, cooperation can be favored if the benefit-to-cost ratio exceeds a threshold determined by the network structure. The results are derived for both finite and infinite populations, and are validated by numerical simulations. The paper does not consider punishment, rewards, or explicit public goods games, but provides general insights into how network structure affects the efficiency and prevalence of cooperation.

- source: Hilbe, C. (2011). Local Replicator Dynamics: A Simple Link Between Deterministic and Stochastic Models of Evolutionary Game Theory. *Bulletin of Mathematical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals interact and update strategies in finite groups (local replicator dynamics), the evolutionary outcome can be captured by a replicator equation with a modified payoff matrix. This approach allows the use of deterministic replicator dynamics to predict outcomes in finite populations, including the emergence of cooperation, risk aversion, and even the dominance of strategies that are not optimal in the classical sense. The model shows that group size has a significant effect: smaller groups can lead to the spread of spiteful or dominated strategies, while larger groups approximate the classical infinite-population results. The paper does not address punishment, sanctions, or public goods games directly, and thus does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It is relevant for understanding how group size and local updating affect efficiency and the evolution of cooperation in general evolutionary games, but it does not include punishment mechanisms or analyze public goods games with or without punishment. Therefore, it should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects. However, its theoretical insights on group size and local updating may be contextually informative for understanding the broader dynamics of efficiency in group-structured games.

  findings: The paper finds that evolutionary game dynamics on regular graphs can be described by a replicator equation with a transformed payoff matrix, which incorporates local competition effects. For the Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift games, spatial structure (graph topology) can promote or inhibit cooperation depending on the update rule and game parameters, thereby affecting the equilibrium group payoff and efficiency. The framework is general and applies to any n-strategy game, but does not address punishment, reward, or explicit public goods games. The results are most relevant for understanding how population structure alters the efficiency and stability of cooperative outcomes in evolutionary games.

The absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms means the paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.

- source: Smaldino, P. E., Schank, J. C., & McElreath, R. (2013). Increased Costs of Cooperation Help Cooperators in the Long Run. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The model demonstrates that in spatially structured populations where survival requires occasional cooperation, increasing the cost of living or the cost of unreciprocated cooperation can promote the long-term survival and dominance of cooperators. This occurs because defectors, while initially advantaged, eventually die out when they cannot find enough cooperators to exploit, allowing cooperator clusters to recover and expand. However, if costs become too high, the population may collapse entirely. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and the main outcome is the frequency of cooperators, not explicit group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings are relevant to understanding how environmental adversity and the cost structure of cooperation can affect the prevalence of cooperation in spatially structured populations, but it does not provide direct evidence about efficiency outcomes or the impact of punishment. Therefore, it should not be used as primary evidence for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions involving punishment.

  findings: The paper finds that environmental harshness is a key moderator of cooperative behavior in coordination games like the Stag Hunt. In both models and human experiments, individuals are more likely to choose cooperative, high-risk strategies when the environment is harsh and survival is threatened. Communication between agents (or players) allows for better coordination and increases the viability of cooperation. The study does not include punishment, reward, or sanction mechanisms, and does not use a public goods game structure, but rather a coordination game with binary choices and pairwise matching. The main outcomes are behavioral (strategy choice and switching), with survival probability and energy reserves as related but not directly group efficiency outcomes.

- source: Wakano, J. Y. (2007). Evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods games with common resource dynamics. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in spatially structured, anonymous, and obligate public goods games with resource regeneration and diffusion, cooperative strategies (farmers) can outcompete exploiters under certain parameter regimes, even when cooperation is costly (strong altruism). The mechanism is that cooperators can increase their population density and locally exhaust the benefits of cooperation, preventing exploiters from free-riding. However, the evolution of cooperation does not always improve the environmental resource level; sometimes, higher population density of cooperators leads to a degraded environment. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and thus does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant as an adjacent theoretical model showing that spatial structure and resource dynamics can enable cooperation without punishment, but it does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency in the sense required for the prediction task. Its findings may be useful for understanding alternative mechanisms for cooperation, but it should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., & Nowak, M. A. (2006). Evolutionary games on cycles. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary games on cycles, the update rule strongly affects whether cooperation can evolve. For the Prisoner's Dilemma, birth-death updating never favors cooperation, but death-birth and imitation updating can favor cooperation if the benefit-to-cost ratio exceeds a threshold (b/c > 2 for death-birth, b/c > 4 for imitation, for large N). In coordination games, death-birth and imitation updating can favor Pareto efficient outcomes over risk-dominant ones, unlike birth-death updating. The results are analytic and apply to two-strategy games on cycles, not to public goods games or punishment. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes as in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding how spatial structure and update rules affect the evolution of cooperation in two-strategy games (such as the Prisoner's Dilemma), and discusses payoff-based concepts like risk dominance and Pareto efficiency in coordination games. However, it does not analyze punishment, sanctions, or public goods games, nor does it report group efficiency or welfare outcomes. Its findings may be indirectly informative for understanding how local interactions and update mechanisms can support cooperation, but it does not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects in public goods games.

- source: Capraro, V., & Cococcioni, G. (2015). Social setting, intuition and experience in laboratory experiments interact to shape cooperative decision-making. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that, among Indian participants (a population presumed to have internalized non-cooperative strategies), promoting intuition (via time pressure) versus deliberation (via time delay) does not affect cooperation rates among inexperienced subjects. However, experienced subjects (those with prior exposure to economic games) are more cooperative than inexperienced ones, but only under time pressure. The main dependent variable is the percentage of endowment transferred in a continuous-strategy prisoner's dilemma. No punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms are present, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it studies a two-person continuous-strategy prisoner's dilemma without punishment or reward options. The findings are only indirectly relevant: they suggest that cooperation rates (and thus, by implication, group payoffs) are low in non-cooperative settings without institutional support or experience, and that experience with economic games can increase cooperation under intuitive conditions. However, since no punishment mechanism is present, and no efficiency or group payoff is reported, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games. It may be useful for contextualizing baseline cooperation levels in non-cooperative settings.

The studies use non-strategic, anonymous allocation tasks (not public goods or social dilemma games) and do not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are directly measured or reported. The findings suggest that cognitive reflection is associated with a specific pattern of social preferences, but do not inform about the effects of punishment or efficiency in public goods contexts.

  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment

  findings: The study finds that in one-shot, anonymous ultimatum games, proposer offers vary significantly across villages, but responder behavior is highly uniform: almost all offers are accepted, and responders' self-reported minimum acceptable offers are very low. There is no evidence for cultural conformity or coordinated expectations at the ethno-linguistic group level. The findings suggest that local environmental factors, rather than group-level fairness norms, may drive variation in bargaining behavior. The study does not include punishment or analyze efficiency, welfare, or group payoff.

- source: Powers, S. T., Ekárt, A., & Lewis, P. R. (2018). Modelling enduring institutions: The complementarity of evolutionary and agent-based approaches. *Cognitive Systems Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that participants' choices in one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma games are influenced by information about the other player's action and by individual personality traits. The disjunction effect (greater cooperation under uncertainty) is present but weaker than in previous studies and is not explained by a general bias to cooperate. Higher potential payoffs (high variability matrices) reduce the disjunction effect, suggesting more optimal responding when incentives are larger. Reward Responsiveness predicts more optimal (defecting) choices when the optimal strategy is to defect and the other player's action is known. Agreeableness predicts more cooperative choices when the optimal strategy is to cooperate. No evidence is found for a general bias toward cooperation, and the results are similar whether the task is framed as a social dilemma or a neutral card game. The study does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not include punishment or sanctions, nor does it analyze efficiency, group payoff, or related payoff-based outcomes. Its findings on the influence of payoff variability and personality on cooperation/defection may be contextually informative for understanding individual decision-making in social dilemmas, but it does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects or efficiency.

- source: Du, J., Thomas, M. G., Bårdsen, B. J., Mace, R., & Næss, M. W. (2019). Comparison of social complexity in two independent pastoralist societies. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both kinship and herding group membership predict gift-giving among pastoralists, with some cross-cultural differences: Saami herders in Norway emphasize reciprocity and group membership, while Tibetan herders in China emphasize kinship and giving to poorer herders. Gifts are more likely to be given within herding groups and to close kin, but the relative importance of these factors varies by site. There is no evidence of punishment, sanctions, or explicit efficiency/payoff outcomes. The association between gifts received and herd size (wealth) is negative in Tibetan sites (gifts go to poorer herders) and absent in Norwegian sites.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report group efficiency or payoff outcomes. The design is adjacent to PGGs in that it involves allocation of resources in a social context, but the absence of punishment and efficiency outcomes means it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task. It may provide some contextual insight into how cooperation is structured in real-world groups, but not in a way that informs the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Tan, S. L. (2019). Proximity inheritance explains the evolution of cooperation under natural selection and mutation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that proximity inheritance in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma model leads to the emergence and maintenance of high levels of cooperation, even in the presence of mutation. Cooperation is favored when offspring are placed within the neighborhood of their parents, and the effect is robust to variations in temptation of defection, selection intensity, and mutation rate. The model demonstrates that dynamic clustering of cooperators can arise, and that cooperation collapses if proximity inheritance is removed (i.e., if offspring are placed randomly or at the site of the dead individual). However, the paper does not analyze group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, and does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does not include punishment or efficiency outcomes, and the game structure is a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma rather than a public goods game. The findings may be contextually informative for understanding how spatial structure and inheritance of proximity can promote cooperation, but they do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

  findings: The paper finds that allowing players to opt out of the game (become loners) relaxes the social dilemma and can promote the abundance of cooperation, especially as the number of loner strategies increases. The effect of optionality and spatial structure (cycles vs. complete graphs) is analyzed, with the main result being that more loner strategies (or higher mutational bias toward loners) make cooperation more likely to be more abundant than defection. There is no evidence of synergy between optionality and spatial structure. The main dependent variable is the abundance (frequency) of each strategy, not group payoff or efficiency.

It should not be used as direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: FRANK, R. H., GILOVICH, T., & REGAN, D. T. (1993). THE EVOLUTION OF ONE-SHOT COOPERATION - AN EXPERIMENT. *Ethology and Sociobiology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that, after 30 minutes of social interaction, subjects can predict with significant accuracy whether their partners will cooperate or defect in a one-shot prisoner's dilemma. The observed cooperation rate is 73.7%, and the prediction accuracy is 76.3%, both substantially above chance. The study provides evidence that cooperative dispositions can be discerned and that this may support the evolution of cooperation in one-shot settings. However, the paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report on efficiency, group payoff, or related payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. It does not manipulate or include punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its findings are only weakly relevant to the prediction task, as they pertain to cooperation in one-shot prisoner's dilemmas without sanctions. The only indirect relevance is the demonstration that high cooperation can occur in the absence of punishment, especially when social cues are available, but this does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Molho, C., Soraperra, I., Schulz, J. F., & Shalvi, S. (2025). Guilt drives prosociality across 20 countries. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Across 20 countries, participants were more likely to make prosocial choices in dictator games when they had full information about the consequences of their actions (guilt condition) compared to when they could remain ignorant. Making choices observable to others (shame condition) had negligible or inconsistent effects on prosociality. Individual-level guilt-proneness predicted greater responsiveness to information, but country-level differences were not robust moderators. The study does not include punishment, sanctions, or public goods game structures, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment, sanctions, or group interaction, and does not report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes. Its findings on the effect of information and observability on individual prosocial choice in dictator games may be contextually informative for understanding mechanisms of prosociality, but are not directly transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency in PGG-like settings.

The findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (choices, rejections) and do not address efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. - source: Pan, X. S., & Houser, D. (2013). Cooperation during cultural group formation promotes trust towards members of out-groups. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that the degree of cooperation required during group formation (cooperative vs. independent production tasks) causally affects trust and reciprocity in subsequent trust games. Specifically, participants from cooperative production groups (CP) trust and reciprocate with out-group members at similar rates as with in-group members, showing little parochial altruism. In contrast, participants from independent production groups (IP) display significant in-group favouritism and out-group discrimination. These effects persist even after negative experiences (betrayal) in the trust game. The results are explained by differences in beliefs about out-group trustworthiness, shaped by the group formation process. No efficiency, payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/payoff outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, suggesting that the cooperative context of group formation can influence trust and reciprocity, but not in a payoff or efficiency framework. It should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Fletcher, J. A., & Zwick, M. (2004). Strong altruism can evolve in randomly formed groups. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper demonstrates that, contrary to previous consensus, strong altruism can evolve in randomly formed groups if groups persist for multiple generations. This is due to increased positive assortment (measured by the regression coefficient between actors and recipients) that arises after one or more generations within groups, even without kin selection, homogeneous groups, or population growth. The models show that smaller group size, higher benefit-to-cost ratio, and intermediate numbers of generations within groups favor the evolution of altruism. Stochasticity in group formation and population regulation can further affect the likelihood of altruism evolving. However, the models do not include punishment, reward, or explicit payoff/efficiency outcomes, and thus do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Its relevance is limited to providing theoretical context about the evolution of cooperation/altruism in group-structured populations, especially regarding the effects of group persistence, assortment, and stochasticity. It does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled efficiency.

- source: Smith, K. M. M., Mabulla, I. A. A., & Apicella, C. L. L. (2022). Hadza hunter-gatherers with greater exposure to other cultures share more with generous campmates. *Biology Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A

  findings: The study finds that Hadza participants are more likely to share with campmates they rank higher on generosity and, to a lesser extent, foraging ability. Importantly, the preference for generosity is significantly stronger among Hadza with greater exposure to other cultures. The effect of exposure is specific to generosity and does not extend to foraging ability. The results are interpreted as evidence for the flexibility of partner choice psychology, with socioecological context (exposure to other cultures) shaping the traits valued in social partners. The study does not involve punishment, sanctions, or analyze efficiency or group payoff.
