Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects on efficiency in PGG-like settings.

- source: Garfield, Z. H., Ringen, E. J., Buckner, W., Medupe, D., Wrangham, R. W., & Glowacki, L. (2023). Norm violations and punishments across human societies. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=weak | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that evidence for punishment of norm violations is common across societies, with physical, material, and execution punishments each present in about a third of societies, and reputational punishments somewhat less common. The presence of reputational punishment is associated with egalitarianism and the absence of food storage, while material punishment is associated with food storage and animal husbandry. Execution punishment is moderately associated with social stratification. There is little evidence for strong phylogenetic (cultural history) effects, suggesting that punishment systems adapt quickly to local socioecology. The study does not analyze or report any efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, nor does it use experimental economic games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It is relevant for understanding the cross-cultural diversity of punishment systems and their socioecological correlates, but does not report on payoff-based outcomes or experimental manipulations of punishment. Its findings may be contextually informative for understanding the types of punishment that might arise in different socioecological settings, but it does not inform the quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in controlled game environments.

- source: Powell, K. L., Roberts, G., & Nettle, D. (2012). Eye Images Increase Charitable Donations: Evidence From an Opportunistic Field Experiment in a Supermarket. *Ethology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=weak | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: Displaying subtle eye images on charity collection buckets in a supermarket increased the amount donated per thousand customers by 48% compared to control images. The effect was stronger (59% increase) when the supermarket was quiet and weaker (28% increase) when it was busy. The setting lacked any injunctive or descriptive norm to donate, and non-donation was the norm. The authors interpret the results as supporting the idea that cues of observation (eye images) can increase prosocial behavior even in the absence of norm enforcement or punishment. The study does not involve a public goods game, group payoff, or efficiency outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The experimental context is not a PGG or a close variant, and there is no manipulation or measurement of punishment, sanctions, or efficiency. The outcome is individual charitable giving in response to subtle cues of observation, not group-level payoff or efficiency. The findings may be weakly relevant to the broader literature on cues of observation and prosociality, but do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects in PGG-like games.

- source: Buckholtz, J. W., Martin, J. W., Treadway, M. T., Jan, K., Zald, D. H., Jones, O., & Marois, R. (2015). From Blame to Punishment: Disrupting Prefrontal Cortex Activity Reveals Norm Enforcement Mechanisms. *Neuron*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that the dorsolateral prefrontal cortex (DLPFC) plays a selective, causal role in punishment decision-making for norm violations. Disrupting DLPFC function via rTMS reduces the amount of punishment assigned to culpable agents, especially for low- to moderate-harm crimes, but does not affect blameworthiness ratings. fMRI results show that DLPFC activity is higher during punishment decisions than during blameworthiness judgments. The study supports an integration-and-selection model in which DLPFC integrates information about harm and culpability to determine punishment. However, the study does not use a public goods game or any economic game with payoffs, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not use such games or report any payoff-based outcomes. Its findings are relevant for understanding the cognitive and neural mechanisms of punishment assignment in norm enforcement, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency or group payoff in economic games. It may be useful for mechanistic or theoretical context about punishment, but not for the downstream efficiency prediction task.

- source: Gächter, S., Molleman, L., & Nosenzo, D. (2025). Why people follow rules. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that 55-70% of participants follow an arbitrary costly rule even when acting anonymously and alone, and when rule-breaking has no consequences for others. Social expectations and intrinsic respect for rules explain most rule-following. Adding externalities (harm to a charity) or punishment (with varying probabilities) increases rule-following rates, but a substantial baseline of rule-following exists without these. Observing peer rule-breaking reduces rule-following, but even with all peers breaking the rule, a significant fraction still comply. The main outcome is behavioral (rule-following), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar group cooperation settings. While it provides evidence that punishment increases rule-following in a single-player, non-strategic, asocial rule-following task, the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, and the context is not a PGG or its variant. The findings may be relevant for understanding the motivational basis of compliance and the effect of sanctions on rule-following, but cannot be directly transferred to efficiency predictions in PGG-like environments.

- source: Bateson, P. (2014). Evolution, epigenetics and cooperation. *Journal of Biosciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper reviews and synthesizes evolutionary theories of cooperation, emphasizing the integration of epigenetics and development into these frameworks. It discusses kin selection, mutualism (including reciprocal altruism and punishment as a stabilizer), and group selection, arguing for a pluralistic approach. Punishment is mentioned as a mechanism that can stabilize cooperation, but the discussion is conceptual and not tied to experimental or payoff-based outcomes. No public goods game or laboratory game paradigms are analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative findings relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its discussion of punishment is theoretical and evolutionary, not experimental or game-theoretic. It may provide conceptual background on why punishment can stabilize cooperation, but it does not inform parameter-level predictions or the impact of design dimensions on efficiency.

- source: Bar-On, K. K., & Lamm, E. (2023). The interplay of social identity and norm psychology in the evolution of human groups. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper proposes that norm psychology (the internalization of social norms) and social identity theory (the internalization of group identity) are deeply intertwined processes that together shape human norm-following behaviour. It suggests that norm adherence is flexible and context-dependent, influenced by both personal commitment to norms and the desire to maintain social identity. Punishment of norm violators is discussed as a social process (e.g., gossip, humour) that reinforces group identity and norm compliance. However, the paper does not address efficiency, payoff, or public goods game outcomes, nor does it provide empirical evidence on the effects of punishment or sanctions in economic games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not discuss game design dimensions, control or treatment efficiency, or economic outcomes. Its relevance is limited to high-level theoretical framing about the psychological and social mechanisms underlying norm enforcement and group behaviour. It may inform background understanding of why punishment and norm enforcement are important in human groups, but it does not provide actionable evidence for the downstream prediction task.
